Preface & Introduction

A Comprehensive 12-Week Guide for Graduate Students

Covering: Java · Spring Boot · React · PostgreSQL · Docker · AWS · System Design

This book teaches full stack Java development through the progressive construction of FinTrack — a complete, production-grade banking application. Every concept is introduced with real code, every chapter adds a working layer to the application, and every section connects directly to how professional engineering teams operate.

Preface

This is not a reference manual. It is a guided construction project.

Over twelve chapters, you will build FinTrack — a banking application with real customer accounts, real transactions, real security, and real deployment to the cloud. Each chapter introduces the tools and concepts you need for that week, then applies them immediately to the application. By the end, you will have built something you can point to, demonstrate, and be genuinely proud of.

Who This Book Is For

This book is written for graduate students who are comfortable with basic programming concepts — variables, loops, functions — but have not yet built a complete, multi-layer web application. If you have taken an introductory programming course and want to know what real professional software development looks like, this book answers that question thoroughly.

What You Will Build

FinTrack Banking Application:

  • Customer registration and JWT-based authentication
  • Checking and savings accounts with full OOP inheritance hierarchy
  • Deposits, withdrawals, and inter-account transfers with full ACID database transactions
  • Transaction history with date-range filtering and pagination
  • Spring Boot REST API with input validation and centralized error handling
  • React frontend with responsive design and real-time validation
  • Spring Data JPA persistence with PostgreSQL
  • Spring Security with BCrypt password hashing and JWT tokens
  • Docker containerization and Docker Compose local orchestration
  • GitHub Actions CI/CD pipeline with automated testing
  • AWS deployment (ECS, RDS, CloudFront)

How to Use This Book

Read each chapter in order — the application grows progressively and each chapter builds on the last. Every code example is complete and runnable unless noted. Do not skip the “Try This” exercises — understanding solidifies through practice, not reading.

Prerequisites to install before Chapter 1: - JDK 21 (Eclipse Temurin or Oracle) - IntelliJ IDEA Community Edition - Git - Node.js 20 LTS - Docker Desktop - PostgreSQL 16

Chapter 1Foundations

Starting From Zero — Everything You Need to Know Before Writing Your First Line of Code

Chapter Objectives

By the end of this chapter you will be able to:

✓ Explain what a computer is and describe the role of RAM, storage, and CPU in plain English

✓ Describe the history of computing from the 1940s to the 2020s and explain why each decade mattered

✓ Explain what a programming language is and why Java was invented

✓ Navigate your computer using only the terminal — no file explorer

✓ Explain how a web request travels from a browser to a server and back

✓ Write, compile, and run your first Java program

✓ Declare variables, write if/else decisions, and use for loops

✓ Set up your full development environment: JDK 21, IntelliJ IDEA, and Git

✓ Write user stories for FinTrack using the professional format

A Letter to You Before We Begin

You picked up this book. That already makes you different from most people.

Maybe you have no idea what programming is. Maybe you have tried to learn before and felt lost. Maybe someone told you it is too hard, or that you are not a “technical person.” Maybe you are nervous that everyone else in the room seems to know things you do not.

None of that matters.

What matters is that you are here now, and you are willing to start. That is the only thing that has ever separated people who become developers from people who do not — not intelligence, not talent, not a special kind of brain. Just the willingness to start, and the patience to keep going when it gets confusing.

This chapter is written for someone who has never written a single line of code. We will start at the very beginning — what a computer actually is, what software actually does, what programming actually means — before we write anything at all. Taking this time at the start will save you from confusion later, when every other beginner is asking “but why does this work?” while you already know the answer.

By the end of this chapter, you will have your tools set up, you will understand how professional teams build software, and you will have written your first Java program. It will not be much — but it will be yours, and you will understand every word of it.

Let us begin.

1.0 Before We Start — The Conversation Every Student Deserves to Hear

Most programming courses begin with code. This one begins with something more important: the reason.

If you know why you are learning something, you learn it differently. You stop memorising and start understanding. You stop following instructions and start building intuition. So before we write a single line of code, let us answer the questions that most books assume you will never ask.

Why do programming languages exist? Why did humans create them? Why does any of this matter right now, in a world where artificial intelligence can write code, where cloud computers process billions of operations per second, and where the technology landscape changes faster than any textbook can keep up?

And most importantly — how does learning Java, Spring Boot, React, and the rest of the FinTrack technology stack prepare you for a world that looks dramatically different every three years?

These are not soft questions. They are the foundation of everything. Read this section carefully, even if you are tempted to skip to the technical content. The students who understand this section learn everything else twice as fast.

1.1 The Story of Computing — How We Got Here

To understand where you are going, you need to understand how we got here. Not a textbook timeline, but the real story — why each step happened, what problem it solved, and how it led directly to the code you will write in this book.

The Problem: Humans Are Slow, Machines Are Dumb

Here is the fundamental tension that drives the entire history of computing:

Humans are brilliant. We think in concepts, make judgements, understand context, and solve problems creatively. But we are also slow. Terribly slow. A human can add two numbers in about a second. A modern CPU does it four billion times per second.

Machines are fast. Astonishingly fast. But they are also, on their own, completely dumb. A machine has no judgement, no creativity, no understanding of context. It does only exactly what it is told, in exactly the order it is told, without question.

The entire history of computing is the story of humans trying to bridge this gap — to harness machine speed while providing human intelligence. Every programming language, every framework, every tool you will learn in this book is a solution to one version of that same problem.

The 1940s — The First Computers: Teaching Machines With Switches

The first general-purpose computers were built in the 1940s. ENIAC, completed in 1945 at the University of Pennsylvania, weighed 30 tonnes, occupied 1,800 square feet of floor space, and consumed 150 kilowatts of electricity — enough to power a small neighbourhood.

Programming ENIAC did not involve writing code. It involved physically reconfiguring the machine. Operators connected cables, flipped switches, and set dials to tell the machine what to do. Reprogramming it for a new task took days.

There was no concept of a “program” that you stored. There was no concept of “software” at all. The machine and its instructions were inseparable.

This was computing at its rawest. The humans who operated these machines were not programmers — they were engineers working directly with hardware. The machine spoke only one language: electricity. On and off. One and zero. Binary.

The 1950s — Assembly Language: The First Translation Layer

Scientists quickly realised that reconfiguring machines physically was impossibly slow. They needed a way to give instructions symbolically — in text — that the machine could then translate into its native binary.

The first solution was assembly language. Instead of flipping switches, you could write:

MOV AX, 5     ; put the number 5 into register AX
ADD AX, 3     ; add 3 to it

A program called an assembler translated these human-readable instructions into binary that the machine could execute. This was a revolutionary idea: for the first time, there was a layer of abstraction between the human and the machine.

But assembly language was still painfully close to the machine. Every instruction did only one tiny thing. Writing even simple programs required hundreds of lines. And code written for one type of machine would not work on another — every computer had its own assembly dialect.

The problem was getting clearer: humans needed to think in concepts. Machines executed primitive operations. The gap between the two was too wide.

The 1950s-60s — High-Level Languages: Speaking in Concepts

The breakthrough came when scientists asked: what if we could write code that looked like human language or mathematics, and the computer handled the translation automatically?

FORTRAN (1957) was the first widely used high-level language, designed for scientific calculations. A line like Y = X * 2 + 3 was now valid code. The compiler translated it into dozens of assembly instructions — but the programmer never saw them.

COBOL (1959) was designed for business applications — banking, payroll, inventory. Its syntax was deliberately wordy and English-like:

MOVE 500.00 TO ACCOUNT-BALANCE.
ADD DEPOSIT-AMOUNT TO ACCOUNT-BALANCE.

This was the moment programming became accessible to people who were not electrical engineers. Business logic — the rules of how a bank works, how payroll is calculated, how inventory is tracked — could now be expressed in something approaching human language.

COBOL is still running today. Billions of dollars in banking transactions process through COBOL systems every day. When you use an ATM, there is a reasonable chance that somewhere in the chain, a 50-year-old COBOL program is involved.

The 1970s-80s — Personal Computers: Computing for Everyone

For the first three decades of computing, computers existed only in large institutions — universities, governments, corporations. They filled rooms, cost millions of dollars, and required specialist operators.

The transistor changed everything. As transistors shrank from the size of a finger to microscopic, it became possible to put entire CPUs on a single chip. The microprocessor was born, and with it, the personal computer.

The Apple II (1977) and the IBM PC (1981) brought computers into homes and small businesses for the first time. These machines were primitive by today’s standards — the original IBM PC had 16 kilobytes of RAM (modern iPhones have 6 gigabytes, nearly 400,000 times more) — but they were the first machines that ordinary people could own and program.

This decade also saw the rise of the C programming language (1972). C was a masterpiece of design — low enough to control hardware directly, high enough to write readable, maintainable code. It gave programmers a universal language that could run on almost any machine. Java, which you will learn in this book, was directly inspired by C’s syntax. When you write if (balance > 0) { in Java, you are writing code whose ancestry traces directly to Dennis Ritchie writing C in a Bell Labs office in New Jersey in the early 1970s.

The 1990s — The Internet Changes Everything

In 1991, a British scientist named Tim Berners-Lee released the World Wide Web — a system for sharing documents over the internet using hyperlinks. He intended it for scientists to share research papers. Within five years, it had transformed into the foundation of modern commerce, communication, and culture.

This decade matters enormously for you because it established the client-server model you will implement in FinTrack:

  • A client (browser) requests information
  • A server sends it back
  • The communication protocol is HTTP
  • The content format is HTML

Everything you build in this book — the React frontend, the Spring Boot backend, the REST API — is built on top of the architectural decisions made by engineers in the 1990s who were figuring out how the web should work.

Java was born in 1995, designed by James Gosling at Sun Microsystems with a revolutionary goal: write once, run anywhere. A Java program compiled on a Windows machine could run on a Unix server without modification. For enterprises deploying software across heterogeneous networks, this was transformative. Java became the language of choice for large-scale business applications, and it has never stopped being so.

By 1999, Amazon was selling books online. Google had just launched its search engine. The dot-com boom was changing the world at a speed that felt impossible. Every company suddenly needed software developers.

The 2000s — Mobile, Cloud, and the Scale Problem

The 2000s brought two developments that changed computing permanently.

The smartphone. The iPhone launched in 2007, followed by the first Android phone in 2008. Within five years, a majority of humans on earth had a supercomputer in their pocket, connected to the internet 24 hours a day. This created a new category of software — mobile applications — and a new requirement: software had to work perfectly at all times, from anywhere, for anyone.

Cloud computing. Amazon launched AWS (Amazon Web Services) in 2006. The idea was conceptually simple but practically revolutionary: instead of every company buying and maintaining their own servers, they could rent computing power from Amazon by the hour. Need more capacity? Click a button. Need less? Click another button.

Cloud computing did not just change how software was deployed — it changed what software was possible. A startup with ten employees could now run on the same infrastructure as a Fortune 500 company. The barriers to building world-class software collapsed.

This is why Chapter 11 of this book covers AWS. Cloud deployment is not optional knowledge for a modern developer — it is as fundamental as knowing how to write a function.

The 2010s — Big Data, Machine Learning, and Microservices

The 2010s saw the internet reach a scale that nobody had imagined possible. Facebook had a billion users. Google processed 3.5 billion searches per day. WhatsApp was handling 50 billion messages daily. The sheer volume of data these systems generated created an entirely new field: big data.

Processing billions of records required new tools and architectures. Applications that had been built as single large programs (monoliths) were broken into dozens of small, independent services (microservices), each doing one thing well, each deployable independently. Docker, which you will learn in Chapter 11, was created in 2013 specifically to solve the problem of running microservices consistently across environments.

Meanwhile, machine learning — a branch of artificial intelligence that had existed in theory for decades — suddenly became practical. The combination of large datasets, cheap cloud computing, and better algorithms created systems that could recognise faces in photos, translate languages in real time, and recommend what to watch on Netflix. These systems were not programmed with explicit rules. They learned patterns from data.

The world began to feel different. Software was no longer just a tool that humans used — it was beginning to anticipate what humans wanted before they asked.

The 2020s — The Decade You Are Entering

You are learning to programme in the most extraordinary moment in the history of computing. Here is what defines this decade:

Artificial Intelligence has become mainstream. Systems like ChatGPT, GitHub Copilot, and Google Gemini can write code, summarise documents, generate images, and hold conversations that feel startlingly human. AI is no longer a research curiosity — it is a tool that millions of people use every day, including millions of developers.

Cloud is the default. No modern company builds and maintains its own servers. Cloud is where software lives. AWS, Microsoft Azure, and Google Cloud Platform are as fundamental to the industry as electricity is to a factory.

Data centres are the new infrastructure. The world’s data centres consume roughly 200 terawatt-hours of electricity per year — about 1% of global electricity consumption, and growing. Every search, every video stream, every AI query runs through these facilities. Understanding distributed systems is now a core professional skill.

Quantum computing is approaching. Traditional computers process information as bits — 0 or 1. Quantum computers use qubits, which can be 0, 1, or both simultaneously. This allows them to solve certain classes of problems — drug discovery, optimisation, cryptography — exponentially faster than classical computers. Quantum computers are not yet commercially practical for most tasks, but IBM, Google, and Microsoft are advancing rapidly. The developers who will build quantum applications are being trained today in classical computing — exactly what you are doing.

The skills that matter have never mattered more. In a world where AI can generate basic code, the value of a developer has shifted. What matters is not the ability to type syntax — it is the ability to design systems, understand requirements, evaluate trade-offs, and make decisions that machines cannot. The senior developer has never been more valuable. And you cannot become a senior developer without first becoming a junior developer — which is what this book is for.

1.2 Why Programming Languages Exist — The Real Answer

Now that you understand where computers came from, you can understand why programming languages were invented.

A CPU understands one thing: binary instructions. Move this value. Add these two numbers. Jump to this memory address. These instructions are specific to each CPU architecture — Intel processors use different binary instructions than ARM processors.

A programming language is a contract between human thought and machine execution. It lets you express what you want to happen in terms that humans can read, reason about, and maintain — and then have that expression automatically translated into the machine instructions needed to make it actually happen.

Here is the chain of translation for the Java code you will write:

You write:
  if (balance >= amount) {
      balance = balance - amount;
  }

The Java Compiler (javac) translates this into bytecode:
  [binary instructions for the JVM]

The JVM translates bytecode into:
  [binary instructions for your specific CPU]

Your CPU executes:
  [millions of transistors switching on and off]

Each layer of translation is a layer of abstraction — a way of thinking about a problem at a higher level without needing to worry about the lower level. This is the central idea of all of computer science, and it is why you can write:

account.deposit(new BigDecimal("500.00"));

…and not think for a single moment about transistors, electricity, or binary numbers.

Why So Many Languages?

There are hundreds of programming languages. Why?

Because different problems need different tools. C is used where you need direct control of hardware — operating systems, embedded systems, game engines. Python is used in data science and machine learning because it is concise and has excellent libraries for mathematics. JavaScript is used in web browsers because it is the one language that all browsers understand natively. SQL is used to query databases because it expresses data retrieval in a way that maps naturally to how relational databases work.

Java occupies a particular niche: large-scale, enterprise-grade applications that need to be robust, maintainable, and deployable anywhere. Banking systems. Insurance platforms. Government services. E-commerce backends. Healthcare records. These systems need to run for years without failure, be maintained by teams of dozens of developers, and handle millions of transactions reliably. Java was designed for exactly this.

When you learn Java, you are not just learning a syntax. You are learning to think in a way that applies to every serious programming language. The concepts of variables, conditionals, loops, functions, objects, inheritance, concurrency — these exist in Java, Python, C#, Go, Rust, and every other serious language. The syntax differs; the thinking is the same.

1.3 The Web’s Architecture — What You Are Actually Building

How a Web Application Works — The Complete Picture

Every modern web application follows this architecture. By Chapter 11, you will have built every layer shown here.

┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│                         USER'S DEVICE                               │
│                                                                     │
│   ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐     │
│   │  Browser — runs your React application                   │     │
│   │                                                          │     │
│   │  AccountDashboard.jsx    TransactionList.jsx             │     │
│   │  LoginPage.jsx           TransferForm.jsx                │     │
│   └──────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────────┘     │
└──────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────┘
                       │  HTTPS Request
                       │  GET /api/accounts/1
                       │  Authorization: Bearer eyJhbGci...
                       ▼
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│                     CLOUD INFRASTRUCTURE (AWS)                      │
│                                                                     │
│   ┌──────────────┐                                                  │
│   │  CloudFront  │  — Content Delivery Network                      │
│   │  CDN         │  — Serves React files from nearest location      │
│   └──────┬───────┘                                                  │
│          │                                                          │
│   ┌──────▼───────┐                                                  │
│   │ Load Balancer│  — Distributes requests across servers           │
│   │     ALB      │  — Health checks remove failed servers           │
│   └──┬───────┬───┘                                                  │
│      │       │                                                      │
│  ┌───▼──┐ ┌──▼───┐                                                 │
│  │ App  │ │ App  │  — Spring Boot Java backend                      │
│  │  1   │ │  2   │  — Multiple copies for reliability               │
│  └───┬──┘ └──┬───┘                                                 │
│      └───┬───┘                                                      │
│          │  SQL Query                                               │
│   ┌──────▼───────┐         ┌──────────────┐                        │
│   │  PostgreSQL  │         │    Redis     │                        │
│   │     RDS      │         │    Cache     │                        │
│   │  (Primary)   │         │  (Sessions)  │                        │
│   └──────┬───────┘         └──────────────┘                        │
│          │                                                          │
│   ┌──────▼───────┐                                                  │
│   │  PostgreSQL  │  — Automatic failover                            │
│   │    (Read     │  — Read-heavy queries go here                   │
│   │   Replica)   │                                                  │
│   └──────────────┘                                                  │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
                       │  JSON Response
                       │  {"id":1,"balance":"2500.00",...}
                       │  HTTP 200 OK
                       ▼
                  React updates the
                  UI automatically

Each layer has one job:

LayerTechnologyResponsibility
FrontendReactWhat the user sees and does
CDNCloudFrontFast file delivery worldwide
Load BalancerALBDistribute traffic, absorb failures
BackendSpring BootBusiness logic and security rules
CacheRedisFast access to frequently-read data
DatabasePostgreSQLPermanent, reliable data storage

You will build each of these layers over the next 12 chapters.

When most people hear “web application,” they picture a website. But a modern web application is far more sophisticated than a website. Understanding the architecture tells you why each technology in this book exists.

The Three Layers of a Modern Web Application

Every modern web application has three layers, each with a distinct responsibility:

┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│                  FRONTEND (Client)                   │
│   React  ·  HTML  ·  CSS  ·  JavaScript             │
│   Runs in the USER'S browser, on their device        │
│   Responsible for: what the user sees and does       │
└────────────────────┬────────────────────────────────┘
                     │  HTTP requests (JSON data)
                     │
┌────────────────────▼────────────────────────────────┐
│                  BACKEND (Server)                    │
│   Spring Boot  ·  Java  ·  REST API                  │
│   Runs on a SERVER in a data centre or cloud         │
│   Responsible for: business logic, security, rules   │
└────────────────────┬────────────────────────────────┘
                     │  SQL queries
                     │
┌────────────────────▼────────────────────────────────┐
│                  DATABASE                            │
│   PostgreSQL                                         │
│   Runs on a dedicated server, often managed          │
│   Responsible for: permanent, reliable data storage  │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

This three-layer architecture was not arbitrary. It evolved over decades as engineers discovered what worked:

Why separate the frontend? Because the user interface needs to be fast and responsive. If every button click required a round trip to a server in another country, the application would feel slow and broken. The frontend handles presentation locally, only contacting the server when it needs data.

Why separate the backend? Because business rules — who can see what, what constitutes a valid transaction, how fees are calculated — need to be enforced in one central place. If those rules lived in the frontend (running on the user’s device), anyone could modify them. The backend is the trusted authority.

Why separate the database? Because data outlives code. The application code will be rewritten, upgraded, or replaced many times. The data — every transaction, every customer record, every account history — must survive all of those changes, intact and correct, sometimes for decades.

This is the architecture you will build in FinTrack, and it is the architecture of essentially every serious web application in the world: banking platforms, e-commerce sites, healthcare systems, social networks, government services. Once you understand this architecture, you can work in any company, on any system.

The Modern Cloud Architecture

In production, this three-layer architecture runs on cloud infrastructure:

Internet Users
      │
      ▼
CloudFront CDN          (serves the React frontend files from
      │                  locations near each user — fast)
      │
      ▼
Load Balancer           (distributes traffic across
      │                  multiple backend servers)
      │
   ┌──┴───────────┐
   ▼              ▼
Backend 1      Backend 2    ← Multiple copies of Spring Boot
   │              │           running simultaneously for
   └──────┬───────┘           reliability and scale
          │
          ▼
     RDS PostgreSQL          (managed database with automatic
          │                   backups and failover)
          │
          ▼
   ElastiCache Redis         (cache for frequently-accessed data)

This is not complexity for its own sake. Every component solves a specific problem: - The CDN ensures files load quickly regardless of where the user is in the world - The load balancer ensures that if one server crashes, traffic goes to the others - Multiple backend instances ensure that if ten thousand people log in simultaneously, the system handles it - The managed database ensures that even if the entire server room loses power, no financial data is lost

You will build and deploy this in Chapter 11. The concepts will feel abstract until then — but when you see your own application running on AWS, accessible from any browser in the world, it will all click.

1.4 The World You Are Entering — A Quick Overview

You are learning to programme at an extraordinary moment. Artificial intelligence writes code. Cloud computers process billions of transactions per second. Quantum computers are approaching commercial viability.

This matters for you — and Chapter 12 addresses it fully, once you have the context to appreciate it. After twelve weeks of building a real banking application, the landscape of AI, cloud, and quantum computing will mean something concrete rather than abstract. You will understand exactly how your skills fit into it.

For now, here is the short version:

AI makes skilled developers more productive — not redundant. A developer who understands fundamentals deeply uses AI to accelerate their work. A developer who does not understand fundamentals cannot evaluate whether AI’s output is correct, secure, or appropriate.

Cloud is not optional. Every modern application runs on cloud infrastructure. Chapter 11 teaches you AWS deployment, Docker, and CI/CD — the skills companies ask for in every job description.

The fundamentals do not go out of date. Java, SQL, HTTP, algorithms, design patterns — these have not changed in their essentials for decades, and they will not change in your career. Everything else is built on top of them.

Build the fundamentals first. The rest follows.

1.6 What Is a Computer, Really?

You have used a computer for years. You know how to browse the internet, open files, send emails. But do you know what is actually happening inside the machine when you do those things? Understanding this — even at a basic level — will make everything about programming click faster.

A Machine That Follows Instructions — Extremely Fast

Here is the most important thing to understand about computers:

A computer does not think. It follows instructions. Very, very fast.

That is it. Everything — every app, every website, every video game, every banking system — is just a very long, very carefully written list of instructions that a computer follows. The computer itself has no creativity, no judgment, no common sense. It only does exactly what it is told.

This is actually good news for you, because it means programming is not about being clever or mathematical. It is about learning the right way to write instructions that the computer can follow. And that is something anyone can learn.

The Three Most Important Parts of a Computer

Imagine your kitchen. When you cook a meal: - You use counter space to prepare the ingredients - You store food in the refrigerator and cupboards for later - You use a chef’s brain to decide what to do and in what order

A computer works the same way:

RAM (Random Access Memory) — Your Desk

RAM is where the computer works right now. When you open an app, the computer copies it from storage into RAM so it can work with it quickly. RAM is fast — very fast. But it is also temporary: the moment you turn the computer off, everything in RAM is gone, like clearing your desk at the end of the day.

This is why you have lost work when your computer crashed — it was in RAM and had not been saved yet.

Storage (Hard Drive or SSD) — Your Filing Cabinet

Storage is where files live permanently. Your documents, photos, music, installed programs — they all live on storage. When you turn the computer off and back on, they are all still there. Storage is slower than RAM, but it remembers.

When you open a document, the computer copies it from storage (slow but permanent) into RAM (fast but temporary) so you can work on it quickly.

CPU (Central Processing Unit) — The Chef

The CPU is the actual “doer” — it reads instructions and carries them out. When you double-click an app, the CPU starts reading the app’s instructions one by one, billions of times per second, and following them. The CPU cannot do anything on its own — it only follows what it is told.

Modern computers have multiple CPUs (called “cores”) so they can follow multiple streams of instructions at the same time — like having multiple chefs in the kitchen working on different dishes simultaneously.

When you open a banking app on your computer:

1. CPU reads "open FinTrack" instruction
2. CPU tells storage: "send me the FinTrack program files"
3. Storage copies those files into RAM
4. CPU starts reading FinTrack's instructions from RAM and carrying them out
5. You see the login screen appear

Everything that happens on a computer follows this same basic pattern.

1.7 What Is Software?

Software is a set of instructions that tells the computer what to do. That is the whole definition.

A word processor is software. Instagram is software. The operating system (Windows, macOS, Linux) is software. A banking app is software. A video game is software.

All of these, underneath their beautiful interfaces and smooth animations, are just files full of instructions that a CPU reads and executes.

What Is an Operating System?

The operating system (OS) is the master software that manages everything else. When you see your desktop with icons and folders, that is the operating system. It handles:

  • Showing you the screen
  • Letting you click and type
  • Managing all your files
  • Letting multiple programs run at the same time
  • Connecting to the internet

Windows, macOS, and Linux are all operating systems. Servers (powerful computers that run websites and apps) usually run Linux.

When we build FinTrack, your code will talk to the operating system all the time — to open files, connect to a database, listen for network requests — even if you never think about it directly.

1.8 What Is Programming?

Programming is the act of writing those instructions that tell the computer what to do.

Here is the problem: computers only understand one language — binary, which is just ones and zeros. Nobody writes programs this way. It would take a lifetime to write a single app.

So humans invented programming languages — languages that are readable by humans, which get automatically translated into the ones and zeros that the CPU understands.

You write this (Java):
    System.out.println("Welcome to FinTrack!");

A program called a "compiler" translates it.

The CPU reads something like this (simplified):
    10110000 01000001 11101011 ...

You never have to see or understand the binary.
That translation happens automatically.

Why Java?

Java runs on almost any computer without modification. When you write Java code and compile it, you get instructions for an imaginary machine called the Java Virtual Machine (JVM). Every major operating system has a JVM. So your compiled Java program runs on all of them without any changes.

This is why Java’s original motto was “Write Once, Run Anywhere.” Java is also used by billions of devices worldwide and is one of the most in-demand programming skills for jobs.

1.9 Files, Folders, and the File System

Everything on a computer is stored as a file. Files have a name and an extension that tells you the type:

  • Main.java — a Java source code file
  • styles.css — a CSS style sheet
  • data.json — a JSON data file

Folders (also called directories) contain files and other folders, forming a tree:

Your computer:
└── Users
    └── YourName
        └── Projects
            └── fintrack           ← your banking app lives here
                ├── src
                │   └── Main.java
                └── pom.xml

Every file has a path — its full address:

  • Mac/Linux: /Users/YourName/Projects/fintrack/src/Main.java
  • Windows: C:\Users\YourName\Projects\fintrack\src\Main.java

Reading left to right, you go deeper into the folder tree. Programmers use paths constantly — to tell programs where to find files, where to save data, where to read configuration from.

1.10 The Terminal — Your Programmer’s Control Panel

Every computer has a terminal — a window where you type text commands. It looks intimidating at first. But it is one of the most powerful tools you will use.

How to Open the Terminal

  • Mac: Press Command + Space, type Terminal, press Enter
  • Windows: Install Git Bash (from git-scm.com), then open it
  • Linux: Press Ctrl + Alt + T

Your Six Essential Commands

When you open the terminal, you see a prompt — the terminal is waiting for your input.

pwd — “Where am I right now?”

$ pwd                                  # ← [Type this and press Enter]
/Users/YourName                        # ← [The terminal shows your current folder location]

ls — “Show me what is in this folder”

$ ls                                   # ← [Lists the contents of where you currently are]
Desktop  Documents  Downloads  Projects # ← [These are the files and folders here]

cd — “Move to a different folder”

$ cd Documents                         # ← [cd = Change Directory. Now you are inside Documents]
$ cd ..                                # ← [Two dots means "go up one level" — back to where you came from]
$ cd Projects/fintrack                 # ← [You can go multiple levels at once, separated by /]

mkdir — “Create a new folder”

$ mkdir Projects                       # ← [mkdir = Make Directory. Creates a folder named Projects]
$ mkdir Projects/fintrack              # ← [Creates fintrack inside the Projects folder]

touch — “Create a new empty file”

$ touch notes.txt                      # ← [Creates an empty file called notes.txt here]

clear — “Clean up the screen”

$ clear                                # ← [Removes all previous output — gives you a fresh, empty terminal]

Try It Now — Your First Terminal Session

Open your terminal and type these commands one at a time, pressing Enter after each:

pwd                                    # ← [See where you currently are]
ls                                     # ← [See what is here]
mkdir FinTrackProject                  # ← [Create your project folder]
cd FinTrackProject                     # ← [Move into it]
pwd                                    # ← [Confirm you are now inside FinTrackProject]
mkdir src                              # ← [Create a src folder inside FinTrackProject]
ls                                     # ← [You should now see "src" listed]
cd ..                                  # ← [Go back up to where you were before]
pwd                                    # ← [Confirm you are back]

If you did that without errors — congratulations. You just navigated your computer the way professional developers do every day.

Understanding Error Messages

If you mistype a command, the terminal will tell you:

$ cd Docoments                         # ← [Typo: "Docoments" does not exist]
bash: cd: Docoments: No such file or directory  # ← [The terminal tells you EXACTLY what went wrong]

This is not a crash. It is information. Check your spelling and try again.

This is the single most important mindset shift in programming: an error message is not a failure. It is information. Read it carefully, understand what it is saying, and fix the specific problem it describes.

1.11 The Internet — How It Actually Works

FinTrack will be a web application — a React frontend in the browser, talking to a Java backend on a server. To understand what we are building, you need to understand how the internet works at a basic level.

What Happens When You Open a Website

Type www.google.com into your browser. Here is what happens in the next fraction of a second:

Step 1 — DNS Lookup (like looking up a phone number)

The name www.google.com is human-friendly, but computers communicate using numbers called IP addresses (like 142.250.80.46). Your computer contacts a DNS server (the phone book of the internet) to translate the name into a number.

Step 2 — TCP Connection (like dialing the phone)

Your computer opens a connection to Google’s server at that IP address using rules called TCP/IP — agreements about how computers talk to each other.

Step 3 — HTTP Request (like saying what you want)

Your browser sends a message:

GET / HTTP/1.1                         # ← [GET means "give me". / means "the home page". HTTP/1.1 is the protocol version]
Host: www.google.com                   # ← [Which website you are contacting — a server can host many sites]
Accept: text/html                      # ← [Tells the server what format of response you can handle]

Step 4 — HTTP Response (the server answers)

Google sends back:

HTTP/1.1 200 OK                        # ← [200 is the status code. 200 means "everything worked, here is your answer"]
Content-Type: text/html                # ← [Tells your browser what format the response is in]

<html>...</html>                       # ← [The actual web page content starts here]

Step 5 — Rendering

Your browser reads the HTML code and draws the page on your screen.

HTTP Status Codes — The Server’s One-Word Answer

Every HTTP response includes a three-digit status code:

CodeMeaningWhen you will see it
200OK — successSuccessful login, loaded account data
201Created — something new was madeNew account opened
400Bad Request — invalid data sentTransfer with negative amount
401Unauthorized — not logged inAccessing account without logging in
403Forbidden — logged in but not allowedViewing another customer’s account
404Not Found — does not existAccount number that does not exist
500Internal Server Error — server crashedA bug in our backend code

1.12 How Professional Teams Build Software — The SDLC

Only about 30% of a developer’s time is actually spent writing code. The rest is planning, communicating, reviewing, testing, and maintaining. Understanding how teams work makes you valuable from day one.

The Six Phases

Phase 1 — Requirements: “What are we building?” Interviews with bank managers and customers produce a list of specific things the system must do. Every requirement is written down, reviewed, and agreed upon before anyone touches code.

Phase 2 — Design: “How are we building it?” Architecture decisions: Java and Spring Boot for the backend, React for the frontend, PostgreSQL for the database, AWS for hosting. Bad design decisions made here can doom a project for years.

Phase 3 — Implementation: “Now we code” If requirements and design were done well, implementation is almost mechanical — you have a clear plan and follow it.

Phase 4 — Testing: “Does it actually work?” Unit tests, integration tests, edge case testing. What happens if someone transfers a negative amount? What if two people withdraw the last $500 simultaneously?

Phase 5 — Deployment: “Now the world can use it” Packaging into Docker containers, running on AWS servers, automated checks before anything goes live.

Phase 6 — Maintenance: “The software is never finished” Users find bugs. Requirements change. Security vulnerabilities appear. Code you wrote six months ago will be modified when requirements change — it needs to be readable.

Agile — Short Repeated Cycles

Traditional Waterfall does all requirements, then all design, then all coding — taking months before any working software exists. By then, requirements have changed.

Agile does all phases in short cycles called sprints (typically two weeks). In each sprint: 1. Pick a small set of requirements 2. Design, build, and test just those features 3. Demonstrate working software to stakeholders 4. Get feedback and plan the next sprint

FinTrack is built Agile-style in this book — each chapter delivers real, working software.

User Stories — Requirements That Sound Human

As a bank customer,                    # ← [WHO wants this feature]
I want to see my current balance,      # ← [WHAT they want to do]
so that I know how much I can spend.   # ← [WHY it matters to them]

Acceptance criteria:                   # ← [The specific conditions that mean this story is "done"]
- Balance shown is up-to-date          # ← [Not cached from yesterday]
- Customer can only see their OWN accounts  # ← [Not other customers' accounts]

1.13 Variables and Logic — The Building Blocks of All Programs

Every program, no matter how complex, is built from three basic things: storing information, making decisions, and repeating actions.

Storing Information — Variables

A variable is a named storage location. Think of it as a labelled box:

┌─────────────────┐
│    balance      │  ← the label (the variable name)
│                 │
│   $2,500.00     │  ← the contents (the value stored inside)
└─────────────────┘

You can look inside at any time (read the value) or change what is inside (update the value). The label stays the same; only the contents change.

Making Decisions — Conditionals

Programs do different things based on conditions:

“IF my balance is enough, THEN approve. OTHERWISE, decline.”

Repeating Actions — Loops

Programs often do the same action many times:

“FOR each of the last 30 transactions, add the amount to the total.”

Instead of writing the same instruction 30 times, a loop writes it once and repeats it automatically.

These three concepts are the same in every programming language. The syntax (spelling) differs, but the ideas are identical.

1.15 Your First Java Program

Now let us write something. This is the moment you become a programmer.

Step 1: Open IntelliJ IDEA Step 2: Click “New Project” → Choose “Java” → Select JDK 21 → Click Create Step 3: Name the project FinTrack Step 4: Right-click the src folder → New → Java Class → name it Main Step 5: Replace all the template code with this:

public class Main {                                  // ← [Creates a class named "Main". In Java, ALL code must live inside a class. The { opens the class body.]
    public static void main(String[] args) {         // ← [THE ENTRY POINT. When you run the program, Java finds this exact method name and starts executing here. Memorise this line — it appears in every Java program.]
        System.out.println("Hello! Welcome to FinTrack.");      // ← [Prints this text to the screen, then moves to the next line. System.out = the screen output. println = "print line".]
        System.out.println("The banking app you are building."); // ← [Prints a second line. Each println() creates a new line of output.]
        System.out.println("================================");  // ← [Prints a line of equals signs — a simple visual divider.]
        System.out.println("Starting up...");                    // ← [Prints the last line of output.]
    }                                                // ← [This } closes the main method that was opened on line 2.]
}                                                    // ← [This } closes the Main class that was opened on line 1. Every { needs a matching }.]

Step 6: Click the green Play button (▶) at the top, or right-click in the code and choose “Run ‘Main’”

You should see at the bottom of the screen:

Hello! Welcome to FinTrack.
The banking app you are building.
================================
Starting up...

You just ran your first Java program.

Understanding Every Word

Let us go through each piece of that code and understand exactly what it does:

public — means “this is visible to everything outside this file.” We will cover visibility fully in Chapter 4.

class — the keyword that creates a class. A class is a named container for related code.

Main — the name we chose for our class. Class names in Java always start with a capital letter.

{ and } — every opening brace must have a matching closing brace. Everything between them “belongs to” the thing they wrap.

static — means this method belongs to the class itself, not to an object created from it. Chapter 4 covers this in depth.

void — means this method returns no value. It just does something and finishes.

main — this specific name is special. Java looks for a method named exactly main when you run a program.

String[] args — a way to pass information to your program when starting it from the terminal. You will use this later; for now, just include it.

System.out.println(...) — three parts: System is a built-in class. out is its output channel. println is a method that prints one line. The text goes inside the parentheses, in quotes.

; — the semicolon ends every statement in Java. Like a period ends a sentence in English. Forget it and you get an error.

1.16 Variables — Storing Information

Now let us store some data about a bank account:

public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {

        // Lines starting with // are COMMENTS — the computer ignores them.
        // Comments are notes for humans reading the code.

        String customerName = "Alice Johnson";  // ← [String means text. customerName is the variable label. = assigns "Alice Johnson" as the value. The ; ends the statement.]
        String accountNumber = "CHK-0001";      // ← [Another text variable. Account numbers are stored as text (not numbers) because we never do maths on them.]
        int accountBalance = 2500;              // ← [int means a whole number. 2500 has no quotes — it is a number, not text. int stands for "integer".]
        boolean accountIsActive = true;         // ← [boolean holds exactly two possible values: true or false. Nothing else. It is named after mathematician George Boole.]

        System.out.println("Welcome, " + customerName + "!");   // ← [The + joins pieces of text together. "Welcome, " + "Alice Johnson" + "!" becomes "Welcome, Alice Johnson!"]
        System.out.println("Account: " + accountNumber);        // ← [Joins the label "Account: " with the value stored in accountNumber]
        System.out.println("Balance: $" + accountBalance);      // ← [Java converts the number 2500 to text "2500" automatically when joining with a string]
        System.out.println("Active: " + accountIsActive);       // ← [true becomes the text "true" automatically]
    }
}

Output:

Welcome, Alice Johnson!
Account: CHK-0001
Balance: $2500
Active: true

Java’s Data Types — What Kind of Box Is It?

Just like a bank has different types of accounts for different purposes, Java has different types of variables for different kinds of data:

TypeHoldsExampleNotes
StringText"Alice Johnson"Always in double quotes
intWhole numbers2500No decimal point
doubleDecimal numbers2500.75Has a decimal point — but use BigDecimal for money
booleanTrue or falsetrueExactly two possible values
BigDecimalExact decimal numbersnew BigDecimal("2500.75")Always use this for money, never double

1.17 Making Decisions — If/Else

Let us make the program approve or decline a withdrawal based on the actual balance:

public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {

        String customerName = "Alice Johnson";    // ← [Text variable for the customer name]
        int accountBalance = 2500;                // ← [The current balance in the account]
        int withdrawalAmount = 3000;              // ← [How much Alice wants to withdraw]

        System.out.println("Customer: " + customerName);
        System.out.println("Current balance: $" + accountBalance);
        System.out.println("Requested: $" + withdrawalAmount);
        System.out.println("---");

        if (accountBalance >= withdrawalAmount) {      // ← [IF the balance is enough (greater than or equal to the amount requested)...]
            int newBalance = accountBalance - withdrawalAmount; // ← [...THEN calculate the new balance by subtracting the withdrawal]
            System.out.println("Withdrawal APPROVED.");        // ← [Print approval message to screen]
            System.out.println("New balance: $" + newBalance); // ← [Show the updated balance]
        } else {                                               // ← [OTHERWISE (the balance is NOT enough)...]
            System.out.println("Withdrawal DECLINED.");        // ← [...print a decline message]
            System.out.println("Insufficient funds.");
            int shortfall = withdrawalAmount - accountBalance; // ← [Calculate how much more they would need]
            System.out.println("You need $" + shortfall + " more."); // ← [Show the shortfall amount]
        }                                                      // ← [This } ends the else block]
    }
}

Output with balance=2500 and withdrawal=3000:

Customer: Alice Johnson
Current balance: $2500
Requested: $3000
* * *
Withdrawal DECLINED.
Insufficient funds.
You need $500 more.

Now change withdrawalAmount from 3000 to 500 and run again. The program makes a different decision based on different data.

The Comparison Operators

Inside the if (...) you write a condition. Here are the comparison symbols:

SymbolMeaningExample
==Equal tobalance == 0 — is balance exactly zero?
!=Not equal totype != "SAVINGS" — is it not a savings account?
>Greater thanamount > 0 — is amount positive?
<Less thanbalance < 100 — is balance under $100?
>=Greater than or equalbalance >= amount — can we cover it?
<=Less than or equaltransactions <= 6 — within the monthly limit?

1.18 Repeating Actions — Loops

What if we have multiple transactions to display? A loop lets us repeat an action automatically:

public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {

        int[] amounts = {500, -75, 1200, -300, -89};          // ← [An array — a numbered list of values. [] means array. {500, -75, ...} is the list of values inside.]
        String[] descriptions = {                              // ← [A String array — a numbered list of text values]
            "Payroll deposit",                                 // ← [Index 0 — arrays start counting at 0, not 1]
            "Grocery store",                                   // ← [Index 1]
            "Freelance payment",                               // ← [Index 2]
            "Electricity bill",                                // ← [Index 3]
            "Online shopping"                                  // ← [Index 4]
        };

        System.out.println("Transaction History:");
        System.out.println("====================");

        for (int i = 0; i < amounts.length; i++) {            // ← [FOR loop. i is a counter. Start: i=0. Condition: keep going while i < 5 (amounts.length is 5). Step: i++ means add 1 each time.]
            int amount = amounts[i];                           // ← [amounts[i] gets the value at position i. When i=0, this gets 500. When i=1, this gets -75. And so on.]
            String description = descriptions[i];              // ← [Gets the description at the same position i. So amount and description always match up.]
            String type = amount > 0 ? "CREDIT" : "DEBIT";   // ← [Shorthand if/else: if amount > 0, type = "CREDIT", otherwise type = "DEBIT". The ? and : make this a one-liner.]
            System.out.println(description + " | " + type + " | $" + Math.abs(amount)); // ← [Math.abs() removes the minus sign — gives the absolute (positive) value of a number.]
        }                                                      // ← [End of the for loop. Goes back to the top and runs again with i increased by 1.]
    }
}

Output:

Transaction History:
====================
Payroll deposit | CREDIT | $500
Grocery store | DEBIT | $75
Freelance payment | CREDIT | $1200
Electricity bill | DEBIT | $300
Online shopping | DEBIT | $89

The loop ran five times — once for each item — without us writing five separate print statements.

How the For Loop Works Step by Step

Iteration 1: i = 0  →  amounts[0] = 500   →  descriptions[0] = "Payroll deposit"   →  prints line 1
Iteration 2: i = 1  →  amounts[1] = -75   →  descriptions[1] = "Grocery store"     →  prints line 2
Iteration 3: i = 2  →  amounts[2] = 1200  →  descriptions[2] = "Freelance payment" →  prints line 3
Iteration 4: i = 3  →  amounts[3] = -300  →  descriptions[3] = "Electricity bill"  →  prints line 4
Iteration 5: i = 4  →  amounts[4] = -89   →  descriptions[4] = "Online shopping"   →  prints line 5
i = 5  →  5 < 5 is FALSE  →  loop stops

1.19 Common Mistakes Every Beginner Makes

Reading these now will save you hours of frustration later:

Missing Semicolons

System.out.println("Hello")    // ← [WRONG — missing the semicolon at the end]
System.out.println("Hello");   // ← [CORRECT — every statement ends with ;]

Wrong Capitalisation

string name = "Alice";   // ← [WRONG — "string" with a lowercase s is not a Java type. Java is case-sensitive.]
String name = "Alice";   // ← [CORRECT — "String" with a capital S is the Java text type]

Unmatched Braces

public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        System.out.println("Hello");
    }
                           // ← [WRONG — missing the closing } for the class. Every { needs a }.]

public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        System.out.println("Hello");
    }
}                          // ← [CORRECT — both the method and class are properly closed]

Using == to Compare Text

String type = "CHECKING";
if (type == "CHECKING") { ... }       // ← [WRONG — == compares memory locations for objects, not the text content. This may not work as expected.]
if (type.equals("CHECKING")) { ... }  // ← [CORRECT — .equals() compares the actual text characters]

Reading Error Messages Too Quickly

When you get an error, stop. Read the message completely. Find the line number it mentions. Go to that line. The error message is almost always right — it tells you exactly what is wrong.

1.20 FinTrack — Planning Your Banking Application

Professional developers never start by writing code. They start by understanding what they are building. Your first real task is to plan FinTrack.

What FinTrack Will Be

By the end of this book, FinTrack will be a complete, real banking web application:

For customers: register and log in securely, see all accounts and balances, deposit and withdraw money, transfer between accounts, view complete transaction history.

For the bank: administrators can view any account and investigate suspicious patterns.

Under the hood: React frontend + Java/Spring Boot backend + PostgreSQL database + JWT security + Docker + AWS.

Your First Project Task — Write User Stories

Write at least eight user stories for FinTrack using this format:

As a [who],
I want to [what],
so that [why].

Acceptance criteria:
- [specific condition 1]
- [specific condition 2]

Features to cover: checking balance, making a deposit, making a withdrawal, transferring between accounts, viewing transaction history, registering, logging in, logging out.

This planning work is real software engineering. Professional teams do exactly this before writing any code.

Chapter Summary

Computers follow instructions — very fast. RAM (temporary workspace), storage (permanent memory), and CPU (the instruction follower) work together every time you run a program.

Software is just files full of instructions. Programming is writing those instructions in a human-readable language that gets translated for the CPU.

The filesystem organises everything into files and folders with paths. Navigate using pwd, ls, cd, mkdir in the terminal.

The terminal is your programmer’s control panel. Error messages are information, not failures.

HTTP is the language browsers and servers speak. Clients make requests; servers send responses with status codes (200 = OK, 404 = not found, 500 = server error).

The SDLC guides professional software from idea to production. Agile does it in short sprints. User stories capture requirements from the user’s perspective.

Variables store information. Conditionals make decisions. Loops repeat actions. These three concepts are the foundation of all programming.

Your environment is ready — JDK 21, IntelliJ IDEA, and Git are installed.

You have written real Java code — a variable declaration, an if/else statement, and a for loop. That is not nothing.

Interview Prep

  • What is the difference between RAM and storage? Why does it matter for application design?
  • What happens, step by step, when you type a website address into a browser?
  • What does HTTP stand for? What are HTTP status codes and why do they matter?
  • What is the difference between a client and a server?
  • Explain the SDLC. Why do teams use Agile instead of Waterfall for most modern projects?
  • What is a user story? Write one for a “change password” feature.
  • What is a variable? What is the difference between String, int, and boolean?
  • What does the JVM do? Why does it make Java “write once, run anywhere”?
  • What is Git? Why do developers use version control?
  • Why should you never use double for money in a banking application?

Quick Knowledge Check

  • True or False: RAM keeps its contents when the computer is turned off. (False — RAM is temporary. Storage is permanent.)
  • Fill in the blank: A ___ is a human-readable language that gets translated into instructions the CPU understands. (programming language)
  • What HTTP status code means “the resource you asked for does not exist”? (404)
  • True or False: In Java, String and string are the same thing. (False — Java is case-sensitive. String is a valid type; string is not.)
  • What does System.out.println() do? (Prints a line of text to the screen/output, then moves to the next line.)
  • What command shows your current folder location in the terminal? (pwd — Print Working Directory)
  • In an if/else statement, when does the else block run? (Only when the if condition is false.)

Try This — Variables

  • Declare a variable called customerName and assign your own name to it
  • Declare a variable called accountBalance with the value 1500
  • Declare a variable called isActive and set it to true
  • Print all three variables using System.out.println
  • Change accountBalance to 2000 and print it again — notice you can change let variables but not final ones
// Your solution goes here
String customerName = "___________";    // ← fill in your name
int accountBalance  = ____;             // ← fill in the starting balance
boolean isActive    = ____;             // ← true or false?

System.out.println(customerName);
System.out.println(accountBalance);
System.out.println(isActive);

Common Mistakes in Chapter 1

❌ Missing semicolons

System.out.println("Hello")   // WRONG — missing ;
System.out.println("Hello");  // CORRECT

Every statement in Java ends with a semicolon. Think of it as the full stop at the end of a sentence. Forget it and Java will refuse to compile your code.

❌ Wrong capitalisation on String

string name = "Alice";   // WRONG — 'string' is not a Java type
String name = "Alice";   // CORRECT — capital S

Java is case-sensitive. String and string are completely different things.

❌ Using == to compare text

if (type == "CHECKING") { ... }      // WRONG — compares memory addresses
if (type.equals("CHECKING")) { ... } // CORRECT — compares actual text

This is one of the most common Java bugs. Always use .equals() for String comparison.

❌ Unmatched braces Every { must have a matching }. IntelliJ will show you unmatched braces in red. Count them before you run.

❌ Declaring a variable inside a loop then using it outside

for (int i = 0; i < 5; i++) {
    int total = 0;            // WRONG — total is created fresh each loop
}
System.out.println(total);   // ERROR — total doesn't exist here

int total = 0;                // CORRECT — declare BEFORE the loop
for (int i = 0; i < 5; i++) {
    total += amounts[i];
}
System.out.println(total);

Using AI Effectively — Chapter 1

You now have your environment set up and your first program running. This is the perfect moment to introduce how AI fits into your learning.

Ask AI to explain — not to do

When something confuses you, ask AI to explain it. But ask it to explain it the way a teacher would, not to just give you the answer:

“Can you explain what the JVM does, using an analogy I’ve never heard before?”
“Why does Java use a semicolon at the end of every statement? What would happen if we didn’t?”

Use AI to check your understanding

After completing a section, test yourself against AI:

“I think a variable is like a labelled box that stores a value. Is that accurate, and what am I missing?”

Ask AI to review your first program

Paste your Hello World program and ask:

“Here is my first Java program. What would you improve, and why?”

AI will often suggest better practices. Learn from the suggestions — but understand why before accepting them.

One rule to live by from day one

Never paste a problem into AI and copy-paste the solution without understanding it. If you cannot explain every line of the code AI gave you, you have not learned anything — you have just delayed the confusion to a more expensive moment.

As a [who], I want to [what], so that [why].

Write at least five from the customer’s perspective and two from the bank administrator’s perspective. For each story, also write two acceptance criteria — the specific conditions that mean the story is “done.”
