Chapter 2Frontend

Building the Frontend — HTML, CSS, and JavaScript

Chapter Objectives

By the end of this chapter you will be able to:

✓ Write valid semantic HTML5 for a complete web page including forms, tables, and navigation

✓ Apply CSS to control layout, typography, colours, spacing, and responsive behaviour using Flexbox and Grid

✓ Write JavaScript that reads user input, manipulates the DOM, handles events, and validates forms

✓ Explain the difference between synchronous and asynchronous code and use async/await to call an API

✓ Build the static HTML and CSS shell of the FinTrack login and dashboard pages

✓ Use browser developer tools to debug HTML, CSS, and JavaScript

Introduction

Before any data flows through your application, users need something to look at and interact with. This chapter builds the visual layer of FinTrack — the login screen, the account dashboard, and the transfer form. You will learn HTML for structure, CSS for style, and JavaScript for interactivity. None of these pages will talk to a real backend yet — we are building the shell. The wiring comes later.

2.1 HTML — The Skeleton of Every Web Page

HyperText Markup Language (HTML) describes the structure and content of a web page using tags — special keywords wrapped in angle brackets. Every visible element in a browser — a button, a table, a form input — is an HTML tag.

Document Structure

<!DOCTYPE html>                          <!-- ← [Tells the browser this is a modern HTML5 document. Always the first line of any HTML file.] -->
<html lang="en">                         <!-- ← [The root element — everything else goes inside this. lang="en" tells screen readers the language.] -->
<head>                                   <!-- ← [The head section contains metadata — information ABOUT the page, not content visible to the user.] -->
    <meta charset="UTF-8">              <!-- ← [Tells the browser to use UTF-8 encoding — supports all languages, emojis, and special characters.] -->
    <meta name="viewport"               <!-- ← [Controls how the page scales on mobile devices. Without this, phones would show a zoomed-out desktop view.] -->
          content="width=device-width, initial-scale=1.0">
    <title>FinTrack — Online Banking</title>  <!-- ← [The text shown in the browser tab and in search engine results.] -->
    <link rel="stylesheet" href="styles.css"> <!-- ← [Loads an external CSS file. rel="stylesheet" = this is a style sheet. href = where to find it.] -->
</head>
<body>                                   <!-- ← [Everything the user actually SEES goes inside the body.] -->
    <header>                             <!-- ← [Semantic tag — marks this as the page header (logo, nav bar). Screen readers understand what this is.] -->
        <nav><!-- navigation --></nav>   <!-- ← [nav = navigation. This is where links to other pages go.] -->
    </header>
    <main>                               <!-- ← [Semantic tag — marks the primary content of the page. There should be only ONE main per page.] -->
        <!-- primary content -->
    </main>
    <footer>                             <!-- ← [Semantic tag — marks the footer. Usually holds copyright, links, contact info.] -->
        <!-- footer content -->
    </footer>
    <script src="app.js"></script>       <!-- ← [Loads the JavaScript file. Goes at the BOTTOM of body so the HTML loads first before the script runs.] -->
</body>                                  <!-- ← [Closes the body element that opened above.] -->
</html>                                  <!-- ← [Closes the root html element. Nothing comes after this.] -->

The <head> section contains metadata that the browser uses but the user does not see directly. The <body> contains everything visible. Notice that the <script> tag goes at the bottom of the body — this ensures the HTML elements exist before JavaScript tries to interact with them.

Semantic HTML

HTML5 introduced semantic tags — tags whose names describe their purpose. Using semantic HTML improves accessibility (screen readers understand the structure), SEO, and code readability.

<!-- ❌ Non-semantic: meaningless structure -->
<div class="header">
    <div class="nav">...</div>
</div>
<div class="content">...</div>

<!-- ✅ Semantic: structure has meaning -->
<header>
    <nav>...</nav>
</header>
<main>
    <article>...</article>
    <aside>...</aside>
</main>
<footer>...</footer>

Forms — How Users Send Data

Forms are the primary way users send data to a server. Every form element has a name attribute — this becomes the key in the data sent to the backend.

<!-- FinTrack Login Form -->
<form id="loginForm"                     <!-- ← [Creates a form. id="loginForm" gives it a unique name so JavaScript can find it.] -->
      action="/api/auth/login"           <!-- ← [WHERE to send the form data when submitted — our backend login endpoint.] -->
      method="POST">                     <!-- ← [HOW to send it. POST puts the data in the request body (more secure than GET for passwords).] -->
    <div class="form-group">             <!-- ← [div = a general container. class="form-group" adds a CSS class for styling. Divs have no special meaning — just grouping.] -->
        <label for="email">Email Address</label> <!-- ← [A text label. for="email" links it to the input with id="email" — clicking the label focuses the input.] -->
        <input                           <!-- ← [An input field — a box the user can type into. Input is self-closing (no </input> needed).] -->
            type="email"                 <!-- ← [type="email" tells the browser to validate it looks like an email address and show the right keyboard on mobile.] -->
            id="email"                   <!-- ← [Unique identifier. Matches the label's for="email". JavaScript uses this to read the value.] -->
            name="email"                 <!-- ← [The KEY in the submitted data. When form is sent, this becomes the field name the server receives.] -->
            placeholder="you@example.com" <!-- ← [Grey hint text shown when the field is empty. Disappears when the user starts typing.] -->
            required                     <!-- ← [The browser will NOT submit the form if this field is empty. A simple form of validation.] -->
            autocomplete="email"         <!-- ← [Tells the browser this is an email field so it can offer to auto-fill saved email addresses.] -->
        >
    </div>
    <div class="form-group">
        <label for="password">Password</label>
        <input
            type="password"              <!-- ← [type="password" hides the characters as the user types — shows dots instead of letters.] -->
            id="password"
            name="password"
            required
            autocomplete="current-password" <!-- ← [Tells password managers this is the current password field (vs a new password field).] -->
        >
    </div>
    <button type="submit">Sign In</button>  <!-- ← [A clickable button. type="submit" means clicking it triggers the form submission.] -->
    <a href="/forgot-password">Forgot your password?</a> <!-- ← [a = anchor tag = a hyperlink. href is the URL it goes to when clicked.] -->
</form>                                  <!-- ← [Closes the form. Everything between <form> and </form> is part of this form.] -->

Key attributes: - type="email" — browser validates email format automatically - type="password" — browser hides the input characters - required — browser prevents form submission if the field is empty - for on <label> must match id on <input> — clicking the label focuses the input (important for accessibility)

Tables — Displaying Transaction Data

Transaction history is perfect for a table — structured, tabular data.

<table class="transaction-table" aria-label="Recent transactions">
    <thead>
        <tr>
            <th scope="col">Date</th>
            <th scope="col">Description</th>
            <th scope="col">Type</th>
            <th scope="col" class="amount-col">Amount</th>
            <th scope="col" class="amount-col">Balance</th>
        </tr>
    </thead>
    <tbody id="transactionTableBody">
        <!-- Rows will be injected by JavaScript -->
        <tr>
            <td>2025-01-15</td>
            <td>Direct Deposit — Employer</td>
            <td class="type-deposit">DEPOSIT</td>
            <td class="amount positive">+$3,200.00</td>
            <td class="amount">$5,450.00</td>
        </tr>
        <tr>
            <td>2025-01-14</td>
            <td>Amazon.com</td>
            <td class="type-withdrawal">WITHDRAWAL</td>
            <td class="amount negative">-$89.99</td>
            <td class="amount">$2,250.00</td>
        </tr>
    </tbody>
</table>

2.2 CSS — Making It Look Professional

Cascading Style Sheets (CSS) control how HTML elements look. “Cascading” means multiple style rules can apply to one element, and the browser resolves conflicts using a specificity system.

Selectors and Specificity

/* Element selector — applies to all <p> tags */
p {
    line-height: 1.6;
}

/* Class selector — applies to elements with class="card" */
.card {
    border-radius: 8px;
    box-shadow: 0 2px 8px rgba(0, 0, 0, 0.1);
}

/* ID selector — applies to the element with id="loginForm" */
#loginForm {
    max-width: 420px;
    margin: 0 auto;
}

/* Descendant selector — <button> inside .card */
.card button {
    width: 100%;
}

/* Pseudo-class — button when hovered */
button:hover {
    background-color: #1a5276;
    cursor: pointer;
}

Specificity resolves conflicts: ID selectors beat class selectors, which beat element selectors. When two rules have equal specificity, the one declared later wins.

The Box Model

Every HTML element is a rectangular box with four layers:

+----------------------------------+
|           margin                 |
|  +----------------------------+  |
|  |        border              |  |
|  |  +----------------------+  |  |
|  |  |      padding         |  |  |
|  |  |  +----------------+  |  |  |
|  |  |  |    content     |  |  |  |
|  |  |  +----------------+  |  |  |
|  |  +----------------------+  |  |
|  +----------------------------+  |
+----------------------------------+
.account-card {
    width: 340px;             /* ← [Sets the content width to 340 pixels. With box-sizing below, this includes padding and border.] */
    padding: 24px;            /* ← [Inner breathing room — 24px of space between the content and the border on ALL four sides.] */
    border: 1px solid #ddd;   /* ← [Draws a visible border. 1px = thickness. solid = line style. #ddd = a light grey color code.] */
    border-radius: 8px;       /* ← [Rounds the corners. 0px = sharp corners, higher values = more rounded. 8px gives a subtle card look.] */
    margin-bottom: 16px;      /* ← [Pushes the NEXT element 16px away below this card. Margin is space OUTSIDE the border.] */
    box-sizing: border-box;   /* ← [CRITICAL: Makes width include padding and border. Without this, 340px + 24px padding = 388px total — very confusing.] */
}

Always set box-sizing: border-box globally — it makes layout arithmetic predictable:

*, *::before, *::after {
    box-sizing: border-box;
}

CSS Flexbox — Laying Out in One Direction

Flexbox solves the classic layout problems: centering, equal-height columns, distributing space.

/* The navigation bar */
.navbar {
    display: flex;
    justify-content: space-between;  /* push logo left, links right */
    align-items: center;             /* vertically center all items */
    padding: 0 24px;
    height: 64px;
    background-color: #1F3864;
}

/* Account cards — side by side */
.accounts-grid {
    display: flex;
    gap: 16px;          /* space between cards */
    flex-wrap: wrap;    /* wrap to new line if not enough space */
}

/* Each card takes equal space but minimum 280px */
.account-card {
    flex: 1;
    min-width: 280px;
}

/* Center content vertically and horizontally */
.login-container {
    display: flex;
    justify-content: center;
    align-items: center;
    min-height: 100vh;
}

CSS Grid — Two-Dimensional Layouts

Grid is for two-dimensional layouts — rows AND columns simultaneously.

/* Dashboard layout: sidebar + main content */
.dashboard {
    display: grid;
    grid-template-columns: 240px 1fr;   /* fixed sidebar, flexible main */
    grid-template-rows: 64px 1fr;       /* fixed header, flexible content */
    grid-template-areas:
        "header  header"
        "sidebar main";
    min-height: 100vh;
}

.dashboard-header { grid-area: header; }
.dashboard-sidebar { grid-area: sidebar; }
.dashboard-main   { grid-area: main; }

Responsive Design with Media Queries

FinTrack must work on phones as well as desktops. Media queries apply different styles at different screen widths.

/* Default: mobile-first styles */
.accounts-grid {
    display: flex;
    flex-direction: column;   /* stack cards vertically on small screens */
}

/* Tablet and above (768px+) */
@media (min-width: 768px) {
    .accounts-grid {
        flex-direction: row;  /* side by side on larger screens */
    }

    .dashboard {
        display: grid;
        grid-template-columns: 240px 1fr;
    }
}

/* Desktop (1200px+) */
@media (min-width: 1200px) {
    .dashboard {
        grid-template-columns: 280px 1fr;
    }
}

FinTrack Color Palette and Typography

:root {
    /* Colors */
    --color-primary: #1F3864;         /* deep navy */
    --color-primary-light: #2E5395;   /* medium blue */
    --color-accent: #27AE60;          /* green for positive amounts */
    --color-danger: #E74C3C;          /* red for negative amounts */
    --color-background: #F4F6F9;
    --color-card: #FFFFFF;
    --color-text: #2C3E50;
    --color-text-muted: #7F8C8D;
    --color-border: #E0E0E0;

    /* Typography */
    --font-family: 'Inter', -apple-system, sans-serif;
    --font-size-base: 16px;
    --line-height-base: 1.6;

    /* Spacing */
    --spacing-sm: 8px;
    --spacing-md: 16px;
    --spacing-lg: 24px;
    --spacing-xl: 32px;

    /* Radius */
    --radius-sm: 4px;
    --radius-md: 8px;
    --radius-lg: 12px;
}

body {
    font-family: var(--font-family);
    font-size: var(--font-size-base);
    line-height: var(--line-height-base);
    color: var(--color-text);
    background-color: var(--color-background);
    margin: 0;
}

Using CSS custom properties (--color-primary) makes changing the entire color scheme a one-line edit.

2.3 JavaScript — Bringing the Page to Life

Using Browser Developer Tools

Press F12 (or right-click → Inspect) in Chrome or Firefox to open DevTools. This is your debugging headquarters for frontend work.

┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  FinTrack — Online Banking           ×  + │  ─  □  ✕            │
├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  🔒 localhost:3000                                               │
├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│                                                                  │
│  [FINTRACK APPLICATION VISIBLE HERE]                            │
│                                                                  │
├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ Elements │ Console │ Sources │ Network │ Application             │
├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ ▼ <div class="dashboard">                                       │
│     ▼ <div class="account-card checking">                       │
│         <span class="balance">$2,500.00</span>                  │

└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

The four tabs you will use most:

TabWhat it showsWhen to use it
ElementsHTML structure and CSS stylesFix layout and styling issues
ConsoleJavaScript errors and logsDebug JS, see console.log output
NetworkEvery HTTP request madeCheck API calls, see response data
ApplicationCookies, localStorage, tokensInspect JWT tokens and session data
📸 [Add screenshot of DevTools on your FinTrack login page here]

JavaScript makes pages interactive. It can respond to user actions, update the DOM without reloading the page, and communicate with APIs.

Variables and Modern Syntax

const MAX_TRANSFER_AMOUNT = 10000;  // ← [const = constant — this value CANNOT be changed after this line. Use SCREAMING_SNAKE_CASE for constants.]
const accountNumber = "CHK-0001";   // ← [Another constant. We never reassign accountNumber, so const is correct here.]

let currentBalance = 2500.00;       // ← [let = variable that CAN change. The balance will update after deposits and withdrawals.]
let selectedAccountId = null;       // ← [null means "no value yet". We don't know which account is selected until the user picks one.]

// Never use var — it has confusing scoping behavior

const greeting = `Welcome back, ${customerName}!`; // ← [Template literal — backticks (`) instead of quotes. ${} injects a variable's value directly into the string.]
const balanceDisplay = `Current balance: $${currentBalance.toFixed(2)}`; // ← [.toFixed(2) formats the number to exactly 2 decimal places — "2500.00" not "2500".]

Arrays and Objects

// Array of transactions
const transactions = [
    { id: 1, date: "2025-01-15", description: "Direct Deposit", amount: 3200, type: "DEPOSIT" },
    { id: 2, date: "2025-01-14", description: "Grocery Store",  amount: -89.50, type: "WITHDRAWAL" },
    { id: 3, date: "2025-01-13", description: "Transfer to Savings", amount: -500, type: "TRANSFER" },
];

// Access elements
console.log(transactions[0].description); // "Direct Deposit"

// Array methods — these are essential
const deposits = transactions.filter(t => t.type === "DEPOSIT");
const totalDeposited = transactions
    .filter(t => t.amount > 0)
    .reduce((sum, t) => sum + t.amount, 0);

const formattedDescriptions = transactions.map(t => t.description.toUpperCase());

// Find single item
const largeTransaction = transactions.find(t => Math.abs(t.amount) > 1000);

DOM Manipulation

The Document Object Model is JavaScript’s interface to the HTML page. You can read from it, modify it, create elements, and remove them.

const loginForm = document.getElementById("loginForm");      // ← [getElementById finds ONE element by its id. Returns the element object, or null if not found.]
const emailInput = document.querySelector("#email");         // ← [querySelector finds the FIRST element matching a CSS selector. # means id, . means class.]
const allCards = document.querySelectorAll(".account-card"); // ← [querySelectorAll finds ALL matching elements. Returns a NodeList (like an array).]

const currentText = document.getElementById("balanceDisplay").textContent; // ← [.textContent reads the text inside an element — what the user sees on screen.]
document.getElementById("balanceDisplay").textContent = "$2,500.00";       // ← [Setting .textContent REPLACES what is displayed — the page updates instantly.]

const userEmail = document.getElementById("email").value;    // ← [.value reads what the user TYPED into an input field. textContent is for display; value is for inputs.]

const errorMessage = document.getElementById("error");
errorMessage.style.display = "block";  // ← [Changes inline CSS. display: block makes the element visible (from hidden). This is less clean than using classes.]
errorMessage.style.color = "red";      // ← [Sets the text color to red. Works, but CSS classes are better practice.]

errorMessage.classList.add("visible");    // ← [Adds the CSS class "visible" to the element. Better than inline styles — keeps style in CSS files.]
errorMessage.classList.remove("hidden"); // ← [Removes the class "hidden". The element stays but loses that class's styling.]
errorMessage.classList.toggle("active"); // ← [If "active" class exists: removes it. If it doesn't: adds it. Useful for on/off states like menus.]

function createTransactionRow(transaction) {          // ← [Defines a reusable function. Call it with a transaction object, get back a table row element.]
    const row = document.createElement("tr");         // ← [Creates a new <tr> element in memory. Not on the page yet — just exists in JavaScript.]
    row.innerHTML = `                                 // ← [innerHTML sets the HTML content INSIDE the element. Template literal lets us write multi-line HTML cleanly.]
        <td>${transaction.date}</td>                  // ← [<td> = table data cell. ${} injects the transaction's date value.]
        <td>${transaction.description}</td>
        <td class="type-${transaction.type.toLowerCase()}">${transaction.type}</td>  // ← [.toLowerCase() converts "DEPOSIT" to "deposit" for the CSS class name.]
        <td class="amount ${transaction.amount > 0 ? 'positive' : 'negative'}">     // ← [Conditional class: adds "positive" or "negative" based on the amount.]
            ${transaction.amount > 0 ? '+' : ''}$${Math.abs(transaction.amount).toFixed(2)}  // ← [Shows + for positive amounts. Math.abs() removes the minus sign.]
        </td>
    `;
    return row;                                       // ← [Returns the finished row element so the caller can put it somewhere.]
}

const tbody = document.getElementById("transactionTableBody");
tbody.innerHTML = "";                                 // ← [Clears all existing rows from the table body before re-rendering. Prevents duplicates.]
transactions.forEach(t => tbody.appendChild(createTransactionRow(t))); // ← [For each transaction, creates a row and appends it to the table body.]

Event Handling

// Click events
document.getElementById("transferButton").addEventListener("click", function(event) {
    event.preventDefault(); // prevent default form submission
    handleTransfer();
});

// Form submission
document.getElementById("loginForm").addEventListener("submit", async function(event) {
    event.preventDefault();

    const email = document.getElementById("email").value.trim();
    const password = document.getElementById("password").value;

    if (!email || !password) {
        showError("Email and password are required");
        return;
    }

    await performLogin(email, password);
});

// Input change — real-time validation
document.getElementById("amount").addEventListener("input", function(event) {
    const value = parseFloat(event.target.value);
    const maxBalance = parseFloat(document.getElementById("currentBalance").dataset.balance);

    if (isNaN(value) || value <= 0) {
        showFieldError("amount", "Please enter a valid positive amount");
    } else if (value > maxBalance) {
        showFieldError("amount", `Cannot exceed your balance of $${maxBalance.toFixed(2)}`);
    } else {
        clearFieldError("amount");
    }
});

Async JavaScript — Talking to APIs

When JavaScript communicates with a server, it does so asynchronously — it sends the request and continues running other code, then handles the response when it arrives. The modern way to do this uses async/await.

// Async function — uses await to wait for asynchronous operations
async function loadAccountData(accountId) {  // ← [async marks this as asynchronous. Inside, you can use await to pause and wait without freezing the browser.]
    try {
        // fetch returns a Promise that resolves to a Response
        const response = await fetch(`/api/accounts/${accountId}`, {  // ← [fetch() sends an HTTP request. await pauses here until the response arrives. Does NOT freeze the browser.]
            headers: {
                "Authorization": `Bearer ${getToken()}`,  // ← [Sends the JWT login token so the server knows who is asking. Bearer is the standard token type prefix.]
                "Content-Type": "application/json"  // ← [Tells the server we are sending JSON and want JSON back.]
            }
        });

        if (!response.ok) {  // ← [response.ok is true for 2xx status codes. False for 4xx and 5xx. This catches server errors.]
            // HTTP error (4xx or 5xx)
            throw new Error(`Server returned ${response.status}: ${response.statusText}`);  // ← [throw creates an error and immediately jumps to the catch block.]
        }

        const account = await response.json();  // ← [.json() reads and parses the response body — also async. Returns a JavaScript object from the JSON text.]

        // Update the UI
        document.getElementById("balanceDisplay").textContent =
            `$${account.balance.toLocaleString("en-US", { minimumFractionDigits: 2 })}`;
        document.getElementById("accountNumber").textContent = account.accountNumber;

        return account;

    } catch (error) {
        // Network error or parsing error
        console.error("Failed to load account:", error);
        showError("Unable to load account data. Please try again.");
    }
}

// POST request — submit a transfer
async function submitTransfer(fromAccountId, toAccountId, amount, description) {
    const button = document.getElementById("transferButton");
    button.disabled = true;
    button.textContent = "Processing...";

    try {
        const response = await fetch("/api/transfers", {
            method: "POST",
            headers: {
                "Authorization": `Bearer ${getToken()}`,  // ← [Sends the JWT login token so the server knows who is asking. Bearer is the standard token type prefix.]
                "Content-Type": "application/json"  // ← [Tells the server we are sending JSON and want JSON back.]
            },
            body: JSON.stringify({
                fromAccountId,
                toAccountId,
                amount,
                description
            })
        });

        if (response.status === 400) {
            const error = await response.json();
            showError(error.message);
            return;
        }

        if (!response.ok) {  // ← [response.ok is true for 2xx status codes. False for 4xx and 5xx. This catches server errors.]
            throw new Error(`Transfer failed: ${response.status}`);
        }

        const result = await response.json();
        showSuccess(`Transfer of $${amount} completed successfully`);
        await loadAccountData(fromAccountId); // refresh balance

    } catch (error) {
        showError("Transfer failed. Please try again.");
    } finally {
        button.disabled = false;
        button.textContent = "Transfer Funds";
    }
}

Chapter Summary

  • HTML provides structure through semantic tags. Use the right tag for the right purpose — forms for user input, tables for tabular data, header/main/footer for page layout.
  • CSS controls appearance. The box model, Flexbox, and Grid are the three essential layout tools.
  • CSS custom properties make design systems maintainable — change one value to update the entire theme.
  • JavaScript adds interactivity through DOM manipulation and event handling.
  • async/await is the modern pattern for API communication. Always handle both success and error states.
  • Use const by default, let when the value must change, and never var.

Interview Prep

  • What is the DOM, and how is it different from the raw HTML source of a page?
  • Explain the CSS box model. What does box-sizing: border-box do, and why is it useful?
  • What is the difference between === and == in JavaScript?
  • What is event bubbling? Give an example where it matters.
  • Explain what async/await does. What problem does it solve compared to callback-based code?
  • What HTTP methods would you use to create, retrieve, update, and delete a bank account?

Quick Knowledge Check

  • True or False: CSS class selectors have higher specificity than ID selectors. (False — ID selectors have higher specificity.)
  • What does event.preventDefault() do in a form submit handler? (Prevents the default browser behavior of submitting the form and reloading the page.)
  • Fill in the blank: The fetch API returns a ___, which must be awaited to get the actual response. (Promise)
  • True or False: querySelector returns all matching elements as a NodeList. (False — it returns only the first match. querySelectorAll returns all.)
  • What CSS property enables Flexbox on a container? (display: flex)

Build a deposit form from scratch without looking at your notes:

  • Create a <form> with id="depositForm"
  • Add a labelled input for “Account Number” (type text, required)
  • Add a labelled input for “Amount” (type number, min=“0.01”, required)
  • Add a dropdown (<select>) for “Transaction Type” with options: Deposit, Withdrawal, Transfer

Then add JavaScript that: - Prevents the default form submission - Reads the amount the user typed - Alerts “Depositing $X to account Y” using the values from the form

Common Mistakes in Chapter 2

❌ Forgetting alt text on images

<img src="logo.png">              <!-- WRONG — no alt text -->
<img src="logo.png" alt="FinTrack logo">  <!-- CORRECT -->

Alt text is required for accessibility and affects search engine ranking.

❌ Using id when you mean class An id must be unique on the page. A class can be reused. If you want to style multiple elements the same way, use a class.

❌ Forgetting box-sizing: border-box Without it, adding padding to a 400px element makes it wider than 400px. Always add this to your CSS:

* { box-sizing: border-box; }

❌ Using var instead of let or const in JavaScript var has confusing scoping rules. Use const for values that won’t change, let for values that will. Never use var in modern JavaScript.

❌ Not handling fetch errors

const response = await fetch('/api/accounts');
const data = await response.json();              // WRONG if response is 404 or 500

const response = await fetch('/api/accounts');
if (!response.ok) throw new Error('Request failed'); // CORRECT
const data = await response.json();

Using AI Effectively — Chapter 2

Ask AI to review your CSS

Paste your CSS and ask:

“This CSS is supposed to centre the login form on the page. It is not working. What is wrong?”

AI is excellent at spotting CSS bugs. But always read the explanation — CSS bugs teach you how the box model works.

Use AI to understand browser compatibility

“Does CSS Grid work in all modern browsers? Are there any properties I should avoid?”

Ask AI to help you think through JavaScript logic

Before writing code, describe what you want to achieve:

“I want to show an error message if the user types a negative amount in the deposit field. The error should disappear when they fix it. Walk me through how to structure this.”

Get the plan first, then write the code yourself.

Do NOT ask AI to build the whole form for you

If you let AI build your Chapter 2 project, you will reach Chapter 9 (Spring Boot) unable to understand what your own frontend is doing. Every hour you save now costs three hours later.

Chapter 3Frontend

React — Building the Modern Frontend

Chapter Objectives

By the end of this chapter you will be able to:

✓ Explain what React is and why it exists — specifically what problem it solves compared to vanilla JavaScript

✓ Create functional React components that accept and use props

✓ Manage component state with useState and trigger re-renders correctly

✓ Use useEffect to load data when a component mounts and respond to state changes

✓ Connect a React component to a backend API using fetch with proper loading and error states

✓ Implement client-side routing with React Router so your app has multiple pages without full reloads

✓ Build the FinTrack dashboard with account cards and a transaction list pulling from a mock API

Introduction

Plain HTML/CSS/JS works, but as applications grow more interactive, managing state and DOM updates manually becomes unwieldy. React solves this by letting you describe what the UI should look like at any given moment, and handling all the DOM updates automatically. This chapter rebuilds FinTrack’s frontend as a proper React application.

3.1 Components — Building Blocks

In React, everything is a component — a reusable function that returns JSX (which looks like HTML but compiles to JavaScript).

// Simplest possible component
function WelcomeBanner({ customerName }) {
    return (
        <div className="welcome-banner">
            <h2>Welcome back, {customerName}!</h2>
        </div>
    );
}

// Usage
<WelcomeBanner customerName="Alice Johnson" />

JSX rules: - All tags must be closed (<input /> not <input>) - Use className instead of class (since class is a JavaScript reserved word) - Wrap multiple elements in a single parent (<>...</> fragment works) - JavaScript expressions go in {}

A Real Account Card Component

function AccountCard({ account, onDeposit, onWithdraw, onTransfer }) {
    const isLowBalance = account.balance < 100;

    return (
        <div className={`account-card ${isLowBalance ? 'account-card--low-balance' : ''}`}>
            <div className="account-card__header">
                <span className="account-type-badge">{account.accountType}</span>
                <span className="account-number">{account.accountNumber}</span>
            </div>

            <div className="account-balance">
                <span className="balance-label">Available Balance</span>
                <span className={`balance-amount ${isLowBalance ? 'balance--warning' : ''}`}>
                    {formatCurrency(account.balance)}
                </span>
            </div>

            {isLowBalance && (
                <div className="low-balance-alert">
                    ⚠ Balance below $100
                </div>
            )}

            <div className="account-actions">
                <button onClick={() => onDeposit(account.id)} className="btn btn-primary">
                    Deposit
                </button>
                <button onClick={() => onWithdraw(account.id)} className="btn btn-secondary">
                    Withdraw
                </button>
                <button onClick={() => onTransfer(account.id)} className="btn btn-secondary">
                    Transfer
                </button>
            </div>
        </div>
    );
}

function formatCurrency(amount) {
    return new Intl.NumberFormat('en-US', {
        style: 'currency',
        currency: 'USD'
    }).format(amount);
}

3.2 State and Hooks

State is data owned by a component that can change over time, triggering re-renders when it does.

import { useState, useEffect, useCallback } from 'react';

function Dashboard() {
    // useState returns [currentValue, setterFunction]
    const [accounts, setAccounts] = useState([]);
    const [selectedAccountId, setSelectedAccountId] = useState(null);
    const [transactions, setTransactions] = useState([]);
    const [loading, setLoading] = useState(true);
    const [error, setError] = useState(null);

    // useEffect runs after render — use for side effects (API calls, subscriptions)
    useEffect(() => {
        loadAccounts();
    }, []); // empty dependency array = run once on mount

    useEffect(() => {
        if (selectedAccountId) {
            loadTransactions(selectedAccountId);
        }
    }, [selectedAccountId]); // re-run when selectedAccountId changes

    const loadAccounts = async () => {
        try {
            setLoading(true);
            const response = await api.get('/accounts');
            setAccounts(response.data);
            if (response.data.length > 0) {
                setSelectedAccountId(response.data[0].id);
            }
        } catch (err) {
            setError('Failed to load accounts. Please try again.');
        } finally {
            setLoading(false);
        }
    };

    const loadTransactions = async (accountId) => {
        try {
            const response = await api.get(`/accounts/${accountId}/transactions`);
            setTransactions(response.data);
        } catch (err) {
            setError('Failed to load transactions.');
        }
    };

    const handleDeposit = useCallback(async (accountId, amount) => {
        try {
            await api.post(`/accounts/${accountId}/deposit`, { amount });
            await loadAccounts(); // refresh balances
        } catch (err) {
            setError(err.response?.data?.message || 'Deposit failed');
        }
    }, []);

    if (loading) return <LoadingSpinner />;
    if (error)   return <ErrorMessage message={error} onRetry={loadAccounts} />;

    return (
        <div className="dashboard">
            <AccountsList
                accounts={accounts}
                selectedId={selectedAccountId}
                onSelect={setSelectedAccountId}
                onDeposit={handleDeposit}
            />
            <TransactionHistory
                transactions={transactions}
                accountId={selectedAccountId}
            />
        </div>
    );
}

3.3 The Transfer Form — Controlled Components

A controlled component stores its value in React state, giving you complete control over input behavior.

function TransferForm({ accounts, onTransfer, onCancel }) {
    const [formData, setFormData] = useState({
        fromAccountId: accounts[0]?.id || '',
        toAccountId: '',
        amount: '',
        description: ''
    });
    const [errors, setErrors] = useState({});
    const [submitting, setSubmitting] = useState(false);
    const [success, setSuccess] = useState(false);

    const validate = () => {
        const newErrors = {};

        if (!formData.fromAccountId) newErrors.fromAccountId = 'Please select a source account';
        if (!formData.toAccountId)   newErrors.toAccountId   = 'Please select a destination account';
        if (formData.fromAccountId === formData.toAccountId) {
            newErrors.toAccountId = 'Source and destination must be different accounts';
        }

        const amount = parseFloat(formData.amount);
        if (isNaN(amount) || amount <= 0) {
            newErrors.amount = 'Please enter a positive amount';
        } else {
            const fromAccount = accounts.find(a => a.id === parseInt(formData.fromAccountId));
            if (fromAccount && amount > fromAccount.balance) {
                newErrors.amount = `Amount exceeds available balance of ${formatCurrency(fromAccount.balance)}`;
            }
        }

        setErrors(newErrors);
        return Object.keys(newErrors).length === 0;
    };

    const handleChange = (e) => {
        const { name, value } = e.target;
        setFormData(prev => ({ ...prev, [name]: value }));
        // Clear error when user starts fixing it
        if (errors[name]) {
            setErrors(prev => ({ ...prev, [name]: null }));
        }
    };

    const handleSubmit = async (e) => {
        e.preventDefault();
        if (!validate()) return;

        setSubmitting(true);
        try {
            await onTransfer({
                fromAccountId: parseInt(formData.fromAccountId),
                toAccountId: parseInt(formData.toAccountId),
                amount: parseFloat(formData.amount),
                description: formData.description
            });
            setSuccess(true);
            setTimeout(() => setSuccess(false), 3000);
        } catch (err) {
            setErrors({ submit: err.message || 'Transfer failed' });
        } finally {
            setSubmitting(false);
        }
    };

    return (
        <form onSubmit={handleSubmit} className="transfer-form" noValidate>
            <h2>Transfer Funds</h2>

            {success && <div className="alert alert-success">Transfer completed successfully!</div>}
            {errors.submit && <div className="alert alert-error">{errors.submit}</div>}

            <div className="form-group">
                <label htmlFor="fromAccountId">From Account</label>
                <select
                    id="fromAccountId"
                    name="fromAccountId"
                    value={formData.fromAccountId}
                    onChange={handleChange}
                    className={errors.fromAccountId ? 'input--error' : ''}
                >
                    <option value="">Select account...</option>
                    {accounts.map(account => (
                        <option key={account.id} value={account.id}>
                            {account.accountNumber} — {formatCurrency(account.balance)}
                        </option>
                    ))}
                </select>
                {errors.fromAccountId && <span className="field-error">{errors.fromAccountId}</span>}
            </div>

            <div className="form-group">
                <label htmlFor="amount">Amount</label>
                <div className="amount-input">
                    <span className="currency-prefix">$</span>
                    <input
                        id="amount"
                        name="amount"
                        type="number"
                        step="0.01"
                        min="0.01"
                        value={formData.amount}
                        onChange={handleChange}
                        placeholder="0.00"
                        className={errors.amount ? 'input--error' : ''}
                    />
                </div>
                {errors.amount && <span className="field-error">{errors.amount}</span>}
            </div>

            <div className="form-actions">
                <button type="button" onClick={onCancel} className="btn btn-ghost">
                    Cancel
                </button>
                <button type="submit" disabled={submitting} className="btn btn-primary">
                    {submitting ? 'Transferring...' : 'Transfer Funds'}
                </button>
            </div>
        </form>
    );
}

3.4 React Router — Navigation Without Page Reloads

import { BrowserRouter, Routes, Route, Navigate } from 'react-router-dom';

function App() {
    const { isAuthenticated } = useAuth();

    return (
        <BrowserRouter>
            <Routes>
                <Route path="/login" element={
                    isAuthenticated ? <Navigate to="/dashboard" /> : <LoginPage />
                } />

                {/* Protected routes */}
                <Route element={<ProtectedLayout />}>
                    <Route path="/dashboard" element={<Dashboard />} />
                    <Route path="/transfer"  element={<TransferPage />} />
                    <Route path="/accounts/:id" element={<AccountDetailPage />} />
                    <Route path="/statements" element={<StatementsPage />} />
                </Route>

                <Route path="/" element={<Navigate to="/dashboard" />} />
                <Route path="*" element={<NotFoundPage />} />
            </Routes>
        </BrowserRouter>
    );
}

// Protected layout — redirects to login if not authenticated
function ProtectedLayout() {
    const { isAuthenticated } = useAuth();
    const location = useLocation();

    if (!isAuthenticated) {
        return <Navigate to="/login" state={{ from: location }} replace />;
    }

    return (
        <>
            <Navbar />
            <Outlet /> {/* renders the matched child route */}
        </>
    );
}

Chapter Summary

  • React components are functions that accept props and return JSX.
  • useState manages component-local state; useEffect handles side effects like API calls.
  • Controlled components store their value in state, enabling real-time validation.
  • React Router enables client-side navigation without full page reloads.
  • Always handle loading and error states — never assume API calls will succeed.

Interview Prep

  • What is the Virtual DOM? Why does React use it?
  • What is the difference between props and state?
  • Explain the useEffect hook. What does the dependency array control?
  • What is a controlled component? How is it different from an uncontrolled one?
  • Why does React require a unique key prop when rendering lists?

Try This — React State

Build a simple counter component that could represent a session timer:

function SessionTimer() {
    // Add useState here — start at 0
    // Add a button that increments the count
    // Add a button that resets to 0
    // Show "Session time: X minutes" where X is the count
    // If count reaches 30, show a warning: "Consider taking a break!"
}

Then extend it: use useEffect with setInterval to make the counter increment automatically every second. Don’t forget to clear the interval when the component unmounts.

Common Mistakes in Chapter 3

❌ Mutating state directly

account.balance = 2500;        // WRONG — React cannot detect this change
setAccount({...account, balance: 2500}); // CORRECT — creates a new object

React only re-renders when it detects a state change via setState. Direct mutation is invisible to React.

❌ Missing the dependency array in useEffect

useEffect(() => {
    fetchAccounts();
}); // WRONG — runs after EVERY render, causes infinite loop if fetchAccounts sets state

useEffect(() => {
    fetchAccounts();
}, []); // CORRECT — empty array means "run once on mount"

❌ Using index as the key prop in lists

{accounts.map((a, index) => <AccountCard key={index} />)}  // WRONG
{accounts.map(a => <AccountCard key={a.accountId} />)}     // CORRECT

Using the index causes bugs when items are reordered. Always use a stable unique ID.

❌ Calling a hook inside a condition or loop Hooks must always be called in the same order. Never put useState or useEffect inside an if block.

Using AI Effectively — Chapter 3

Ask AI to explain React’s mental model

React can feel strange at first. Ask AI to help you build the right mental model:

“Explain React’s component lifecycle to me using an analogy from real life — birth, life, and death of a component.”
“Why does React re-render? What specifically triggers it, and what does not?”

Use AI as a debugging partner for hooks

Hook-related bugs have specific patterns. When something is not working:

“My useEffect is causing an infinite loop. Here is the code: [paste code]. What is wrong?”

AI almost always catches infinite loop patterns correctly.

Ask AI to explain — not write — your components

“I need to build a component that loads a list of transactions from an API, shows a loading spinner while waiting, and shows an error message if it fails. Explain step by step how to structure this, but don’t write the code yet.”

Get the plan. Write the code. Then ask AI to review it.

Chapter 4Core Java

Core Java and Object-Oriented Programming — The Foundation of Everything

Chapter Objectives

By the end of this chapter you will be able to:

✓ Explain what a class is and how it differs from an object — the blueprint vs the instance

✓ Design and build a class with private fields, constructors, and methods

✓ Apply the four pillars of OOP: Encapsulation, Inheritance, Polymorphism, and Abstraction

✓ Use abstract classes and interfaces and explain when to use each

✓ Explain what the JVM does with your code — stack, heap, garbage collection

✓ Override equals(), hashCode(), and toString() correctly

✓ Build the core domain classes for FinTrack: Account, Customer, Transaction

Introduction

If you skip this chapter, every chapter after it will feel like magic — things will work but you won’t know why. This chapter is the opposite of that. We are going to slow down, zoom in, and build a precise mental model of how Java actually works. What happens in memory when you create an object? Why are some types different from others? What does private really protect? Why does the same method call produce different results depending on which object you call it on?

By the end of this chapter, you will not just know Java syntax — you will understand it. That understanding is what separates someone who copies code from someone who writes it.

4.1 How Java Actually Runs — The JVM

Before writing a single line, you need to know what happens to your code after you write it. This mental model will explain a hundred things that would otherwise seem mysterious.

Compilation and Execution

Most languages compile directly to machine code — binary instructions specific to one type of CPU (Intel x86, ARM, etc.). Java does something different:

Your Java source code (.java)
        |
        | javac (Java Compiler)
        ▼
Java Bytecode (.class)       <-- platform-independent instructions
        |
        | JVM (Java Virtual Machine)
        ▼
Machine code for YOUR CPU    <-- runs on your actual hardware

Bytecode is not machine code — it is instructions for an imaginary machine called the JVM. The JVM then translates bytecode to real machine code at runtime. This is why Java is “write once, run anywhere” — the same .class file runs on Windows, macOS, and Linux, because each platform has its own JVM implementation.

The JVM is also where garbage collection happens — the automatic reclaiming of memory that your program is no longer using. This is one of Java’s biggest advantages over C and C++, where forgetting to free memory causes crashes and security vulnerabilities.

The JIT Compiler

The JVM doesn’t interpret bytecode line by line — that would be slow. It uses a Just-In-Time (JIT) compiler that detects code that runs frequently (“hot code”) and compiles it directly to optimized machine code. This is why Java programs often start a little slowly and then get faster — the JIT is warming up. It also means that benchmarking Java requires a “warm-up” phase.

4.2 Primitive Types vs. Reference Types — The Most Important Distinction in Java

Java has two fundamentally different categories of types. Confusing them causes bugs that are extremely hard to track down.

Primitive Types — Values Stored Directly

Java has eight primitive types. They store their value directly in the variable:

// Primitive types — the variable IS the value
byte   b = 42;          // 8-bit integer: -128 to 127
short  s = 1000;        // 16-bit integer: -32,768 to 32,767
int    i = 1_000_000;   // 32-bit integer: ~-2 billion to ~2 billion
long   l = 10_000_000_000L; // 64-bit integer — note the L suffix
float  f = 3.14f;       // 32-bit floating point — note the f suffix
double d = 3.14159265;  // 64-bit floating point — default for decimals
char   c = 'A';         // 16-bit Unicode character
boolean flag = true;    // true or false, that's it

The underscore in numeric literals (1_000_000) is a readability feature — Java ignores it. Use it freely for large numbers.

Why this matters for FinTrack: Never use float or double for money. Here is why:

double price1 = 0.10;  // wants to be $0.10
double price2 = 0.20;  // wants to be $0.20
System.out.println(price1 + price2); // prints: 0.30000000000000004

// This is not a bug in Java — it's a fundamental limitation of
// binary floating point. 0.1 cannot be represented exactly in binary,
// just as 1/3 cannot be represented exactly in decimal.
// For money, ALWAYS use BigDecimal.

Reference Types — Variables Store an Address

Everything that is not a primitive is a reference type — including all objects, arrays, and Strings. The variable does not store the object itself; it stores the memory address where the object lives.

// Reference type — the variable holds an ADDRESS, not the object
Account account = new Account("CHK-001", new BigDecimal("1000.00"));
//       ^         ^
//       |         |
//    variable   creates the object in the heap
//    holds the  and returns its address
//    address

This has profound consequences:

// Two variables can point to the SAME object
Account account1 = new Account("CHK-001", new BigDecimal("1000.00"));
Account account2 = account1;  // account2 now points to the SAME object

account2.deposit(new BigDecimal("500.00"));
System.out.println(account1.getBalance()); // prints 1500.00 — account1 changed!
// Because account1 and account2 point to the SAME object in memory

This is called aliasing — two names for the same object. It’s the source of many subtle bugs. When you pass an object to a method, you are passing the address, so the method can modify the original object:

// This method DOES modify the account — it receives the address
static void applyMonthlyFee(Account account) {
    account.withdraw(new BigDecimal("25.00")); // modifies the original
}

// This method does NOT affect the caller — primitives are passed by VALUE
static void tryToDoubleIt(int amount) {
    amount = amount * 2; // only changes the local copy
}

Java is always “pass by value” — but for reference types, the value being passed is the address. So the method gets a copy of the address, which still points to the same object.

Stack and Heap — Where Variables Live

The JVM uses two memory regions:

Stack: Each method call gets a stack frame — a block of memory holding that method’s local variables and the return address. Stack frames are created on method entry and destroyed on method exit. Stack access is very fast because of how CPU caches work.

Heap: Objects live on the heap. The heap is shared across all threads and grows dynamically. When you write new Account(...), the JVM allocates memory on the heap for the Account object.

void processTransfer() {
    // Stack frame for processTransfer()
    // ┌──────────────────────────────────────┐
    // │ amount    = 500.00  (primitive)       │ ← stored directly on stack
    // │ accountRef → [0x4A2F7B]  (reference) │ ← address stored on stack
    // └──────────────────────────────────────┘
    //                          |
    //                          ▼
    // Heap:  [0x4A2F7B] Account object { balance: 1000.00, ... }

    BigDecimal amount = new BigDecimal("500.00");
    Account account = findAccount("CHK-001"); // account reference on stack
    account.withdraw(amount); // object modified on heap
} // stack frame destroyed; Account object stays on heap until GC collects it

Null is the absence of a reference — a variable that holds no address:

Account account = null; // points to nothing
account.getBalance();   // NullPointerException — there's no object at "no address"

NullPointerException is Java’s most common runtime error. It means you tried to use a reference that pointed to nothing.

4.3 Strings — Not as Simple as They Look

String is a reference type, but Java gives it special treatment that surprises many beginners.

String Immutability

Strings in Java are immutable — once created, they cannot be changed. What looks like modification is actually creating a new String:

String name = "Alice";
name.toUpperCase(); // creates a NEW string "ALICE" — name is unchanged!
System.out.println(name); // still prints "Alice"

// Correct: capture the result
String upperName = name.toUpperCase();
System.out.println(upperName); // "ALICE"

// This is also why string concatenation in a loop is slow:
String result = "";
for (int i = 0; i < 10000; i++) {
    result = result + "x"; // creates 10,000 intermediate String objects!
}

// Use StringBuilder instead — it is mutable
StringBuilder sb = new StringBuilder();
for (int i = 0; i < 10000; i++) {
    sb.append("x"); // modifies the same buffer
}
String result2 = sb.toString(); // ONE string at the end

String Comparison — == vs .equals()

This is one of the most common Java bugs:

String s1 = "CHK-001";
String s2 = "CHK-001";
String s3 = new String("CHK-001");

System.out.println(s1 == s2);       // true  — same object (String pool)
System.out.println(s1 == s3);       // false — different objects in memory!
System.out.println(s1.equals(s3));  // true  — compares the characters

// RULE: Always use .equals() to compare String content
// Use == only to check if two variables point to the EXACT same object

Java maintains a String pool — a cache of string literals. "CHK-001" always returns the same object from the pool, which is why s1 == s2 is true. But new String("CHK-001") explicitly creates a new object outside the pool.

Essential String Methods

String accountNumber = "  CHK-0001  ";

accountNumber.trim()           // "CHK-0001" — remove whitespace from ends
accountNumber.strip()          // "CHK-0001" — Unicode-aware (prefer this)
accountNumber.length()         // 12
accountNumber.toUpperCase()    // "  CHK-0001  "
accountNumber.toLowerCase()    // "  chk-0001  "
accountNumber.startsWith("CHK") // false (has leading spaces)
accountNumber.strip().startsWith("CHK") // true
accountNumber.contains("0001") // true
accountNumber.replace("CHK", "SAV") // "  SAV-0001  "
accountNumber.split("-")       // ["  CHK", "0001  "] — array of parts

// Check for empty/blank
String empty = "";
empty.isEmpty()  // true (length == 0)
"   ".isEmpty()  // false (has spaces)
"   ".isBlank()  // true (all whitespace — Java 11+)

// Format a string
String formatted = String.format("Account %s: $%,.2f", "CHK-001", 2500.00);
// "Account CHK-001: $2,500.00"

// Or use text blocks (Java 15+) for multi-line strings
String json = """
    {
        "accountNumber": "CHK-001",
        "balance": 2500.00
    }
    """;

4.4 Classes and Objects — The Blueprint and the Building

A class is the definition — the blueprint. An object is a specific instance created from that blueprint. You define a class once; you can create millions of objects from it.

Anatomy of a Class

Let’s build the Account class step by step, understanding every piece:

public class Account {

    // ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
    // FIELDS — the data each Account object holds
    // ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

    // private = only code inside THIS class can access these directly
    // Each Account object has its OWN copy of these fields
    private Long accountId;                     // ← [private = only code INSIDE this class can read or change this. Nothing outside can touch it directly.]
    private String accountNumber;               // ← [String holds text. The account number like "CHK-0001".]
    private BigDecimal balance;                 // ← [BigDecimal for money — gives exact decimal arithmetic. Never use double for currency.]
    private boolean active;                     // ← [true = account is open. false = account is closed. Only two possible values.]
    private LocalDateTime createdAt;            // ← [Records the exact date and time the account was created. LocalDateTime = date + time without timezone.]

    private static int totalAccountsCreated = 0; // ← [static = ONE shared copy for ALL Account objects. Not per-object — tracks the global count. int = whole number.]

    // ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
    // CONSTRUCTORS — called when creating a new object with 'new'
    // ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

    // Primary constructor — the main way to create an Account
    public Account(String accountNumber, BigDecimal openingBalance) {  // ← [Constructor: runs automatically when "new Account(...)" is called. Sets up the object's initial state.]
        this.accountNumber = accountNumber;  // ← [this.accountNumber = the FIELD on this object. accountNumber (no this.) = the parameter passed in. Assigns parameter to field.]
        this.balance = openingBalance;       // ← [Same pattern: sets the object's balance field to whatever was passed as openingBalance.]
        this.active = true;                  // ← [New accounts always start as active. Hard-coded to true — no parameter needed.]
        this.createdAt = LocalDateTime.now(); // ← [.now() gets the current date and time at the moment of creation.]
        totalAccountsCreated++;              // ← [++ adds 1 to the count. Because totalAccountsCreated is static, this updates the ONE shared counter for all accounts.]

        if (accountNumber == null || accountNumber.isBlank()) {  // ← [null = no value at all. isBlank() = only whitespace. Either is invalid for an account number.]
            throw new IllegalArgumentException("Account number cannot be blank"); // ← [throw stops execution and reports an error. IllegalArgumentException = the caller passed invalid input.]
        }
        if (openingBalance == null || openingBalance.compareTo(BigDecimal.ZERO) < 0) { // ← [compareTo returns negative if openingBalance < 0. We reject negative opening balances.]
            throw new IllegalArgumentException("Opening balance cannot be negative"); // ← [Fail fast: catch invalid data immediately in the constructor, not later when it causes stranger bugs.]
        }
    }

    // Overloaded constructor — creates account with zero balance
    // Constructor overloading: multiple constructors with different parameters
    public Account(String accountNumber) {
        this(accountNumber, BigDecimal.ZERO); // calls the primary constructor
        // 'this(...)' must be the FIRST statement in the constructor body
    }

    // ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
    // INSTANCE METHODS — the behaviors each Account object can perform
    // ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

    public void deposit(BigDecimal amount) {    // ← [public = any code can call this. void = returns nothing. amount = the money to add.]
        if (amount == null) {                    // ← [Always check for null first. Calling .compareTo() on null would throw a NullPointerException.]
            throw new IllegalArgumentException("Amount cannot be null");
        }
        if (amount.compareTo(BigDecimal.ZERO) <= 0) {  // ← [compareTo(ZERO) <= 0 means amount is zero or negative. Both are invalid for a deposit.]
            throw new IllegalArgumentException("Deposit amount must be positive, got: " + amount);
        }
        if (!active) {                           // ← [! means NOT. !active = "if account is NOT active". Can't deposit to a closed account.]
            throw new IllegalStateException("Cannot deposit to inactive account: " + accountNumber); // ← [IllegalStateException = the object is in the wrong state for this operation.]
        }
        this.balance = this.balance.add(amount); // ← [BigDecimal.add() returns a NEW BigDecimal — BigDecimal is immutable. We assign the NEW value back to this.balance.]
    }

    public void withdraw(BigDecimal amount) {
        if (amount == null || amount.compareTo(BigDecimal.ZERO) <= 0) {
            throw new IllegalArgumentException("Withdrawal amount must be positive");
        }
        if (!active) {
            throw new IllegalStateException("Cannot withdraw from inactive account");
        }
        if (this.balance.compareTo(amount) < 0) {
            throw new InsufficientFundsException(
                "Cannot withdraw " + amount + " from account with balance " + this.balance);
        }
        this.balance = this.balance.subtract(amount);
    }

    public void close() {
        if (!active) {
            throw new IllegalStateException("Account is already closed");
        }
        if (this.balance.compareTo(BigDecimal.ZERO) != 0) {
            throw new IllegalStateException(
                "Cannot close account with non-zero balance: " + this.balance);
        }
        this.active = false;
    }

    // ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
    // STATIC METHODS — belong to the class, not any instance
    // ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

    public static int getTotalAccountsCreated() {
        return totalAccountsCreated;
    }

    // Factory method pattern — a named way to create objects
    // More readable than constructors when there are multiple creation scenarios
    public static Account openChecking(String accountNumber, BigDecimal deposit) {
        Account account = new Account(accountNumber, deposit);
        // could do checking-specific setup here
        return account;
    }

    // ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
    // GETTERS — controlled read access to private fields
    // ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

    public Long getAccountId()         { return accountId; }
    public String getAccountNumber()   { return accountNumber; }
    public BigDecimal getBalance()     { return balance; }
    public boolean isActive()          { return active; }
    public LocalDateTime getCreatedAt() { return createdAt; }

    // ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
    // STANDARD OBJECT METHODS
    // ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

    @Override
    public String toString() {
        // Called automatically when you do System.out.println(account)
        return String.format("Account[%s, balance=$%,.2f, active=%b]",
            accountNumber, balance, active);
    }

    @Override
    public boolean equals(Object other) {
        // Two accounts are equal if they have the same account number
        if (this == other) return true;           // same object reference
        if (!(other instanceof Account)) return false; // different type
        Account that = (Account) other;
        return this.accountNumber.equals(that.accountNumber);
    }

    @Override
    public int hashCode() {
        // MUST override hashCode whenever you override equals
        // If a.equals(b) then a.hashCode() MUST equal b.hashCode()
        return accountNumber.hashCode();
    }
}

The this Keyword

this is a reference to the current object — the object on which the method was called. It has three uses:

// 1. Distinguish field from parameter with same name
public Account(String accountNumber, BigDecimal balance) {
    this.accountNumber = accountNumber; // this.accountNumber = field, accountNumber = parameter
    this.balance = balance;
}

// 2. Call another constructor from a constructor
public Account(String accountNumber) {
    this(accountNumber, BigDecimal.ZERO); // calls the two-parameter constructor
}

// 3. Pass the current object as an argument
public void registerWith(AccountRegistry registry) {
    registry.register(this); // pass this object to another method
}

4.5 Encapsulation — Protecting Your Data

Encapsulation is the practice of bundling data (fields) with the methods that operate on it, and restricting direct external access to that data. It is enforced by access modifiers.

Access Modifiers

ModifierSame ClassSame PackageSubclassEverywhere
private✅❌❌❌
(none — package-private)✅✅❌❌
protected✅✅✅❌
public✅✅✅✅

The principle: make everything as private as possible. Only expose what external code genuinely needs.

Why Encapsulation Matters — A Concrete Example

// BAD — public fields, no encapsulation
public class BadAccount {
    public BigDecimal balance; // anyone can set this to anything!
}

BadAccount account = new BadAccount();
account.balance = new BigDecimal("-999999999"); // corruption — no protection
account.balance = null;                          // crash waiting to happen

// GOOD — private field, controlled access
public class GoodAccount {
    private BigDecimal balance = BigDecimal.ZERO;

    public void deposit(BigDecimal amount) {
        // Business rules enforced HERE — the only way in
        if (amount == null || amount.compareTo(BigDecimal.ZERO) <= 0) {
            throw new IllegalArgumentException("Amount must be positive");
        }
        this.balance = this.balance.add(amount);
    }

    public BigDecimal getBalance() {
        return balance; // read-only access — can't modify through the getter
    }
    // No setter for balance — can only change via deposit()/withdraw()
}

Encapsulation means the object is always in a valid state. The balance can only be modified through deposit() and withdraw(), which enforce the rules. Nothing external can corrupt the data.

The final Keyword

final prevents reassignment of variables, overriding of methods, and subclassing of classes:

// Final variable — cannot be reassigned after initialization
final String accountNumber = "CHK-001";
accountNumber = "CHK-002"; // COMPILE ERROR

// Final field — must be set in the constructor, then never changes
public class Account {
    private final String accountNumber; // set once in constructor, immutable thereafter

    public Account(String accountNumber) {
        this.accountNumber = accountNumber;
    }
}

// Final method — cannot be overridden in a subclass
public final void close() { ... }

// Final class — cannot be subclassed at all
// String is final — you cannot extend String
public final class Money { ... }

Use final on fields that should never change after construction — it makes your classes easier to reason about and is thread-safe by default.

4.6 Inheritance — Building on What Already Exists

Inheritance lets one class (the subclass or child) inherit fields and methods from another (the superclass or parent), while adding or modifying behavior.

The extends Keyword

// Parent class — shared behavior for all account types
public abstract class Account {
    protected String accountNumber;   // protected = accessible in subclasses
    protected BigDecimal balance;
    protected Customer owner;
    protected boolean active;

    // Constructor — subclasses MUST call this via super()
    protected Account(String accountNumber, BigDecimal initialBalance, Customer owner) {
        if (accountNumber == null || accountNumber.isBlank()) {
            throw new IllegalArgumentException("Account number required");
        }
        if (initialBalance == null || initialBalance.compareTo(BigDecimal.ZERO) < 0) {
            throw new IllegalArgumentException("Initial balance cannot be negative");
        }
        this.accountNumber = accountNumber;
        this.balance = initialBalance;
        this.owner = owner;
        this.active = true;
    }

    // Concrete method — same for all account types, inherited as-is
    public void deposit(BigDecimal amount) {
        validateAmount(amount, "Deposit");
        validateActive();
        this.balance = this.balance.add(amount);
    }

    // Abstract method — MUST be implemented by every subclass
    // 'abstract' means: I promise subclasses will provide this, but I can't implement it here
    public abstract void withdraw(BigDecimal amount);

    // Protected helper — available to this class and all subclasses
    protected void validateAmount(BigDecimal amount, String operation) {
        if (amount == null || amount.compareTo(BigDecimal.ZERO) <= 0) {
            throw new IllegalArgumentException(
                operation + " amount must be positive, got: " + amount);
        }
    }

    protected void validateActive() {
        if (!active) {
            throw new IllegalStateException(
                "Account " + accountNumber + " is not active");
        }
    }

    // Getters
    public String getAccountNumber() { return accountNumber; }
    public BigDecimal getBalance()   { return balance; }
    public Customer getOwner()       { return owner; }
    public boolean isActive()        { return active; }
}

Checking Account — Inherits and Implements

// CheckingAccount IS-A Account
// It gets everything from Account and provides its own withdraw()
public class CheckingAccount extends Account {

    // Checking accounts can optionally have overdraft protection
    private boolean overdraftProtectionEnabled;
    private BigDecimal overdraftLimit;

    // Constructor — MUST call super() first to initialize the parent's fields
    public CheckingAccount(String accountNumber, BigDecimal initialBalance, Customer owner) {
        super(accountNumber, initialBalance, owner); // must be the FIRST statement
        this.overdraftProtectionEnabled = false;
        this.overdraftLimit = BigDecimal.ZERO;
    }

    // Secondary constructor for accounts with overdraft
    public CheckingAccount(String accountNumber, BigDecimal initialBalance,
                           Customer owner, BigDecimal overdraftLimit) {
        super(accountNumber, initialBalance, owner);
        this.overdraftProtectionEnabled = true;
        this.overdraftLimit = overdraftLimit;
    }

    // @Override tells Java "this method replaces the parent's version"
    // The compiler will error if you @Override a method that doesn't exist in the parent
    // — this is a safety net against typos
    @Override
    public void withdraw(BigDecimal amount) {
        validateAmount(amount, "Withdrawal");  // inherited from Account
        validateActive();                      // inherited from Account

        BigDecimal effectiveLimit = overdraftProtectionEnabled
            ? overdraftLimit
            : BigDecimal.ZERO;

        BigDecimal availableFunds = this.balance.add(effectiveLimit);

        if (availableFunds.compareTo(amount) < 0) {
            throw new InsufficientFundsException(
                "Cannot withdraw $" + amount + ". Available: $" + availableFunds);
        }

        this.balance = this.balance.subtract(amount);
    }

    // New method — only exists on CheckingAccount, not on Account
    public void enableOverdraftProtection(BigDecimal limit) {
        if (limit == null || limit.compareTo(BigDecimal.ZERO) <= 0) {
            throw new IllegalArgumentException("Overdraft limit must be positive");
        }
        this.overdraftProtectionEnabled = true;
        this.overdraftLimit = limit;
    }

    @Override
    public String toString() {
        return String.format("CheckingAccount[%s, $%,.2f%s]",
            accountNumber, balance,
            overdraftProtectionEnabled ? ", overdraft=$" + overdraftLimit : "");
    }
}

Savings Account — Its Own Withdrawal Rules

public class SavingsAccount extends Account {

    private static final BigDecimal DEFAULT_DAILY_LIMIT = new BigDecimal("2000.00");
    private static final int DEFAULT_MONTHLY_TRANSACTIONS = 6; // Regulation D limit

    private BigDecimal dailyWithdrawalLimit;
    private int monthlyTransactionsAllowed;

    // Track current usage
    private BigDecimal withdrawnToday;
    private int transactionsThisMonth;
    private LocalDate lastWithdrawalDate;
    private YearMonth currentMonth;

    public SavingsAccount(String accountNumber, BigDecimal initialBalance, Customer owner) {
        super(accountNumber, initialBalance, owner);
        this.dailyWithdrawalLimit = DEFAULT_DAILY_LIMIT;
        this.monthlyTransactionsAllowed = DEFAULT_MONTHLY_TRANSACTIONS;
        this.withdrawnToday = BigDecimal.ZERO;
        this.transactionsThisMonth = 0;
        this.lastWithdrawalDate = LocalDate.now();
        this.currentMonth = YearMonth.now();
    }

    @Override
    public void withdraw(BigDecimal amount) {
        validateAmount(amount, "Withdrawal");
        validateActive();

        // Reset daily tracker on a new day
        if (!LocalDate.now().equals(lastWithdrawalDate)) {
            withdrawnToday = BigDecimal.ZERO;
            lastWithdrawalDate = LocalDate.now();
        }

        // Reset monthly tracker on a new month
        if (!YearMonth.now().equals(currentMonth)) {
            transactionsThisMonth = 0;
            currentMonth = YearMonth.now();
        }

        // Check monthly transaction limit
        if (transactionsThisMonth >= monthlyTransactionsAllowed) {
            throw new TransactionLimitExceededException(
                "Monthly withdrawal limit of " + monthlyTransactionsAllowed +
                " transactions exceeded for savings account " + accountNumber);
        }

        // Check daily withdrawal limit
        BigDecimal newDailyTotal = withdrawnToday.add(amount);
        if (newDailyTotal.compareTo(dailyWithdrawalLimit) > 0) {
            BigDecimal remaining = dailyWithdrawalLimit.subtract(withdrawnToday);
            throw new DailyLimitExceededException(
                "Daily withdrawal limit of $" + dailyWithdrawalLimit +
                " exceeded. Remaining today: $" + remaining);
        }

        // Check sufficient balance
        if (this.balance.compareTo(amount) < 0) {
            throw new InsufficientFundsException(
                "Insufficient funds. Balance: $" + balance + ", Requested: $" + amount);
        }

        // All checks passed — execute the withdrawal
        this.balance = this.balance.subtract(amount);
        this.withdrawnToday = newDailyTotal;
        this.transactionsThisMonth++;
    }

    @Override
    public String toString() {
        return String.format("SavingsAccount[%s, $%,.2f, %d/%d monthly transactions]",
            accountNumber, balance, transactionsThisMonth, monthlyTransactionsAllowed);
    }
}

What Is and Is Not Inherited

FeatureInherited?
public instance methods✅ Yes
protected instance methods✅ Yes
public and protected fields✅ Yes
private fields and methods❌ No — but they exist in the object!
Constructors❌ No — but super() calls them
static methods❌ Not in the OOP sense — they are hidden, not overridden

Private fields exist in the object in memory but cannot be accessed directly from subclass code. This is intentional — the subclass uses the parent’s public/protected methods to interact with private data.

4.7 Polymorphism — One Name, Many Forms

Polymorphism (Greek for “many shapes”) is the ability of a parent type reference to refer to different subtype objects, with behavior determined at runtime.

Runtime Polymorphism — Dynamic Dispatch

// All these can be stored as Account — the parent type
List<Account> accounts = new ArrayList<>();
accounts.add(new CheckingAccount("CHK-001", new BigDecimal("1000"), customer));
accounts.add(new SavingsAccount("SAV-001",  new BigDecimal("5000"), customer));
accounts.add(new CheckingAccount("CHK-002", new BigDecimal("750"),  customer));

// Process all accounts the same way — polymorphic call
for (Account account : accounts) {
    try {
        account.withdraw(new BigDecimal("100")); // Which withdraw() runs?
        // For CheckingAccount objects: CheckingAccount.withdraw()
        // For SavingsAccount objects: SavingsAccount.withdraw()
        // The JVM decides at RUNTIME based on the actual object type
    } catch (InsufficientFundsException | DailyLimitExceededException e) {
        System.out.println("Cannot withdraw from " + account.getAccountNumber()
            + ": " + e.getMessage());
    }
}

This is called dynamic dispatch — the method to call is determined at runtime, not at compile time. Java uses a virtual method table (vtable) for this: every class has a table mapping method names to implementations, and the JVM looks up the right implementation based on the actual object type.

Compile-time Polymorphism — Method Overloading

Overloading means having multiple methods with the same name but different parameter lists. Java resolves which one to call at compile time, based on the argument types:

public class AccountService {

    // Method overloading — same name, different signatures
    public TransactionResult deposit(Long accountId, BigDecimal amount) {
        // Basic deposit
        return performDeposit(accountId, amount, "Manual deposit", null);
    }

    public TransactionResult deposit(Long accountId, BigDecimal amount, String description) {
        // Deposit with custom description
        return performDeposit(accountId, amount, description, null);
    }

    public TransactionResult deposit(Long accountId, BigDecimal amount,
                                      String description, String referenceNumber) {
        // Deposit with full details
        return performDeposit(accountId, amount, description, referenceNumber);
    }

    // The actual implementation
    private TransactionResult performDeposit(Long accountId, BigDecimal amount,
                                              String description, String refNum) {
        // ... implementation
    }
}

// The compiler figures out which overload to call:
service.deposit(1L, new BigDecimal("500"));       // calls first
service.deposit(1L, new BigDecimal("500"), "Payroll"); // calls second

Overloading vs. Overriding — the critical difference:

OverloadingOverriding
WhereSame classSubclass
Parameter listMust be differentMust be identical
Return typeCan differMust be same (or covariant)
ResolvedCompile timeRuntime
@Override annotationNot applicableShould always use

The instanceof Operator

Sometimes you need to know the actual type of an object at runtime:

Account account = getAccountFromSomewhere();

// Old style
if (account instanceof SavingsAccount) {
    SavingsAccount savings = (SavingsAccount) account; // cast
    int remaining = savings.getRemainingMonthlyTransactions();
}
