// Modern style — pattern matching (Java 16+)
if (account instanceof SavingsAccount savings) {
    // 'savings' is already the SavingsAccount type — no explicit cast needed
    int remaining = savings.getRemainingMonthlyTransactions();
}

// Switch expression with pattern matching (Java 21)
String accountInfo = switch (account) {
    case CheckingAccount c when c.isOverdraftEnabled() ->
        "Checking with overdraft limit: $" + c.getOverdraftLimit();
    case CheckingAccount c ->
        "Standard checking: $" + c.getBalance();
    case SavingsAccount s ->
        "Savings (monthly: " + s.getRemainingMonthlyTransactions() + " remaining)";
    default ->
        "Unknown account type";
};

4.8 Abstract Classes and Interfaces — Two Flavors of Contract

Both define requirements for subclasses, but they serve different purposes.

Abstract Classes — Partial Implementation

An abstract class cannot be instantiated directly — it exists to be subclassed. It can have: - Concrete methods (implemented) - Abstract methods (declared but not implemented — subclasses must implement) - Fields - Constructors

public abstract class Account {

    protected BigDecimal balance;
    protected String accountNumber;

    // Concrete — every account deposits the same way
    public void deposit(BigDecimal amount) {
        validatePositive(amount);
        this.balance = this.balance.add(amount);
    }

    // Abstract — each account type has its own withdrawal rules
    public abstract void withdraw(BigDecimal amount);

    // Abstract — each account type generates a different monthly statement
    public abstract String generateMonthlyStatement();

    // Template method pattern — defines the algorithm structure,
    // with abstract "hooks" that subclasses fill in
    public final AccountStatement closeAccount() {
        validateCanBeClosed();         // concrete validation
        BigDecimal finalBalance = performCloseOperations(); // abstract
        generateCloseAuditLog();       // concrete
        this.active = false;
        return new AccountStatement(finalBalance);
    }

    protected abstract BigDecimal performCloseOperations();

    protected void validateCanBeClosed() {
        if (!active) throw new IllegalStateException("Already closed");
    }
}

// You CANNOT do this:
Account account = new Account("CHK-001", ...); // COMPILE ERROR — abstract!

// You MUST use a concrete subclass:
Account account = new CheckingAccount("CHK-001", ...); // OK

Interfaces — Pure Contract

An interface defines a contract — what methods an implementing class must provide — with NO implementation (with minor exceptions). A class can implement multiple interfaces, making them ideal for defining cross-cutting capabilities.

// What it means to have a balance that earns interest
public interface InterestBearing {
    BigDecimal getAnnualInterestRate();
    BigDecimal calculateInterest(int forMonths);
    void applyInterest();
}

// What it means to support automatic bill payments
public interface AutoPayCapable {
    void schedulePay(String payee, BigDecimal amount, LocalDate payDate);
    List<ScheduledPayment> getScheduledPayments();
    void cancelScheduledPayment(Long paymentId);
}

// What it means to have a spending history
public interface Analyzable {
    Map<String, BigDecimal> getSpendingByCategory(YearMonth month);
    BigDecimal getAverageMonthlySpending();
    List<Transaction> getUnusualTransactions();
}

// A CheckingAccount implements ONE interface
public class CheckingAccount extends Account implements AutoPayCapable {
    @Override
    public void schedulePay(String payee, BigDecimal amount, LocalDate payDate) {
        // implementation
    }
    // ... other AutoPay methods
}

// A SavingsAccount implements TWO interfaces — this is fine
// A class can implement as many interfaces as needed
public class SavingsAccount extends Account implements InterestBearing, Analyzable {
    @Override
    public BigDecimal getAnnualInterestRate() { return new BigDecimal("0.045"); }

    @Override
    public BigDecimal calculateInterest(int forMonths) {
        return balance.multiply(getAnnualInterestRate())
                      .multiply(new BigDecimal(forMonths))
                      .divide(new BigDecimal("12"), 2, RoundingMode.HALF_UP);
    }

    @Override
    public void applyInterest() {
        BigDecimal interest = calculateInterest(1);
        this.balance = this.balance.add(interest);
    }

    // ... Analyzable methods
}

// Using interfaces as types — very powerful
// This code works for ANY InterestBearing account
public void applyMonthlyInterestToAll(List<InterestBearing> accounts) {
    for (InterestBearing account : accounts) {
        account.applyInterest();
    }
}

Default and Static Methods in Interfaces (Java 8+)

Interfaces can now have implementation, carefully used:

public interface Auditable {
    String getEntityId();
    String getEntityType();

    // Default method — provides a default implementation
    // Implementing classes can override this if needed
    default String toAuditString() {
        return LocalDateTime.now() + " | " + getEntityType() + " | " + getEntityId();
    }

    // Static method — belongs to the interface, not instances
    static String formatAuditEntry(String entityId, String eventType, String details) {
        return String.format("[%s] %s on %s: %s",
            LocalDateTime.now().format(DateTimeFormatter.ISO_LOCAL_DATE_TIME),
            eventType, entityId, details);
    }
}

Abstract Class vs. Interface — When to Use Which

SituationUse
Sharing code between closely related classesAbstract class
Defining a capability that unrelated classes shareInterface
Multiple inheritance of behaviorInterface (classes can only extend one class)
Maintaining state (fields)Abstract class
“IS-A” relationshipAbstract class
“CAN-DO” relationshipInterface

Concrete guidance for FinTrack: - Account → abstract class (all accounts share fields and some methods) - InterestBearing, AutoPayCapable, Auditable → interfaces (capabilities multiple types share)

4.9 The static Keyword — Belonging to the Class, Not Objects

static members belong to the class itself, not to any particular instance. There is only ONE copy of a static field, no matter how many objects exist.

public class Account {

    // Static field — ONE counter shared by all Account objects
    private static long nextAccountId = 1000L;

    // Instance field — each Account has its OWN id
    private final long accountId;

    public Account(String accountNumber, BigDecimal balance) {
        // Assign and increment the shared counter
        this.accountId = nextAccountId++;
        // ...
    }

    // Static utility method — doesn't need 'this', no instance required
    public static String generateAccountNumber(String prefix, long id) {
        return String.format("%s-%06d", prefix, id); // CHK-000001
    }

    // Static constant — shared value that never changes
    public static final BigDecimal MINIMUM_OPENING_BALANCE = new BigDecimal("25.00");
}

// Calling static members: use the CLASS name, not an instance
String number = Account.generateAccountNumber("CHK", 42); // OK
BigDecimal min = Account.MINIMUM_OPENING_BALANCE;         // OK

// You CAN call via an instance, but it's misleading — avoid
Account a = new Account(...);
String number2 = a.generateAccountNumber("CHK", 42); // Works but confusing!

When to make something static: - It does not depend on any instance’s state - It is a constant that never changes - It is a factory or utility method - It is a counter or registry shared across all instances

4.10 Enums — Fixed Sets of Constants

An enum (enumeration) defines a type that can only take one of a fixed set of values. In FinTrack, account types and transaction types are perfect enums — there are a known set of valid values and nothing else.

// Basic enum
public enum AccountType {
    CHECKING, SAVINGS
}

// Rich enum — each value can have its own fields and methods
public enum TransactionType {

    DEPOSIT("Deposit", true, false),
    WITHDRAWAL("Withdrawal", false, true),
    TRANSFER_OUT("Transfer Out", false, true),
    TRANSFER_IN("Transfer In", true, false),
    FEE("Service Fee", false, true),
    INTEREST("Interest Credit", true, false);

    private final String displayName;
    private final boolean isCredit;  // adds money to the account
    private final boolean isDebit;   // removes money from the account

    // Enum constructor — called once per enum constant
    TransactionType(String displayName, boolean isCredit, boolean isDebit) {
        this.displayName = displayName;
        this.isCredit = isCredit;
        this.isDebit = isDebit;
    }

    // Enum methods
    public String getDisplayName() { return displayName; }
    public boolean isCredit()      { return isCredit; }
    public boolean isDebit()       { return isDebit; }

    // Method that varies per constant
    public BigDecimal applyToBalance(BigDecimal balance, BigDecimal amount) {
        return isCredit ? balance.add(amount) : balance.subtract(amount);
    }
}

// Using enums
TransactionType type = TransactionType.DEPOSIT;
System.out.println(type.getDisplayName()); // "Deposit"
System.out.println(type.isCredit());       // true

// Enums work great in switch
String description = switch (type) {
    case DEPOSIT, TRANSFER_IN, INTEREST -> "Money received";
    case WITHDRAWAL, TRANSFER_OUT, FEE  -> "Money sent";
};

// Iterate all values
for (TransactionType t : TransactionType.values()) {
    System.out.println(t.name() + ": " + t.getDisplayName());
}

// Convert from String (throws IllegalArgumentException if not found)
TransactionType fromString = TransactionType.valueOf("DEPOSIT");

// Compare enums — use == (safe because only one instance per constant)
if (type == TransactionType.DEPOSIT) { ... }

4.11 Records — Immutable Data Carriers (Java 16+)

Records are a concise way to create classes that purely hold data — no mutable state, just values. They automatically get a constructor, getters, equals(), hashCode(), and toString().

// Traditional class for holding a money amount and currency
public class MoneyAmount {
    private final BigDecimal amount;
    private final String currency;

    public MoneyAmount(BigDecimal amount, String currency) {
        this.amount = amount;
        this.currency = currency;
    }
    public BigDecimal getAmount() { return amount; }
    public String getCurrency() { return currency; }
    @Override public boolean equals(Object o) { ... }
    @Override public int hashCode() { ... }
    @Override public String toString() { ... }
}

// Record — does ALL of the above in one line
public record MoneyAmount(BigDecimal amount, String currency) {}

// Usage is identical
MoneyAmount fee = new MoneyAmount(new BigDecimal("2.50"), "USD");
System.out.println(fee.amount());   // BigDecimal(2.50) — note: no "get" prefix
System.out.println(fee.currency()); // "USD"
System.out.println(fee);            // MoneyAmount[amount=2.50, currency=USD]

// Records can have custom methods
public record TransferDetails(
        Long fromAccountId,
        Long toAccountId,
        BigDecimal amount,
        String description
) {
    // Compact canonical constructor — runs before the auto-generated one
    public TransferDetails {
        if (fromAccountId.equals(toAccountId)) {
            throw new IllegalArgumentException("Cannot transfer to the same account");
        }
        if (amount.compareTo(BigDecimal.ZERO) <= 0) {
            throw new IllegalArgumentException("Transfer amount must be positive");
        }
        // description can be normalized
        description = description == null ? "Transfer" : description.strip();
    }

    // Custom method
    public boolean isLargeTransfer() {
        return amount.compareTo(new BigDecimal("10000")) >= 0;
    }
}

Chapter Summary

This chapter covered the essential Java concepts that everything else builds on:

  • The JVM compiles source to bytecode, then JIT-compiles hot code to machine instructions at runtime.
  • Primitive types store values directly; reference types store memory addresses. This distinction explains aliasing, pass-by-value behavior, and NullPointerException.
  • Strings are immutable — apparent modifications create new objects. Always use .equals() for string comparison, never ==.
  • Encapsulation (private fields + public methods) protects object state and enforces business rules. The balance can only change through deposit() and withdraw().
  • Inheritance (extends) lets subclasses reuse and extend parent behavior. Constructors are not inherited but are called via super().
  • Polymorphism (dynamic dispatch) means the JVM calls the correct subclass method at runtime, enabling you to write code against parent types that works correctly for any subclass.
  • Abstract classes provide partial implementation; interfaces provide pure contracts. A class can extend one parent but implement many interfaces.
  • static members belong to the class itself; final prevents reassignment or overriding.
  • Enums model fixed sets of values cleanly, with optional fields and methods per constant.
  • Records provide concise immutable data carriers with auto-generated constructors, accessors, and equality.

Interview Prep

  • Explain the difference between a primitive type and a reference type. What happens in memory when you do Account a = new Account(...)?
  • Why does 0.1 + 0.2 != 0.3 in Java? What is the correct type to use for monetary calculations?
  • What is the difference between method overloading and method overriding? Which is resolved at compile time and which at runtime?
  • A class extends an abstract class. It also implements two interfaces. Can it do both? What are the requirements?
  • What is the difference between final, finally, and finalize?
  • Explain NullPointerException. Write code that would cause one, then fix it.
  • What does @Override do? What happens if you omit it when intending to override a method?
  • Why should you override hashCode() whenever you override equals()? What breaks if you don’t?

Quick Knowledge Check

  • True or False: String s1 = "hello"; String s2 = "hello"; s1 == s2 is always true in Java. (False — it is true for string literals due to the pool, but not guaranteed for all Strings.)
  • Fill in the blank: A method declared with the abstract keyword must be overridden in any non-abstract ___. (subclass)
  • What keyword prevents a method from being overridden by a subclass? (final)
  • True or False: A Java class can extend multiple abstract classes. (False — a class can extend only one class, abstract or not.)
  • What is printed? Account a = new SavingsAccount(...); System.out.println(a instanceof Account); (true — SavingsAccount IS-A Account)

Try This — Your First Class

Without looking at the chapter examples, build a BankCard class from scratch:

Requirements: - Fields: cardNumber (String), cardHolderName (String), expiryMonth (int), expiryYear (int), isBlocked (boolean) - Constructor that takes all fields except isBlocked (which always starts as false) - Method block() that sets isBlocked to true - Method isExpired(int currentYear, int currentMonth) that returns true if the card has expired - Override toString() to show a masked card number: “XXXX-XXXX-XXXX-1234” (only last 4 digits visible)

Test it in a main method with at least three different BankCard objects.

Common Mistakes in Chapter 4

❌ Forgetting this when parameter names match field names

public Account(String accountNumber) {
    accountNumber = accountNumber; // WRONG — assigns parameter to itself!
    this.accountNumber = accountNumber; // CORRECT
}

❌ Not overriding both equals() and hashCode() If you override equals(), you must also override hashCode(). If two objects are equal by equals(), they must have the same hashCode(). Breaking this contract causes bugs in HashMap and HashSet.

❌ Using == to compare objects

Account a1 = new Account("CHK-001", ...);
Account a2 = new Account("CHK-001", ...);
a1 == a2           // FALSE — different objects in memory
a1.equals(a2)      // TRUE — if you've overridden equals() correctly

❌ Making fields public instead of private Public fields allow any code anywhere to change your object’s state — breaking encapsulation. Always make fields private and provide methods to access them.

❌ Calling overridable methods from a constructor If a subclass overrides a method that the parent constructor calls, the subclass version runs before the subclass is fully initialised. This causes subtle, hard-to-find bugs.

Using AI Effectively — Chapter 4

Ask AI to critique your class design

Paste a class you have written and ask:

“Review this Java class for OOP design. Does it violate any of the SOLID principles? Does it have proper encapsulation?”

AI gives detailed OOP design feedback well.

Ask AI to explain polymorphism with a banking example

“Explain polymorphism in Java using a banking application as the example — different account types, same method name, different behaviour.”

Use AI to understand the JVM memory model

“Draw me a text diagram showing what happens in the JVM stack and heap when I create a new Account object and call a method on it.”

A warning about AI and OOP

AI can generate perfectly valid-looking OOP code that violates good design principles — public fields, god classes, unnecessary inheritance. Always ask AI to explain why it made each design choice, and compare against what you’ve learned in this chapter.

📝 Chapter 4 Homework

Complete these tasks before moving to Chapter 5. Estimated time: 4–5 hours.

Task 1 — Model a Real-World Object (Beginner)

Create a Customer class from scratch with: customerId (Long), firstName (String), lastName (String), email (String), phoneNumber (String), and createdAt (LocalDateTime).

Requirements: - All fields private - One constructor that takes firstName, lastName, and email (customerId and createdAt set automatically) - A getFullName() method that returns “FirstName LastName” - Override toString() to show a readable summary - Override equals() and hashCode() based on email — two customers with the same email are the same person

Write a main method that creates two Customer objects, prints them, and tests that two customers with the same email are considered equal.

Task 2 — Inheritance Tree (Intermediate)

Design and build an inheritance tree for FinTrack accounts:

  • Abstract parent class Account with: accountNumber, balance, owner (Customer), and abstract method calculateMonthlyFee()
  • CheckingAccount extends Account: monthly fee is $5 flat, unless balance is above $1500 in which case fee is $0
  • SavingsAccount extends Account: monthly fee is $0 but includes an applyMonthlyInterest() method that adds 0.4% of the balance
  • BusinessAccount extends Account: monthly fee is $25 flat

Write a main method that creates one of each, stores them in a List<Account>, iterates through the list, and calls calculateMonthlyFee() on each. Demonstrate polymorphism by showing that the correct fee calculation runs for each type even though you call the same method name.

Task 3 — Interface Design (Intermediate)

Design and implement two interfaces:

  • Transferable — with methods: canTransferTo(Account destination, BigDecimal amount) and transfer(Account destination, BigDecimal amount, String description)
  • Closeable — with methods: canBeClosed() and closeAccount()

Make CheckingAccount implement Transferable. Make both CheckingAccount and SavingsAccount implement Closeable. The closeAccount() method should throw an exception if the balance is not zero.

Task 4 — The static and final Keywords (Beginner)

Add to your Account class:

  • A static counter that tracks how many Account objects have been created
  • A static method getTotalAccountsCreated() that returns that counter
  • A static final constant MINIMUM_OPENING_BALANCE set to new BigDecimal("25.00")
  • Validate in the constructor that the opening balance meets this minimum

Write a test in main that creates five accounts and confirms the counter reaches 5.

Task 5 — FinTrack Extension (Challenge)

Implement a TransactionRecord class that is immutable (all fields final, no setters). It should store: transactionId, type (use an enum with DEPOSIT, WITHDRAWAL, TRANSFER), amount, description, and timestamp. Add a reverse() method that returns a NEW TransactionRecord with the opposite type (DEPOSIT becomes WITHDRAWAL and vice versa) and the same amount and description.

Chapter 5Core Java

Collections, Streams, and Robust Error Handling

Chapter Objectives

By the end of this chapter you will be able to:

✓ Choose the right Java collection for each situation — List, Set, Map, Queue — and explain the trade-offs

✓ Use ArrayList and LinkedList and explain when each is faster

✓ Use HashMap and explain how it works internally — buckets, hash codes, collisions

✓ Write Stream pipelines that filter, map, reduce, and collect data without loops

✓ Use Optional to eliminate null pointer exceptions in your code

✓ Create and throw custom exceptions with meaningful error information

✓ Implement the transaction filtering and balance calculation logic for FinTrack

Introduction

Every real program manages groups of data. A customer has multiple accounts. An account has hundreds of transactions. A bank processes thousands of transfers per minute. Java’s Collections Framework gives you the right data structure for every situation — each one a tool purpose-built for a specific job. Streams let you process those collections elegantly, without loops and mutable variables. Exception handling ensures your program communicates failures precisely rather than crashing with a cryptic stack trace.

This chapter goes deep on all three. By the end, you will understand not just the API but why each structure works the way it does, which lets you choose confidently and use them correctly even in situations you have never seen before.

5.1 The Collections Framework — A Family of Data Structures

The Java Collections Framework is a unified architecture of interfaces and classes for storing and manipulating groups of objects. Understanding the hierarchy makes the whole framework feel logical rather than a list of things to memorize.

The Interface Hierarchy

Iterable<E>
    └── Collection<E>
            ├── List<E>          — ordered, allows duplicates, indexed access
            │       ├── ArrayList<E>
            │       ├── LinkedList<E>
            │       └── Vector<E>   (legacy — avoid)
            │
            ├── Set<E>           — no duplicates
            │       ├── HashSet<E>
            │       ├── LinkedHashSet<E>   (insertion-ordered)
            │       └── TreeSet<E>          (sorted)
            │
            └── Queue<E>         — designed for FIFO access
                    ├── LinkedList<E>
                    ├── ArrayDeque<E>
                    └── PriorityQueue<E>

Map<K,V>       — key-value pairs (NOT a Collection)
    ├── HashMap<K,V>
    ├── LinkedHashMap<K,V>   (insertion-ordered)
    └── TreeMap<K,V>          (sorted by key)

Why program to interfaces? Because it lets you swap implementations without changing the code that uses them:

// Bad — tied to a specific implementation
ArrayList<Transaction> transactions = new ArrayList<>();

// Good — programming to the interface
List<Transaction> transactions = new ArrayList<>();
// Later you can change to LinkedList without touching any other code:
List<Transaction> transactions = new LinkedList<>();

5.2 List — Ordered Sequences

A List is an ordered sequence of elements. It allows duplicates and provides indexed access.

ArrayList — The Default Choice

ArrayList is backed by a resizable array. Think of it as a spreadsheet that grows automatically when you add rows.

How it works internally:

When you create new ArrayList<>(), Java allocates an internal array with an initial capacity of 10. When you add an 11th element, Java creates a new array of capacity 15 (roughly 1.5× the old size), copies everything over, and the old array is garbage-collected. This is called amortized O(1) — most insertions are O(1), but occasional ones are O(n) to copy. Averaged over many insertions, it is O(1).

// Declaring and adding
List<Transaction> transactions = new ArrayList<>();

transactions.add(new Transaction(DEPOSIT,    new BigDecimal("3200"), "Payroll",  account));
transactions.add(new Transaction(WITHDRAWAL, new BigDecimal("89.50"), "Grocery", account));
transactions.add(new Transaction(TRANSFER_OUT, new BigDecimal("500"), "Rent",    account));

// Capacity hint — if you know the size upfront, pre-allocate to avoid resizing
List<Transaction> monthlyHistory = new ArrayList<>(200); // expects ~200 transactions

// Size
int count = transactions.size(); // 3

// Index access — O(1), instant
Transaction first = transactions.get(0);
Transaction last  = transactions.get(transactions.size() - 1);

// Modify
transactions.set(0, updatedTransaction);        // replace at index 0

// Search
boolean exists = transactions.contains(someTransaction); // O(n) — scans all
int idx = transactions.indexOf(someTransaction); // O(n) — returns -1 if not found

// Remove
transactions.remove(0);                          // by index — O(n) (shifts elements)
transactions.remove(someTransaction);            // by object — O(n) (scans + shifts)

// Conditional remove — much more efficient than removing inside a loop
transactions.removeIf(t -> t.getAmount().compareTo(new BigDecimal("1")) < 0);

// Sort
transactions.sort(Comparator.comparing(Transaction::getTimestamp).reversed()); // newest first
Collections.sort(transactions); // requires Transaction implements Comparable

// Sublist — a VIEW into the original (not a copy!)
List<Transaction> recent = transactions.subList(0, 10); // first 10
// Modifying 'recent' also modifies 'transactions'

// Safe copy
List<Transaction> copy = new ArrayList<>(transactions);

// Unmodifiable view
List<Transaction> readOnly = Collections.unmodifiableList(transactions);
List<Transaction> immutable = List.of(t1, t2, t3); // Java 9+ — truly immutable

// Iteration options
for (Transaction t : transactions) { ... }                          // enhanced for
transactions.forEach(t -> System.out.println(t));                   // forEach
for (int i = 0; i < transactions.size(); i++) { ... }              // index loop
Iterator<Transaction> iter = transactions.iterator(); while (iter.hasNext()) { ... }

Big O complexity for ArrayList:

OperationComplexityReason
get(index)O(1)Direct array access
add(element) at endO(1) amortizedOccasionally resizes
add(index, element)O(n)Shifts subsequent elements
remove(index)O(n)Shifts subsequent elements
contains(element)O(n)Scans entire array
size()O(1)Stored as a counter

LinkedList — When You Insert in the Middle Constantly

LinkedList is backed by a doubly-linked list — each element (a “node”) holds a reference to the previous and next elements.

null ← [node: t1] ↔ [node: t2] ↔ [node: t3] → null
          head                         tail

Inserting at the beginning is O(1) — just rewire the head pointer. No elements shift. But accessing by index is O(n) — you must traverse from the head.

LinkedList<Transaction> transactionLog = new LinkedList<>();

// Fast operations
transactionLog.addFirst(newTransaction);  // O(1) — insert at beginning
transactionLog.addLast(newTransaction);   // O(1) — insert at end
transactionLog.removeFirst();             // O(1) — remove first
transactionLog.removeLast();              // O(1) — remove last
transactionLog.peekFirst();               // O(1) — view first without removing

// Slow operations
transactionLog.get(50);                   // O(n) — must traverse 50 nodes

// LinkedList also implements Deque (double-ended queue)
Deque<TransferRequest> pendingTransfers = new LinkedList<>();
pendingTransfers.offerFirst(urgentTransfer); // add to front
pendingTransfers.offerLast(normalTransfer);  // add to back

Use LinkedList when: you frequently insert or remove elements from the middle or ends of a large list. For everything else, ArrayList is faster in practice because of CPU cache effects — adjacent array elements load together into cache.

When to Use Which

SituationUse
General-purpose, unknown usageArrayList
Frequent insertions/deletions at endsLinkedList (as Deque)
Need to use as a stack or queueArrayDeque (faster than LinkedList)
Truly immutable listList.of(...)

5.3 Set — No Duplicates

A Set guarantees uniqueness — you cannot add the same element twice. This makes it perfect for deduplication and membership testing.

HashSet — Fastest, No Order Guarantee

HashSet is backed by a HashMap internally. Every object in a HashSet is a key in the map (with a dummy value). Understanding how HashMap works (Section 5.4) explains HashSet completely.

// Track which account numbers have been processed (no duplicates needed)
Set<String> processedAccounts = new HashSet<>();

processedAccounts.add("CHK-001"); // added
processedAccounts.add("SAV-001"); // added
processedAccounts.add("CHK-001"); // silently ignored — already present
System.out.println(processedAccounts.size()); // 2

// O(1) membership test — the killer feature
boolean alreadyDone = processedAccounts.contains("CHK-001"); // O(1)!
// Compare: ArrayList.contains() is O(n)

// Set operations
Set<String> activeAccounts = Set.of("CHK-001", "CHK-002", "SAV-001");
Set<String> closedAccounts = Set.of("CHK-003", "SAV-002");

// Intersection — accounts that are both active and... wait, they can't be both
Set<String> intersection = new HashSet<>(activeAccounts);
intersection.retainAll(closedAccounts); // keeps only elements in BOTH sets

// Union — all accounts
Set<String> allAccounts = new HashSet<>(activeAccounts);
allAccounts.addAll(closedAccounts);

// Difference — active accounts that are NOT closed
Set<String> difference = new HashSet<>(activeAccounts);
difference.removeAll(closedAccounts);

Critical requirement: For HashSet (and HashMap) to work correctly, elements must correctly implement equals() and hashCode(). If you store custom objects in a HashSet, you must override both:

public class Account {
    private String accountNumber;

    // Two accounts are equal if they have the same account number
    @Override
    public boolean equals(Object obj) {
        if (this == obj) return true;
        if (!(obj instanceof Account other)) return false;
        return Objects.equals(this.accountNumber, other.accountNumber);
    }

    // hashCode MUST be consistent with equals:
    // if a.equals(b) then a.hashCode() == b.hashCode() MUST be true
    @Override
    public int hashCode() {
        return Objects.hash(accountNumber);
    }
}

// Without these overrides:
Set<Account> accounts = new HashSet<>();
Account a1 = new Account("CHK-001", ...);
Account a2 = new Account("CHK-001", ...); // same number, different object
accounts.add(a1);
accounts.add(a2); // NOT detected as duplicate without equals/hashCode!
accounts.size();  // 2 — wrong!

LinkedHashSet — Insertion Order Preserved

Same as HashSet but maintains the order in which elements were inserted. Slightly slower than HashSet due to maintaining a linked list alongside the hash table.

// Process accounts in the order they were added
Set<String> orderedAccounts = new LinkedHashSet<>();
orderedAccounts.add("SAV-001"); // added first
orderedAccounts.add("CHK-001"); // added second
orderedAccounts.add("CHK-002"); // added third

for (String acct : orderedAccounts) {
    System.out.println(acct); // SAV-001, then CHK-001, then CHK-002
}

TreeSet — Always Sorted

TreeSet keeps elements in sorted order using a Red-Black Tree (a balanced binary search tree). All operations are O(log n) — slower than HashSet’s O(1) but you get sorting for free.

// Account numbers sorted alphabetically
Set<String> sortedAccounts = new TreeSet<>();
sortedAccounts.add("CHK-002");
sortedAccounts.add("SAV-001");
sortedAccounts.add("CHK-001");

System.out.println(sortedAccounts); // [CHK-001, CHK-002, SAV-001] — always sorted

// Custom sort order
Set<Transaction> byAmountDesc = new TreeSet<>(
    Comparator.comparing(Transaction::getAmount).reversed()
);

// Range operations — only possible because elements are sorted
NavigableSet<String> nav = new TreeSet<>(sortedAccounts);
nav.headSet("CHK-002")    // all elements before CHK-002
nav.tailSet("CHK-001")    // all elements from CHK-001 onwards
nav.subSet("CHK-001", "SAV-001") // range

5.4 HashMap — The Most Important Data Structure You Will Use

HashMap is the Swiss Army knife of data structures. It maps keys to values with O(1) average-time lookup, insertion, and deletion. Understanding how it works internally makes you a dramatically better programmer.

How HashMap Works Internally

A HashMap is backed by an array of “buckets.” Each bucket can hold a linked list of key-value pairs (called “entries” or “nodes”).

Index:  0     1     2     3     4     5     6     7
Array: [null][null][node][null][node][node][null][null]
                     |         |     |
                   (k,v)     (k,v) (k,v)
                             (collision — same bucket)

Putting a key-value pair: 1. Call key.hashCode() to get an integer 2. Apply a “spreading” function to distribute hash codes more evenly 3. Take (n-1) & hash to get an array index (where n is array size, always a power of 2) 4. Place the entry at that index (or chain to existing entries at that index)

Getting a value by key: 1. Compute the same hash and index 2. Find the entry at that index where key.equals(foundKey) 3. Return its value

// Create with an initial capacity and load factor
// Default capacity: 16 buckets, load factor: 0.75
// When entries / capacity > 0.75, the map RESIZES (doubles capacity, rehashes all)
Map<String, Account> accountMap = new HashMap<>(32, 0.75f);

// Basic operations
accountMap.put("CHK-001", checkingAccount);     // ← [.put(key, value): stores or replaces the entry. O(1) average — uses hashCode() to find the right bucket instantly.]
Account found = accountMap.get("CHK-001");       // ← [.get(key): O(1) average. Computes hashCode(), goes straight to the bucket, uses .equals() to find the right entry. Returns null if not found.]
boolean exists = accountMap.containsKey("CHK-001"); // ← [.containsKey: O(1). Use this instead of checking get() != null — it is clearer and more explicit about intent.]
accountMap.remove("CHK-001");                    // O(1)

// Safe retrieval patterns
Account account = accountMap.get("CHK-999");     // returns null if not found
Account safe = accountMap.getOrDefault("CHK-999", defaultAccount); // returns default

// Conditional operations
accountMap.putIfAbsent("CHK-001", newAccount);  // ← [Only stores the value if the key does NOT already exist. Atomic — safe for concurrent use. Prevents accidental overwrites.]
accountMap.computeIfAbsent("CHK-001", key -> createAccount(key)); // compute and put if absent

// Update an existing value
accountMap.merge("CHK-001", additionalFunds,
    (existingAccount, funds) -> {
        existingAccount.deposit(funds.getBalance());
        return existingAccount;
    });

// Iteration — order is NOT guaranteed (use LinkedHashMap if order matters)
for (Map.Entry<String, Account> entry : accountMap.entrySet()) {
    System.out.println(entry.getKey() + " → " + entry.getValue().getBalance());
}

// Stream iteration
accountMap.entrySet().stream()
    .filter(e -> e.getValue().isActive())
    .map(Map.Entry::getValue)
    .forEach(System.out::println);

// Get all keys or values
Set<String>      keys   = accountMap.keySet();   // returns a SET — keys are unique
Collection<Account> values = accountMap.values(); // returns a COLLECTION — duplicates possible

What Happens with Hash Collisions?

If two different keys produce the same bucket index (a “collision”), they are chained in the same bucket:

Bucket 3: → ("CHK-001", account1) → ("SAV-099", account2) → null

Finding "CHK-001" at bucket 3 requires scanning the chain using .equals(). If collisions are rare, this chain is short and lookup is still effectively O(1). If everything hashes to the same bucket (a very bad hashCode() implementation), lookup degrades to O(n).

Java 8+ optimization: When a bucket’s chain grows beyond 8 entries, it is converted to a balanced tree (Red-Black Tree), giving O(log n) worst-case performance instead of O(n). This protects against hash flooding attacks.

This is why hashCode() quality matters:

// Terrible hashCode — everything goes in the same bucket
@Override
public int hashCode() {
    return 42; // constant — technically valid but defeats the purpose
}
// Result: HashMap becomes O(n) for all operations

// Good hashCode — distributes values across many buckets
@Override
public int hashCode() {
    return Objects.hash(accountNumber, accountType); // uses multiple fields
}

TreeMap — Sorted Keys, O(log n)

Like TreeSet but for key-value pairs. Keys stay sorted, enabling range queries:

// Transaction history indexed by timestamp — sorted oldest to newest
TreeMap<LocalDateTime, Transaction> timeline = new TreeMap<>();
timeline.put(t1.getTimestamp(), t1);
timeline.put(t2.getTimestamp(), t2);

// Range query: all transactions in January 2025
LocalDateTime janStart = LocalDateTime.of(2025, 1, 1, 0, 0);
LocalDateTime janEnd   = LocalDateTime.of(2025, 1, 31, 23, 59, 59);
SortedMap<LocalDateTime, Transaction> january = timeline.subMap(janStart, janEnd);

// Navigation
Map.Entry<LocalDateTime, Transaction> latest = timeline.lastEntry();
Map.Entry<LocalDateTime, Transaction> earliest = timeline.firstEntry();

5.5 Queue and Deque — Ordered Processing

A Queue processes elements in order. A Deque (double-ended queue, pronounced “deck”) supports adding and removing from both ends.

// Process transfers in arrival order (FIFO)
Queue<TransferRequest> transferQueue = new ArrayDeque<>();

transferQueue.offer(new TransferRequest(fromId, toId, amount)); // add to back
TransferRequest next = transferQueue.poll();  // remove from front, null if empty
TransferRequest peek = transferQueue.peek();  // view front without removing

// Stack (LIFO) — used for undo functionality
Deque<AccountOperation> undoStack = new ArrayDeque<>();
undoStack.push(operation);          // add to front (stack push)
AccountOperation last = undoStack.pop(); // remove from front (stack pop)

// PriorityQueue — highest priority out first
// Process larger transfers before smaller ones
PriorityQueue<TransferRequest> priorityQueue = new PriorityQueue<>(
    Comparator.comparing(TransferRequest::getAmount).reversed() // largest first
);
priorityQueue.offer(transferOf100);
priorityQueue.offer(transferOf5000); // this will come out first
priorityQueue.offer(transferOf250);

TransferRequest first = priorityQueue.poll(); // returns transferOf5000

5.6 Choosing the Right Collection — A Decision Guide

Before writing new ArrayList<>() by reflex, ask:

QuestionPoints to
Do I need indexed access (get(5))?List
Do I need to prevent duplicates?Set
Do I need key → value lookup?Map
Do I need FIFO processing?Queue
Do I need LIFO (stack)?Deque
Do I need sorted order?TreeSet or TreeMap
Do I need insertion order?LinkedHashSet or LinkedHashMap
Do I need O(1) lookup by key?HashMap or HashSet
Do I need range queries?TreeMap
Will this be accessed by multiple threads?ConcurrentHashMap, CopyOnWriteArrayList

5.7 Exception Handling — Communicating Failure Precisely

An exception is an object that represents an error condition. When Java “throws” an exception, it interrupts normal execution and looks for a matching “catch” block in the call stack. If none is found, the program terminates with a stack trace.

The Exception Hierarchy

Throwable
├── Error   — serious JVM-level failures; do NOT catch
│   ├── OutOfMemoryError   — heap is full
│   ├── StackOverflowError — infinite recursion
│   └── VirtualMachineError
│
└── Exception
    ├── IOException (checked)       — I/O failures
    ├── SQLException (checked)      — database failures
    ├── ParseException (checked)    — parsing failures
    └── RuntimeException (unchecked)
        ├── NullPointerException    — null reference used
        ├── IllegalArgumentException — invalid method argument
        ├── IllegalStateException   — object in wrong state
        ├── ClassCastException      — invalid cast
        ├── IndexOutOfBoundsException — invalid index
        └── ArithmeticException     — e.g., division by zero

Checked exceptions — the compiler forces you to either catch them or declare them with throws. They represent recoverable conditions the caller should handle (file not found, network timeout).

Unchecked exceptions (RuntimeExceptions) — the compiler does NOT force you to handle them. They represent programming errors (using a null reference, invalid argument) or conditions the caller usually cannot meaningfully recover from.

Creating Custom Exceptions for FinTrack

Custom exceptions carry specific information about what went wrong, making error handling precise:

// Base exception for all FinTrack-specific errors
public class FinTrackException extends RuntimeException {

    // Machine-readable error code for the API response
    private final String errorCode;

    public FinTrackException(String message, String errorCode) {
        super(message);
        this.errorCode = errorCode;
    }

    // Constructor for wrapping a lower-level exception
    // Exception chaining preserves the original cause for debugging
    public FinTrackException(String message, String errorCode, Throwable cause) {
        super(message, cause);
        this.errorCode = errorCode;
    }

    public String getErrorCode() {
        return errorCode;
    }
}

// Specific exception with domain-specific data
public class InsufficientFundsException extends FinTrackException {

    private final BigDecimal requested;
    private final BigDecimal available;

    public InsufficientFundsException(BigDecimal requested, BigDecimal available) {
        super(
            String.format("Insufficient funds. Requested: $%,.2f, Available: $%,.2f",
                requested, available),
            "INSUFFICIENT_FUNDS"
        );
        this.requested = requested;
        this.available = available;
    }

    // Getters let callers use the structured data in the response
    public BigDecimal getRequested() { return requested; }
    public BigDecimal getAvailable() { return available; }
}

public class AccountNotFoundException extends FinTrackException {
    private final String accountIdentifier;

    public AccountNotFoundException(String accountIdentifier) {
        super("Account not found: " + accountIdentifier, "ACCOUNT_NOT_FOUND");
        this.accountIdentifier = accountIdentifier;
    }

    public AccountNotFoundException(Long accountId) {
        this(accountId.toString());
    }

    public String getAccountIdentifier() { return accountIdentifier; }
}

public class DailyLimitExceededException extends FinTrackException {
    public DailyLimitExceededException(BigDecimal limit, BigDecimal attempted,
                                        BigDecimal alreadyWithdrawn) {
        super(
            String.format("Daily limit of $%,.2f exceeded. " +
                "Already withdrawn today: $%,.2f, Attempted: $%,.2f",
                limit, alreadyWithdrawn, attempted),
            "DAILY_LIMIT_EXCEEDED"
        );
    }
}

public class TransactionLimitExceededException extends FinTrackException {
    public TransactionLimitExceededException(int limit) {
        super("Monthly transaction limit of " + limit + " reached", "MONTHLY_LIMIT_EXCEEDED");
    }
}

try-catch-finally — Handling Exceptions

public TransferResult transfer(Long fromId, Long toId,
                               BigDecimal amount, String description) {
    try {
        // Code that might throw exceptions
        Account fromAccount = accountRepository.findById(fromId)
            .orElseThrow(() -> new AccountNotFoundException(fromId));

        Account toAccount = accountRepository.findById(toId)
            .orElseThrow(() -> new AccountNotFoundException(toId));

        if (amount == null || amount.compareTo(BigDecimal.ZERO) <= 0) {
            throw new IllegalArgumentException("Transfer amount must be positive");
        }

        if (fromId.equals(toId)) {
            throw new IllegalArgumentException("Cannot transfer to the same account");
        }

        // These can throw InsufficientFundsException or DailyLimitExceededException
        fromAccount.withdraw(amount);
        toAccount.deposit(amount);

        Transaction debit  = transactionRepo.save(new Transaction(TRANSFER_OUT, amount, description, fromAccount));
        Transaction credit = transactionRepo.save(new Transaction(TRANSFER_IN,  amount, description, toAccount));

        return TransferResult.success(debit, credit);

    } catch (AccountNotFoundException e) {
        // Handle specific exception — we know exactly what happened
        log.warn("Transfer failed — account not found: {}", e.getAccountIdentifier());
        return TransferResult.failure("ACCOUNT_NOT_FOUND", e.getMessage());

    } catch (InsufficientFundsException e) {
        // We can use the structured data in the exception
        log.info("Transfer rejected — insufficient funds. Account: {}, Available: ${}",
            fromId, e.getAvailable());
        return TransferResult.failure("INSUFFICIENT_FUNDS",
            "Available balance: $" + e.getAvailable());

    } catch (DailyLimitExceededException | TransactionLimitExceededException e) {
        // Multi-catch — handle multiple exceptions the same way
        return TransferResult.failure(e.getErrorCode(), e.getMessage());

    } catch (FinTrackException e) {
        // Catch-all for other known banking errors
        log.error("Banking error during transfer: {}", e.getMessage());
        return TransferResult.failure(e.getErrorCode(), e.getMessage());

    } catch (Exception e) {
        // Unexpected error — log the full stack trace
        log.error("Unexpected error during transfer from {} to {}", fromId, toId, e);
        return TransferResult.failure("INTERNAL_ERROR", "An unexpected error occurred");

    } finally {
        // ALWAYS runs — whether an exception was thrown or not, caught or not
        // Use for: logging, closing resources, recording audit events
        auditLog.record("TRANSFER_ATTEMPT", fromId, toId, amount, LocalDateTime.now());
    }
}

Rules for ordering catch blocks: 1. More specific exceptions come BEFORE more general ones 2. InsufficientFundsException before FinTrackException before Exception 3. The compiler will error if an earlier catch would catch everything the later one catches

try-with-resources — Automatic Resource Closing

Resources like database connections, file handles, and network sockets must be closed after use. try-with-resources guarantees closure even if an exception is thrown:

// WITHOUT try-with-resources — verbose and error-prone
Connection conn = null;
PreparedStatement stmt = null;
ResultSet rs = null;
try {
    conn = dataSource.getConnection();
    stmt = conn.prepareStatement("SELECT * FROM accounts WHERE account_id = ?");
    stmt.setLong(1, accountId);
    rs = stmt.executeQuery();
    // ... process results
} finally {
    // Must close in reverse order, handling exceptions in close() too
    if (rs   != null) try { rs.close();   } catch (SQLException e) { log.error(...); }
    if (stmt != null) try { stmt.close(); } catch (SQLException e) { log.error(...); }
    if (conn != null) try { conn.close(); } catch (SQLException e) { log.error(...); }
}

// WITH try-with-resources — clean and guaranteed
// Any class implementing AutoCloseable can be used here
try (Connection conn = dataSource.getConnection();
     PreparedStatement stmt = conn.prepareStatement(
         "SELECT * FROM accounts WHERE account_id = ?")) {

    stmt.setLong(1, accountId);

    try (ResultSet rs = stmt.executeQuery()) {
        if (rs.next()) {
            return mapToAccount(rs);
        }
    }
    return Optional.empty();

} catch (SQLException e) {
    // conn and stmt are ALREADY CLOSED by the time we get here
    throw new DatabaseException("Failed to find account " + accountId, e);
}
// conn and stmt are closed here — no finally block needed

Resources are closed in reverse order of declaration. If an exception occurs in close(), it is suppressed (but accessible via getSuppressed()) — the original exception is preserved.

Exception Best Practices

// ✅ Throw early, be specific
public void setBalance(BigDecimal balance) {
    if (balance == null) throw new NullPointerException("balance cannot be null");
    if (balance.compareTo(BigDecimal.ZERO) < 0) {
        throw new IllegalArgumentException("balance cannot be negative: " + balance);
    }
    this.balance = balance;
}

// ❌ Never swallow exceptions silently
try {
    processTransaction(t);
} catch (Exception e) {
    // Silent swallow — the error disappears, debugging becomes impossible
}

// ✅ Log and rethrow, or wrap with context
try {
    processTransaction(t);
} catch (DatabaseException e) {
    log.error("Failed to process transaction {}", t.getId(), e);
    throw new TransactionProcessingException("Processing failed for " + t.getId(), e);
}

// ❌ Catching Exception broadly (too wide)
try { ... }
catch (Exception e) { handle(e); } // catches NullPointerException, ClassCastException, etc.

// ✅ Catch what you can handle
try { ... }
catch (InsufficientFundsException e) { returnError(e); }
catch (AccountNotFoundException   e) { returnError(e); }
// Let unexpected RuntimeExceptions propagate — they indicate bugs

// ❌ Using exceptions for control flow
try {
    int index = Integer.parseInt(userInput);
} catch (NumberFormatException e) {
    // Using exception to detect invalid input — awkward
}

// ✅ Validate before attempting
if (userInput.matches("\\d+")) {
    int index = Integer.parseInt(userInput);
}

5.8 Generics — Type Safety Without Repetition

Generics let you write classes and methods that work with any type, while maintaining compile-time type safety. Without generics, you would write a separate AccountList, TransactionList, CustomerList class for every type.

// Without generics — uses Object, loses type safety
List list = new ArrayList();
list.add("CHK-001");
list.add(42);             // No compile error — anything goes!
String s = (String) list.get(1); // ClassCastException at RUNTIME — crash!

// With generics — type is checked at COMPILE TIME
List<String> accountNumbers = new ArrayList<>();
accountNumbers.add("CHK-001"); // fine
accountNumbers.add(42);        // COMPILE ERROR — caught before running!
String s = accountNumbers.get(0); // no cast needed — always a String

Writing Generic Classes and Methods

// Generic class — T is a type parameter, like a placeholder for a type
public class Pair<A, B> {
    private final A first;
    private final B second;

    public Pair(A first, B second) {
        this.first = first;
        this.second = second;
    }

    public A getFirst()  { return first; }
    public B getSecond() { return second; }

    @Override
    public String toString() {
        return "(" + first + ", " + second + ")";
    }
}

// Using the generic class
Pair<String, BigDecimal> accountBalance = new Pair<>("CHK-001", new BigDecimal("1250.00"));
Pair<Long, List<Transaction>> accountHistory = new Pair<>(42L, transactions);

// Generic method — works with any type that extends Account
public static <T extends Account> T findHighestBalance(List<T> accounts) {
    if (accounts == null || accounts.isEmpty()) {
        throw new IllegalArgumentException("Account list cannot be empty");
    }
    T highest = accounts.get(0);
    for (T account : accounts) {
        if (account.getBalance().compareTo(highest.getBalance()) > 0) {
            highest = account;
        }
    }
    return highest;
}

// Works for any Account subtype, and returns that specific type
SavingsAccount richest = findHighestBalance(savingsAccounts);
CheckingAccount mostCash = findHighestBalance(checkingAccounts);

Bounded Type Parameters and Wildcards

// Upper bounded wildcard — T must be Account or a subclass
// Useful for reading: "I accept a list of any kind of Account"
public BigDecimal sumBalances(List<? extends Account> accounts) {
    return accounts.stream()
        .map(Account::getBalance)
        .reduce(BigDecimal.ZERO, BigDecimal::add);  // ← [reduce combines all stream elements into one. Starts with ZERO, then: 0 + amount1 = x, x + amount2 = y... Terminal operation — triggers the whole pipeline.]
}

sumBalances(checkingAccounts);  // works
sumBalances(savingsAccounts);   // works
sumBalances(mixedAccounts);     // works

// Lower bounded wildcard — T must be Account or a superclass
// Useful for writing: "I can put Accounts into this"
public void addToCollection(Account account, List<? super Account> collection) {
    collection.add(account);
}

5.9 Java 8+ Functional Programming — Lambdas, Streams, Optional

Java 8 introduced a functional programming style alongside the existing OOP style. Understanding both and knowing when to use each is a mark of a skilled Java developer.

Lambda Expressions — Functions as Values

A lambda is an anonymous function — a function with no name, declared inline where it is used. It enables you to pass behavior as a parameter.

// Before lambdas — anonymous inner class (verbose)
Comparator<Transaction> byAmount = new Comparator<Transaction>() {
    @Override
    public int compare(Transaction t1, Transaction t2) {
        return t1.getAmount().compareTo(t2.getAmount());
    }
};

// With lambda — much cleaner
Comparator<Transaction> byAmount = (t1, t2) -> t1.getAmount().compareTo(t2.getAmount());

// Even cleaner with method reference
Comparator<Transaction> byAmount = Comparator.comparing(Transaction::getAmount);

// Lambda syntax variations
() -> System.out.println("no params, no return")
(x) -> x * 2                   // one param, implicit return
x -> x * 2                     // parens optional for single param
(x, y) -> x + y                // two params
(x, y) -> { return x + y; }    // explicit return with braces
(BigDecimal x) -> x.negate()   // explicit type annotation

Functional Interfaces — The Type of a Lambda

A functional interface is an interface with exactly one abstract method. Lambdas implement functional interfaces.

// The four most important functional interfaces in java.util.function:

// Supplier<T>: () -> T — produces a value
Supplier<Account> accountFactory = () -> new CheckingAccount("CHK-NEW", BigDecimal.ZERO, customer);
Account newAccount = accountFactory.get();

// Consumer<T>: T -> void — consumes a value
Consumer<Transaction> logger = t -> System.out.println("Processing: " + t);
transactions.forEach(logger);

// Function<T, R>: T -> R — transforms a value
Function<Account, BigDecimal> getBalance = Account::getBalance;
Function<Account, String> toSummary = a -> a.getAccountNumber() + ": $" + a.getBalance();

// Predicate<T>: T -> boolean — tests a condition
Predicate<Transaction> isLarge = t -> t.getAmount().compareTo(new BigDecimal("1000")) > 0;
Predicate<Account> isActive = Account::isActive;

// Composing predicates
Predicate<Account> activeWithHighBalance = isActive.and(
    a -> a.getBalance().compareTo(new BigDecimal("5000")) > 0
);

// BiFunction<T, U, R>: (T, U) -> R — two inputs, one output
BiFunction<BigDecimal, BigDecimal, BigDecimal> add = BigDecimal::add;

Method References — Compact Lambda Shorthand

// Lambda:  t -> t.getAmount()
// Method reference: Transaction::getAmount
// These are equivalent — method reference is just shorter

// Four types of method references:
// 1. Static method: ClassName::staticMethodName
Function<String, Integer> parse = Integer::parseInt;

// 2. Instance method on a particular object: instance::methodName
AccountService service = new AccountService();
Consumer<Long> closer = service::closeAccount; // service.closeAccount(id)

// 3. Instance method on an arbitrary object of the type: ClassName::instanceMethodName
Function<Transaction, BigDecimal> getAmount = Transaction::getAmount;
// equivalent to: t -> t.getAmount()

// 4. Constructor: ClassName::new
BiFunction<String, BigDecimal, CheckingAccount> creator = CheckingAccount::new;
CheckingAccount account = creator.apply("CHK-NEW", new BigDecimal("1000"));

The Stream API — Declarative Data Processing

A Stream is a pipeline of operations on a sequence of elements. It does not store data — it processes a source (collection, array, or generator) through intermediate and terminal operations.

┌─────────────┐     ┌──────────────────────────────────┐     ┌──────────────────┐
│   Source    │ ──► │   Intermediate Operations        │ ──► │ Terminal Op.     │
│             │     │   (lazy — not executed until     │     │ (triggers exec.) │
│ collection  │     │    the terminal op is called)    │     │                  │
│ array       │     │   filter()  map()  flatMap()     │     │ collect()        │
│ Stream.of() │     │   sorted()  distinct() peek()    │     │ reduce()         │
│ Stream.gen()│     │   limit()   skip()               │     │ count()          │
│             │     │                                  │     │ findFirst()      │
└─────────────┘     └──────────────────────────────────┘     │ anyMatch()       │
                                                              │ forEach()        │
                                                              └──────────────────┘

Key insight: Intermediate operations are lazy — they do no work until a terminal operation is called. This allows the JVM to optimize the whole pipeline.

List<Transaction> transactions = account.getTransactions(); // can be a large list

// Example 1: Find all large deposits from this month
List<Transaction> largeDepositsThisMonth = transactions.stream()
    .filter(t -> t.getType() == TransactionType.DEPOSIT)  // ← [filter keeps elements where the lambda returns true. t -> is shorthand for a function: "given a transaction t, check if its type is DEPOSIT".]       // keep deposits
    .filter(t -> t.getTimestamp().getMonth() == Month.JANUARY) // from January
    .filter(t -> t.getAmount().compareTo(new BigDecimal("500")) > 0) // over $500
    .sorted(Comparator.comparing(Transaction::getAmount).reversed()) // largest first
    .collect(Collectors.toList());

// Example 2: Calculate total spent by category
Map<String, BigDecimal> spendingByCategory = transactions.stream()
    .filter(t -> t.getType() == TransactionType.WITHDRAWAL)
    .collect(Collectors.groupingBy(
        Transaction::getCategory,                      // group by category
        Collectors.reducing(BigDecimal.ZERO,           // start with zero
                            Transaction::getAmount,    // extract amount
                            BigDecimal::add)           // combine
    ));

// Example 3: Total balance across all accounts
BigDecimal totalBalance = accounts.stream()
    .filter(Account::isActive)
    .map(Account::getBalance)
    .reduce(BigDecimal.ZERO, BigDecimal::add);

// Example 4: Flat map — each account has a list of transactions
// flatMap flattens "List of Lists" into a single stream
List<Transaction> allTransactions = accounts.stream()
    .flatMap(account -> account.getTransactions().stream()) // Account → Stream<Transaction>
    .distinct()
    .collect(Collectors.toList());

// Example 5: Statistics
DoubleSummaryStatistics stats = transactions.stream()
    .mapToDouble(t -> t.getAmount().doubleValue())
    .summaryStatistics();

System.out.println("Count: " + stats.getCount());
System.out.println("Average: " + stats.getAverage());
System.out.println("Max: " + stats.getMax());
System.out.println("Sum: " + stats.getSum());

// Example 6: Partitioning
Map<Boolean, List<Transaction>> creditVsDebit = transactions.stream()
    .collect(Collectors.partitioningBy(
        t -> t.getType() == DEPOSIT || t.getType() == TRANSFER_IN
    ));

List<Transaction> credits = creditVsDebit.get(true);
List<Transaction> debits  = creditVsDebit.get(false);

// Example 7: Joining strings
String summary = transactions.stream()
    .map(t -> t.getDescription())
    .collect(Collectors.joining(", ", "Transactions: [", "]"));
// "Transactions: [Payroll, Grocery, Rent]"

// Example 8: Short-circuit terminal operations
// These stop processing as soon as the answer is known
Optional<Transaction> firstLarge = transactions.stream()
    .filter(t -> t.getAmount().compareTo(new BigDecimal("1000")) > 0)
    .findFirst(); // stops after finding the first match — does not process the rest

boolean hasOverdraft = transactions.stream()
    .anyMatch(t -> t.getType() == FEE && t.getDescription().contains("Overdraft"));
    // stops as soon as one match is found

boolean allCompleted = transactions.stream()
    .allMatch(t -> t.getStatus() == COMPLETED);
    // stops as soon as one non-match is found

Optional — Explicit Absence of a Value

Optional<T> is a container that either holds a value or is empty. It forces you to acknowledge that a value might not exist, preventing NullPointerException.

public Optional<Account> findByAccountNumber(String accountNumber) {
    return accounts.stream()
        .filter(a -> a.getAccountNumber().equals(accountNumber))
        .findFirst();
    // Returns Optional.of(account) if found, Optional.empty() if not
}

// WRONG patterns with Optional
Optional<Account> opt = findByAccountNumber("CHK-001");
Account account = opt.get(); // Throws NoSuchElementException if empty!
// Optional.get() without checking presence defeats the purpose

// CORRECT patterns
// 1. Check first (verbose, error-prone — prefer other patterns)
if (opt.isPresent()) {
    process(opt.get());
}

// 2. ifPresent — execute a consumer only if value exists
opt.ifPresent(account -> processAccount(account));
opt.ifPresent(this::processAccount); // same with method reference

// 3. orElse — return a default if empty
Account account = opt.orElse(Account.createDefault());
// CAUTION: orElse() ALWAYS evaluates the default, even if not needed

// 4. orElseGet — lazily evaluate the default (PREFER this)
Account account = opt.orElseGet(() -> createDefaultAccount(customerId));
// The lambda is only called if Optional is empty

// 5. orElseThrow — throw if empty (most common in service methods)
Account account = opt.orElseThrow(
    () -> new AccountNotFoundException("CHK-001")
);

// 6. map — transform the value if present
Optional<BigDecimal> balance = opt.map(Account::getBalance);

// 7. filter — keep the value only if it passes a test
Optional<Account> activeOpt = opt.filter(Account::isActive);

// 8. flatMap — for nested optionals
// If the mapper returns Optional<T>, flatMap avoids Optional<Optional<T>>
Optional<Customer> customer = opt.flatMap(account -> findCustomer(account.getOwnerId()));

// 9. ifPresentOrElse (Java 9+)
opt.ifPresentOrElse(
    account -> processAccount(account),
    () -> log.warn("Account not found")
);

// Creating Optionals
Optional<Account> present = Optional.of(account);       // throws if null!
Optional<Account> nullable = Optional.ofNullable(maybeNull); // safe for null
Optional<Account> empty   = Optional.empty();

What NOT to do with Optional:

// Don't use Optional for method parameters — use overloading instead
public void process(Optional<String> name) { ... } // BAD

// Don't use Optional in fields — use null or a default value
private Optional<BigDecimal> overdraftLimit; // BAD

// Don't use Optional just to avoid null checks — use it for return types
// where absence is a meaningful, expected result

5.10 Comparable and Comparator — Custom Ordering

Comparable — Natural Order

Implement Comparable<T> when your class has a single, obvious “natural” sort order:

public class Transaction implements Comparable<Transaction> {

    private LocalDateTime timestamp;
    private BigDecimal amount;
    // ...

    // Natural order: newest transactions first
    @Override
    public int compareTo(Transaction other) {
        // compareTo returns:
        //  negative: this comes BEFORE other
        //  zero:     they are equal
        //  positive: this comes AFTER other
        return other.timestamp.compareTo(this.timestamp); // reverse for newest-first
    }
}

// Now you can sort without specifying a comparator
List<Transaction> transactions = account.getTransactions();
Collections.sort(transactions);        // uses compareTo
transactions.sort(null);               // also uses compareTo
TreeSet<Transaction> sorted = new TreeSet<>(transactions); // also uses compareTo

Comparator — Multiple Sort Orders

Use Comparator when you need different sort orders or the class is not yours to modify:

// Various comparators for Transaction
Comparator<Transaction> byAmount   = Comparator.comparing(Transaction::getAmount);
Comparator<Transaction> byTime     = Comparator.comparing(Transaction::getTimestamp);
Comparator<Transaction> byType     = Comparator.comparing(Transaction::getType);

// Reversed
Comparator<Transaction> largestFirst  = byAmount.reversed();
Comparator<Transaction> newestFirst   = byTime.reversed();

// Multi-level sort: by type, then within each type by amount descending
Comparator<Transaction> multiSort = byType.thenComparing(byAmount.reversed());

// Nulls handling
Comparator<Transaction> safeByAmount = Comparator.nullsLast(byAmount);

// Using in sort
transactions.sort(newestFirst);
transactions.sort(Comparator.comparing(Transaction::getAmount)
                             .reversed()
                             .thenComparing(Transaction::getTimestamp));

// In TreeMap with custom key order
TreeMap<BigDecimal, Transaction> byAmount = new TreeMap<>(Comparator.reverseOrder());

Chapter Summary

Collections, streams, and exceptions are three tools you will use in every single Java program you write:

  • List is ordered and indexed. ArrayList for general use; LinkedList as a Deque for stack/queue.
  • Set prevents duplicates. HashSet for O(1) lookup; TreeSet for sorted order; LinkedHashSet for insertion order.
  • HashMap maps keys to values in O(1). It uses hashCode() to bucket and equals() to identify. Override both correctly or HashMap breaks silently.
  • TreeMap maps keys to values in sorted order (O(log n)) and supports range queries.
  • Exceptions communicate failure. Use custom exception types that carry domain-specific data. Catch specific exceptions before general ones. Never swallow exceptions silently.
  • try-with-resources automatically closes AutoCloseable resources — always use it for connections and file handles.
  • Lambdas are inline anonymous functions. They implement functional interfaces.
  • Streams are lazy pipelines over data sources. Use filter, map, collect, reduce, and short-circuit operations like findFirst, anyMatch.
  • Optional makes absence explicit. Prefer orElseGet, orElseThrow, and ifPresent over get() without a presence check.
  • Comparable defines natural order; Comparator defines alternative orders and supports chaining.

Interview Prep

  • Explain how HashMap works internally. What happens when two keys hash to the same bucket?
  • What is the time complexity of HashMap.get(), ArrayList.get(index), and TreeMap.get(key)? Explain why.
  • Why must you override hashCode() whenever you override equals()? What specific problem occurs if you don’t?
  • What is the difference between Comparable and Comparator? When would you use each?
  • What is a checked exception? Give an example of when you would create one versus an unchecked exception.
  • Explain what Optional.orElse() does differently from Optional.orElseGet(). When does the difference matter?
  • What does flatMap do in a Stream? Give a concrete example where you would need it instead of map.
  • What is a functional interface? Name four from java.util.function and describe what each does.
  • What is the difference between filter and map in a Stream?
  • If you have a List<Account> and want to find the account with the highest balance without using streams, how would you do it? Now do it with a stream.

Quick Knowledge Check

  • What collection would you use to ensure a set of account numbers has no duplicates AND retrieves in O(1)? (HashSet)
  • True or False: TreeMap retrieves values in O(1) time. (False — O(log n) because it uses a tree structure.)
  • Fill in the blank: If a.equals(b) is true, then a.hashCode() must equal b.hashCode(). This rule is called the ___ contract. (hashCode/equals)
  • What happens if you call Optional.get() on an empty Optional? (Throws NoSuchElementException)
  • True or False: Stream intermediate operations like filter() execute immediately when called. (False — they are lazy and execute only when a terminal operation is called.)
  • What is the difference between Collection and Collections in Java? (Collection is an interface; Collections is a utility class with static methods like sort(), shuffle(), unmodifiableList())

Given this list of transactions, use streams to answer each question with ONE pipeline each (no loops):

List<Transaction> txns = Arrays.asList(
    new Transaction("DEPOSIT",   new BigDecimal("3200.00"), "Payroll"),
    new Transaction("WITHDRAWAL",new BigDecimal("89.99"),  "Amazon"),
    new Transaction("WITHDRAWAL",new BigDecimal("500.00"), "Rent"),
    new Transaction("DEPOSIT",   new BigDecimal("1200.00"),"Freelance"),
    new Transaction("WITHDRAWAL",new BigDecimal("120.00"), "Electricity"),
    new Transaction("DEPOSIT",   new BigDecimal("500.00"), "Refund")
);
  • What is the total amount of all deposits?

Common Mistakes in Chapter 5

❌ Modifying a List while iterating over it

for (Transaction t : transactions) {
    if (t.getAmount().compareTo(ZERO) < 0) {
        transactions.remove(t);  // WRONG — ConcurrentModificationException
    }
}
// CORRECT — use removeIf:
transactions.removeIf(t -> t.getAmount().compareTo(ZERO) < 0);

❌ Expecting HashMap to be sorted HashMap makes no guarantees about order. If you need sorted keys, use TreeMap. If you need insertion order, use LinkedHashMap.

❌ Using double for money in BigDecimal comparisons

new BigDecimal(0.1)           // WRONG — 0.1 cannot be represented exactly in binary
new BigDecimal("0.1")         // CORRECT — always use the String constructor

❌ Forgetting that streams are lazy A stream pipeline does nothing until a terminal operation (collect, forEach, reduce, etc.) is called. This surprises many beginners.

❌ Swallowing exceptions

try {
    account.withdraw(amount);
} catch (Exception e) {
    // WRONG — silently ignores the error
}

try {
    account.withdraw(amount);
} catch (InsufficientFundsException e) {
    log.error("Withdrawal failed: {}", e.getMessage()); // CORRECT — log it
    throw e; // re-throw if the caller needs to know
}

Once you have written a stream pipeline using a loop (it is fine to start that way), paste it and ask:

“I wrote this using a for loop. How would I rewrite it as a Java Stream? Explain each step of the stream version.”

“Explain exactly what happens inside a HashMap when I call put(‘CHK-001’, account) — walk me through hashCode, bucket selection, and collision handling.”

AI and exceptions — ask for the right level

“I have a deposit method. What exceptions should it throw, what information should each exception carry, and when specifically should each one be thrown?”

AI is excellent at reasoning about exception design once you give it the business context.
