Chapter 6DS & Algorithms

Data Structures and Algorithms — How Programs Think Efficiently

Chapter Objectives

By the end of this chapter you will be able to:

✓ Calculate Big O time and space complexity for common operations and explain what it means in practice

✓ Implement a linked list from scratch including add, remove, and search operations

✓ Implement a binary search tree and perform in-order, pre-order, and post-order traversal

✓ Implement binary search and merge sort and explain why each works

✓ Use BFS and DFS to traverse graphs and explain when each is appropriate

✓ Choose the right data structure for a given banking problem with justification

✓ Implement a transaction history system using appropriate data structures for FinTrack

Introduction

Here is a scenario: FinTrack has ten million customers, each with multiple accounts and hundreds of transactions. A customer opens the app and expects their balance to appear in under a second. The database has ten billion transaction records. How do you find the right ones instantly?

The answer is not “a faster computer.” The answer is the right data structure and the right algorithm. A program that takes ten billion steps to answer a question cannot be made fast enough by buying better hardware. A program that takes thirty steps can run on a laptop from 2005.

This chapter teaches you to think about computation. Not just “does the code work?” but “does the code scale?” That shift in thinking is what makes you a real software engineer rather than someone who codes.

6.1 Big O Notation — Measuring How Algorithms Scale

Big O notation describes how an algorithm’s resource consumption grows as the input size n grows. It is not about speed in seconds — it is about the shape of growth.

Why Raw Speed Is the Wrong Metric

Machine A runs 1 billion operations per second
Machine B runs 1 million operations per second

Algorithm X: O(n^2) operations for n inputs
Algorithm Y: O(n log n) operations for n inputs

At n = 1,000,000 (1 million transactions):
  X on Machine A: 1,000,000,000,000 ops / 1B ops/sec = 1,000 seconds
  Y on Machine B: 20,000,000 ops / 1M ops/sec = 20 seconds

Algorithm Y on the SLOWER machine wins by 50x.

The algorithm is more important than the hardware.

The Complexity Classes

O(1) — Constant Time

The operation takes the same amount of time regardless of input size.

// HashMap lookup — always one hash computation, one array access
Map<String, Account> registry = new HashMap<>();
Account found = registry.get("CHK-001"); // O(1) regardless of map size

// Array index access — directly computed: base_address + (index * element_size)
BigDecimal[] balances = {new BigDecimal("100"), new BigDecimal("500")};
BigDecimal first = balances[0]; // O(1)
O(log n) — Logarithmic Time

Each step cuts the remaining work in half.

n = 1,000 → max 10 steps
n = 1,000,000 → max 20 steps
n = 1,000,000,000 → max 30 steps
Doubling n adds only ONE more step. This is incredibly powerful.
// Binary search in a sorted array
public static int binarySearch(int[] sorted, int target) {
    int left = 0, right = sorted.length - 1;
    while (left <= right) {
        int mid = left + (right - left) / 2; // avoids integer overflow
        if (sorted[mid] == target)  return mid;       // found
        if (sorted[mid] < target)   left = mid + 1;   // look right
        else                        right = mid - 1;  // look left
    }
    return -1;
}

// TreeMap operations — balanced binary search tree
TreeMap<LocalDateTime, Transaction> timeline = new TreeMap<>();
timeline.put(timestamp, transaction); // O(log n)
timeline.subMap(start, end);          // O(log n)
O(n) — Linear Time

You must examine each element at least once.

// Find the largest transaction — must check all
public BigDecimal findLargest(List<Transaction> transactions) {
    BigDecimal max = BigDecimal.ZERO;
    for (Transaction t : transactions) { // n iterations
        if (t.getAmount().compareTo(max) > 0) max = t.getAmount();
    }
    return max; // O(n)
}
O(n log n) — Linearithmic Time

The best possible complexity for comparison-based sorting.

// Java's built-in sort — Timsort: O(n log n) in all cases
Collections.sort(transactions, Comparator.comparing(Transaction::getTimestamp));
O(n^2) — Quadratic Time

Every element is compared with every other element.

// Bubble sort — never use for large data
for (int i = 0; i < n - 1; i++) {         // n passes
    for (int j = 0; j < n - i - 1; j++) { // n comparisons per pass
        if (arr[j] > arr[j + 1]) swap(arr, j, j + 1);
    }
}
// n = 10,000: 100,000,000 operations vs Java's sort: ~130,000 operations

Complexity Comparison Table

nO(1)O(log n)O(n)O(n log n)O(n^2)
10131033100
1,0001101,00010,0001,000,000
1,000,0001201,000,00020,000,00010^12

Space Complexity

Big O also measures memory:

// O(1) space — only one extra variable
public BigDecimal sum(List<BigDecimal> amounts) {
    BigDecimal total = BigDecimal.ZERO;
    for (BigDecimal a : amounts) total = total.add(a);
    return total;
}

// O(n) space — creates a new list proportional to input
public List<Transaction> filterDeposits(List<Transaction> all) {
    List<Transaction> result = new ArrayList<>(); // up to n elements
    all.stream().filter(t -> t.getType() == DEPOSIT).forEach(result::add);
    return result;
}

6.2 Arrays — The Foundation of All Data Structures

An array is a contiguous block of memory. Almost every other data structure is built on arrays.

How Array Access Is O(1)

int[] balances = {100, 250, 1800, 950, 2000};

Memory: [100][250][1800][950][2000]
Addr:   1000 1004  1008 1012 1016

Address of balances[3] = 1000 + (3 x 4 bytes) = 1012
The CPU computes this DIRECTLY — no scanning needed.
This is why array[index] is always O(1).

Essential Array Algorithms

// 1. Two-Pointer Technique — O(n) time, O(1) space
// Find if any two transaction amounts sum to a target (sorted input)
public static boolean hasPairWithSum(int[] sorted, int target) {
    int left = 0, right = sorted.length - 1;
    while (left < right) {
        int sum = sorted[left] + sorted[right];
        if (sum == target) return true;
        if (sum < target)  left++;   // need larger sum
        else               right--;  // need smaller sum
    }
    return false;
}

// 2. Sliding Window — O(n) time, O(1) space
// Find the maximum sum of any k consecutive daily balances
public static long maxSumWindow(int[] dailyBalances, int k) {
    if (dailyBalances.length < k) throw new IllegalArgumentException();

    // Compute sum of first window
    long windowSum = 0;
    for (int i = 0; i < k; i++) windowSum += dailyBalances[i];

    long maxSum = windowSum;

    // Slide the window: add next element, remove first element
    for (int i = k; i < dailyBalances.length; i++) {
        windowSum += dailyBalances[i] - dailyBalances[i - k];
        maxSum = Math.max(maxSum, windowSum);
    }
    return maxSum;
}

// 3. Prefix Sums — precompute O(n), then answer any range query in O(1)
// Sum of transactions[left..right] without iterating every time
public static long[] buildPrefixSums(long[] amounts) {
    long[] prefix = new long[amounts.length + 1];
    prefix[0] = 0;
    for (int i = 0; i < amounts.length; i++) {
        prefix[i + 1] = prefix[i] + amounts[i];
    }
    return prefix;
}

// O(1) range sum query after O(n) preprocessing
public static long rangeSum(long[] prefix, int left, int right) {
    return prefix[right + 1] - prefix[left];
}

// FinTrack use: precompute daily totals once,
// then answer "total deposited in January" instantly

6.3 Linked Lists — Chains of Nodes

A linked list is a sequence of nodes, each containing data and a reference to the next node. Unlike arrays, nodes are scattered in memory — no address formula, so random access is O(n). But insertion and deletion at known positions are O(1).

Singly: head -> [data|next] -> [data|next] -> [data|next] -> null

Doubly: null <- [prev|data|next] <-> [prev|data|next] <-> [prev|data|next] -> null
               head                                        tail

Building a Linked List from Scratch

public class TransactionLinkedList {

    private static class Node {
        Transaction data;
        Node next;
        Node(Transaction data) { this.data = data; }
    }

    private Node head;
    private Node tail;
    private int size;

    // Add to front — O(1) no shifting
    public void addFirst(Transaction t) {
        Node node = new Node(t);
        node.next = head;
        head = node;
        if (tail == null) tail = head;
        size++;
    }

    // Add to back — O(1) with tail pointer
    public void addLast(Transaction t) {
        Node node = new Node(t);
        if (tail == null) { head = tail = node; }
        else { tail.next = node; tail = node; }
        size++;
    }

    // Access by index — O(n) — must walk from head
    public Transaction get(int index) {
        if (index < 0 || index >= size) throw new IndexOutOfBoundsException();
        Node curr = head;
        for (int i = 0; i < index; i++) curr = curr.next;
        return curr.data;
    }

    // Delete — O(n) to find, O(1) to remove
    // Before: [A] -> [B] -> [C]
    // Remove B: [A] -> [C]  (just update A's next pointer)
    public boolean remove(Transaction t) {
        if (head == null) return false;
        if (head.data.equals(t)) { head = head.next; if (head == null) tail = null; size--; return true; }
        Node prev = head;
        while (prev.next != null) {
            if (prev.next.data.equals(t)) {
                if (prev.next == tail) tail = prev;
                prev.next = prev.next.next;
                size--;
                return true;
            }
            prev = prev.next;
        }
        return false;
    }

    // Reverse — O(n) time, O(1) space — classic interview question
    // Before: head -> [1] -> [2] -> [3] -> null
    // After:  head -> [3] -> [2] -> [1] -> null
    public void reverse() {
        Node prev = null, current = head;
        tail = head;
        while (current != null) {
            Node next = current.next; // 1. save next
            current.next = prev;      // 2. reverse arrow
            prev = current;           // 3. advance prev
            current = next;           // 4. advance current
        }
        head = prev;
    }

    // Detect cycle — Floyd's Tortoise and Hare — O(n) time, O(1) space
    public boolean hasCycle() {
        Node slow = head, fast = head;
        while (fast != null && fast.next != null) {
            slow = slow.next;        // one step
            fast = fast.next.next;  // two steps
            if (slow == fast) return true; // they met inside a cycle
        }
        return false;
    }
}

Array vs. Linked List Decision Guide

OperationArrayListLinkedList
Access by indexO(1)O(n)
Insert at frontO(n) — shiftsO(1)
Insert at backO(1) amortizedO(1)
Insert at middleO(n)O(1) at node
MemoryCompact, cache-friendlyExtra pointer overhead

Rule of thumb: Start with ArrayList. Switch to LinkedList (as Deque) only when you need a stack or queue with frequent front insertion/removal.

6.4 Stacks — Last In, First Out

A stack enforces LIFO order. Think of a stack of trays — you add to the top and take from the top.

Deque<AccountOperation> undoStack = new ArrayDeque<>();

// Push — O(1)
undoStack.push(operation);

// Pop — O(1), throws if empty
AccountOperation last = undoStack.pop();

// Peek — O(1), doesn't remove
AccountOperation top = undoStack.peek();

Three Real Uses of Stacks

// 1. UNDO — the last action is the first to undo
Deque<Transaction> undoLog = new ArrayDeque<>();
undoLog.push(transaction);  // record each action
transaction = undoLog.pop(); // undo = reverse the most recent

// 2. BALANCED BRACKETS — how compilers validate syntax
public static boolean isBalanced(String expr) {
    Deque<Character> stack = new ArrayDeque<>();
    for (char c : expr.toCharArray()) {
        if (c == '(' || c == '[' || c == '{') {
            stack.push(c);
        } else if (c == ')' || c == ']' || c == '}') {
            if (stack.isEmpty()) return false;
            char top = stack.pop();
            if ((c == ')' && top != '(') ||
                (c == ']' && top != '[') ||
                (c == '}' && top != '{')) return false;
        }
    }
    return stack.isEmpty();
}

// 3. THE JVM CALL STACK
// Every method call pushes a frame; every return pops it
// StackOverflowError = the stack ran out of space (infinite recursion)
//
// Thread stack when processTransfer() calls validateAccount():
// [Frame: validateAccount(id=42)]   <-- top
// [Frame: processTransfer(req)]
// [Frame: handleHttpRequest(req)]
// [Frame: main(args)]               <-- bottom

6.5 Queues — First In, First Out

A queue processes elements in arrival order. Like a line at a bank teller.

// FIFO: first offer() is first poll()
Queue<TransferRequest> pending = new ArrayDeque<>();
pending.offer(req1);  // add to back
pending.offer(req2);
TransferRequest next = pending.poll(); // removes req1 — first in, first out

// PRIORITY: highest priority polled first
PriorityQueue<TransferRequest> priorityQueue = new PriorityQueue<>(
    Comparator.comparing(TransferRequest::getPriority).reversed()
);
priorityQueue.offer(new TransferRequest(Priority.LOW,  req1));
priorityQueue.offer(new TransferRequest(Priority.HIGH, req2)); // polled first
priorityQueue.offer(new TransferRequest(Priority.MED,  req3));
priorityQueue.poll(); // returns HIGH priority — O(log n)

How a PriorityQueue (Min-Heap) Works

A heap is a COMPLETE binary tree where every parent <= its children (min-heap).
It is stored in an array — no pointers needed:

          3
         / \
       10    7
      /  \ /
     15  12 8

Array: [3, 10, 7, 15, 12, 8]
Parent of index i: (i-1)/2
Left child of i: 2i+1
Right child of i: 2i+2

poll() (remove minimum):
1. Swap root (min) with last element
2. Remove last element (old root = returned value)
3. "Sift down" the new root to restore heap property: O(log n)

offer() (insert):
1. Add to end of array
2. "Sift up" until parent <= new element: O(log n)

peek() (view minimum): O(1) — always at index 0

6.6 Trees — Hierarchical Structure

A tree has a root node, internal nodes, and leaf nodes (no children). Each node has exactly one parent (except root).

Binary Search Tree (BST)

Every node’s left subtree contains ONLY values less than the node. Right subtree: ONLY values greater.

public class BST {
    private static class Node { BigDecimal val; Node left, right; }
    private Node root;

    // Insert — O(log n) average
    private Node insert(Node node, BigDecimal val) {
        if (node == null) return new Node(val);
        int cmp = val.compareTo(node.val);
        if (cmp < 0) node.left  = insert(node.left,  val);
        if (cmp > 0) node.right = insert(node.right, val);
        return node; // cmp==0: duplicate, ignore
    }

    // Search — O(log n) average
    public boolean contains(BigDecimal val) {
        Node curr = root;
        while (curr != null) {
            int cmp = val.compareTo(curr.val);
            if (cmp == 0) return true;
            curr = cmp < 0 ? curr.left : curr.right;
        }
        return false;
    }

    // In-order traversal (left->root->right) gives SORTED output
    public List<BigDecimal> sorted() {
        List<BigDecimal> result = new ArrayList<>();
        inOrder(root, result);
        return result;
    }
    private void inOrder(Node n, List<BigDecimal> out) {
        if (n == null) return;
        inOrder(n.left, out);
        out.add(n.val);
        inOrder(n.right, out);
    }

    // Level-order (BFS) — visits level by level
    public List<List<BigDecimal>> levelOrder() {
        List<List<BigDecimal>> result = new ArrayList<>();
        if (root == null) return result;
        Queue<Node> q = new LinkedList<>();
        q.offer(root);
        while (!q.isEmpty()) {
            int size = q.size();
            List<BigDecimal> level = new ArrayList<>();
            for (int i = 0; i < size; i++) {
                Node n = q.poll();
                level.add(n.val);
                if (n.left  != null) q.offer(n.left);
                if (n.right != null) q.offer(n.right);
            }
            result.add(level);
        }
        return result;
    }
}

Why Balance Matters

Inserting 10, 20, 30, 40, 50 into a BST:

[10]
    \
   [20]         <- A degenerate tree — a linked list
        \         Search for 50: 5 steps = O(n)
       [30]
            \
           [40]
                \
               [50]

Balanced:      [30]       Search for 50: 3 steps = O(log n)
              /    \
           [20]   [40]
           /         \
         [10]        [50]

Java’s TreeMap and TreeSet use a Red-Black Tree which self-balances on every insert/delete to guarantee O(log n) height always.

6.7 Graphs — Networks

A graph is vertices (nodes) + edges (connections). Trees are a special case of graphs (no cycles, one parent per node).

// Adjacency list representation — best for sparse graphs
Map<String, List<String>> transferNetwork = new HashMap<>();
transferNetwork.put("CHK-001", List.of("SAV-001", "CHK-002"));
transferNetwork.put("SAV-001", List.of("CHK-003"));

// BFS — finds SHORTEST PATH (fewest hops)
public int shortestPath(Map<String, List<String>> graph, String src, String dst) {
    Queue<String> queue = new LinkedList<>();
    Map<String, Integer> dist = new HashMap<>();
    queue.offer(src);
    dist.put(src, 0);
    while (!queue.isEmpty()) {
        String curr = queue.poll();
        for (String neighbor : graph.getOrDefault(curr, List.of())) {
            if (!dist.containsKey(neighbor)) {
                dist.put(neighbor, dist.get(curr) + 1);
                if (neighbor.equals(dst)) return dist.get(neighbor);
                queue.offer(neighbor);
            }
        }
    }
    return -1; // no path
}

// DFS — exhaustive search, cycle detection
public boolean pathExists(Map<String, List<String>> graph,
                           String curr, String target, Set<String> visited) {
    if (curr.equals(target)) return true;
    visited.add(curr);
    for (String neighbor : graph.getOrDefault(curr, List.of())) {
        if (!visited.contains(neighbor))
            if (pathExists(graph, neighbor, target, visited)) return true;
    }
    return false;
}
BFSDFS
UsesQueueStack / Recursion
Finds shortest pathYesNo
MemoryO(width of graph)O(depth of graph)
Best forLevel traversal, shortest pathCycle detection, exhaustive search

6.8 Sorting — Understanding by Building

Bubble Sort — O(n^2) — Learn to Understand What Bad Looks Like

public static void bubbleSort(int[] arr) {
    for (int i = 0; i < arr.length - 1; i++) {
        boolean swapped = false;
        for (int j = 0; j < arr.length - i - 1; j++) {
            if (arr[j] > arr[j+1]) { swap(arr, j, j+1); swapped = true; }
        }
        if (!swapped) break; // already sorted — early exit
    }
}

Merge Sort — O(n log n) — Divide and Conquer

// Divide into halves, sort each half, merge sorted halves
// Guaranteed O(n log n) in all cases — STABLE (equal elements keep relative order)
public static int[] mergeSort(int[] arr) {  // ← [Recursively sorts by divide-and-conquer. Takes any array, returns a NEW sorted array without modifying the original.]
    if (arr.length <= 1) return arr;           // ← [BASE CASE: 0 or 1 elements is already sorted. Return immediately. Every recursive function must have this or it runs forever.]
    int mid = arr.length / 2;                  // ← [Find the midpoint. Integer division: length=5 → mid=2.]
    int[] left  = mergeSort(Arrays.copyOfRange(arr, 0, mid));       // ← [Recursively sort the left half. copyOfRange extracts elements [0, mid). These calls keep halving until we hit the base case.]
    int[] right = mergeSort(Arrays.copyOfRange(arr, mid, arr.length)); // ← [Recursively sort the right half.]
    return merge(left, right);                 // ← [Combine both SORTED halves. merge() is O(n) — this is the key step.]
}

private static int[] merge(int[] left, int[] right) {
    int[] result = new int[left.length + right.length];
    int i = 0, j = 0, k = 0;
    while (i < left.length && j < right.length) {
        result[k++] = (left[i] <= right[j]) ? left[i++] : right[j++];
    }
    while (i < left.length)  result[k++] = left[i++];
    while (j < right.length) result[k++] = right[j++];
    return result;
}

// Trace: [38, 27, 43, 3]
// Split:    [38,27]   [43,3]
// Split:  [38] [27] [43] [3]
// Merge:  [27,38]  [3,43]
// Merge:  [3, 27, 38, 43]

Quick Sort — O(n log n) Average — The Practical King

// Choose a pivot. Partition: smaller left, larger right. Recurse.
// O(log n) extra space (call stack). NOT stable. O(n^2) worst case.
public static void quickSort(int[] arr, int low, int high) {
    if (low >= high) return;
    int p = partition(arr, low, high);
    quickSort(arr, low, p - 1);
    quickSort(arr, p + 1, high);
}

private static int partition(int[] arr, int low, int high) {
    int pivot = arr[high];
    int i = low - 1;
    for (int j = low; j < high; j++) {
        if (arr[j] <= pivot) swap(arr, ++i, j);
    }
    swap(arr, i + 1, high);
    return i + 1;
}

Which Sort Does Java Use?

TypeAlgorithmWhy
Collections.sort() on objectsTimsort (merge + insertion)Stable, adaptive, O(n log n)
Arrays.sort() on primitivesDual-pivot quicksortFast in practice, no stability needed

Timsort detects already-sorted runs and merges them efficiently — O(n) on nearly-sorted data.

6.9 Recursion and Memoization

Every recursive function needs: a base case (stop condition) and a recursive case that makes progress toward the base case.

// Tree height — a natural recursive problem
public int height(TreeNode root) {
    if (root == null) return 0;  // base case
    int leftHeight  = height(root.left);  // recursive case
    int rightHeight = height(root.right); // recursive case
    return 1 + Math.max(leftHeight, rightHeight);
}

// Memoization: cache results to avoid recomputation
// Without: fib(50) makes 2^50 calls
// With: fib(50) makes only 50 unique calls
Map<Integer, Long> cache = new HashMap<>();
public long fib(int n) {
    if (n <= 1) return n;
    if (cache.containsKey(n)) return cache.get(n);
    long result = fib(n-1) + fib(n-2);
    cache.put(n, result);
    return result;
}

// FinTrack: cache monthly spending totals
Map<YearMonth, BigDecimal> spendingCache = new HashMap<>();
public BigDecimal monthlySpending(Account account, YearMonth month) {
    return spendingCache.computeIfAbsent(month, m ->
        account.getTransactions().stream()
            .filter(t -> YearMonth.from(t.getTimestamp()).equals(m))
            .filter(t -> t.getType() == WITHDRAWAL || t.getType() == TRANSFER_OUT)
            .map(Transaction::getAmount)
            .reduce(BigDecimal.ZERO, BigDecimal::add)
    );
}

6.10 FinTrack Data Engine — Putting It All Together

public class FinTrackDataEngine {

    // O(1) account lookup — HashMap, not List with linear scan
    private final Map<String, Account> byNumber = new HashMap<>();
    private final Map<Long, Account>   byId     = new HashMap<>();

    // O(log n) date range queries — TreeMap per account
    private final Map<Long, TreeMap<LocalDateTime, Transaction>> timelines = new HashMap<>();

    // O(1) recent transaction access — bounded Deque per account
    private final Map<Long, ArrayDeque<Transaction>> recentCache = new HashMap<>();
    private static final int MAX_RECENT = 10;

    // Priority-ordered processing queue — PriorityQueue (min-heap)
    private final PriorityQueue<TransferRequest> processingQueue = new PriorityQueue<>(
        Comparator.comparing(TransferRequest::getPriority).reversed()
    );

    public void recordTransaction(Transaction txn) {
        Long id = txn.getAccount().getAccountId();

        // Add to sorted timeline
        timelines.computeIfAbsent(id, k -> new TreeMap<>())
                 .put(txn.getTimestamp(), txn);

        // Maintain rolling window of 10 most recent
        ArrayDeque<Transaction> recent = recentCache.computeIfAbsent(id, k -> new ArrayDeque<>());
        recent.addFirst(txn);
        if (recent.size() > MAX_RECENT) recent.removeLast();
    }

    // O(log n) date range — TreeMap.subMap()
    public List<Transaction> getInRange(Long accountId, LocalDateTime start, LocalDateTime end) {
        TreeMap<LocalDateTime, Transaction> timeline = timelines.get(accountId);
        if (timeline == null) return List.of();
        return new ArrayList<>(timeline.subMap(start, true, end, true).values());
    }

    // O(1) recent transactions — pre-maintained
    public List<Transaction> getRecent(Long accountId) {
        return new ArrayList<>(recentCache.getOrDefault(accountId, new ArrayDeque<>()));
    }
}

Chapter Summary

  • Big O measures growth rate, not raw speed. Always choose algorithms based on how they scale.
  • Arrays give O(1) indexed access because element addresses are computed directly. The basis of ArrayList, HashMap internals, and heaps.
  • Linked lists give O(1) insert/delete at known positions but O(n) random access. Use ArrayDeque for stacks and queues.
  • Stacks (LIFO) model undo operations and the JVM call stack. Queues (FIFO) model any first-come-first-served system. Priority queues (heap) give O(log n) priority-ordered access.
  • BSTs give O(log n) operations when balanced. Java’s TreeMap/TreeSet use Red-Black Trees for guaranteed balance.
  • Graphs model networks. BFS finds shortest paths; DFS is for exhaustive traversal and cycle detection.
  • Merge sort is O(n log n), stable, and guaranteed. Quicksort is O(n log n) average, in-place. Binary search is O(log n) and requires sorted input.
  • Recursion solves divide-and-conquer problems elegantly. Always define a base case and use memoization to avoid redundant computation.

Interview Prep

  • Explain O(1), O(log n), O(n), and O(n log n) with a concrete banking example for each.
  • What is the time complexity of ArrayList.get(index) vs LinkedList.get(index)? Why?
  • Implement binary search. Why use mid = left + (right - left) / 2 instead of (left + right) / 2?
  • Explain BFS vs DFS. Which finds the shortest path? Why?
  • What is the BST property? What happens to performance if you insert already-sorted data?
  • Write cycle detection in a linked list in O(1) extra space.
  • How does PriorityQueue work internally? What is the time complexity of offer() and poll()?
  • What is the difference between merge sort and quicksort in terms of stability and space complexity?

Quick Knowledge Check

  • What Big O complexity is binary search? (O(log n))
  • True or False: Java’s PriorityQueue always returns the largest element first. (False — it is a min-heap by default, returns the smallest.)
  • Fill in the blank: An algorithm with O(2^n) complexity is called ___ time. (exponential)
  • Which traversal of a BST gives sorted output? (In-order: left > root > right)
  • **BFS uses a ___ and DFS uses a ___.** (Queue; Stack or recursion)

Try This — Binary Search

Without looking at the chapter, implement binary search from scratch:

public static int findTransaction(int[] sortedAmounts, int target) {
    // Your implementation here
    // Return the INDEX of target if found, -1 if not found
    // Must be O(log n) — no linear scan allowed
}

Test cases you must pass:

int[] amounts = {100, 250, 500, 750, 1000, 1500, 2000, 3200};
findTransaction(amounts, 500)   // should return 2
findTransaction(amounts, 3200)  // should return 7
findTransaction(amounts, 100)   // should return 0
findTransaction(amounts, 999)   // should return -1

Common Mistakes in Chapter 6

❌ Off-by-one errors in binary search The most common binary search bug is using left < right instead of left <= right, which causes the algorithm to miss the last remaining element. Always trace through a one-element and two-element array manually.

❌ Integer overflow in the midpoint calculation

int mid = (left + right) / 2;   // WRONG — overflows if both are large
int mid = left + (right - left) / 2; // CORRECT

❌ Forgetting to handle null in linked list operations Every linked list operation must handle three cases: empty list, single element, and multiple elements. Most bugs appear in the first two.

❌ Confusing time complexity with real-world performance O(1) does not mean “instant.” A HashMap lookup is O(1) but involves a hash calculation and potential cache misses. Always measure real performance with realistic data sizes.

❌ Infinite recursion Every recursive function needs a base case that stops the recursion. The most common mistake is a base case that is never reached because the problem is not getting smaller with each call.

AI and algorithm interviews

DSA is one area where AI is extremely good at explaining but can mislead you about difficulty. An algorithm might look simple when AI explains it, but genuinely hard to produce from scratch under pressure. Always implement from memory after reading — that is the skill interviews test.
