Chapter 9Spring

Spring Boot — Building the REST API

Chapter Objectives

By the end of this chapter you will be able to:

✓ Explain what Spring Boot is and what problems it solves compared to raw Java web development

✓ Create REST API endpoints using @RestController, @GetMapping, @PostMapping, and path variables

✓ Use dependency injection with @Autowired and @Service and explain why it matters

✓ Validate request data using Bean Validation annotations and return structured error responses

✓ Separate controllers, services, and repositories into distinct layers with clear responsibilities

✓ Handle exceptions globally using @RestControllerAdvice

✓ Build and test the complete FinTrack Account and Transaction API endpoints

Introduction

Spring Boot is the most widely used Java framework for building web services. It brings together dependency injection, REST controllers, database integration, and security into a coherent, production-ready package. This chapter builds FinTrack’s backend API — the engine behind every account query and transaction.

9.1 Dependency Injection and Spring’s IoC Container

Inversion of Control (IoC) is a fundamental design principle: instead of objects creating their own dependencies, a container creates and injects them. This makes code far more testable and flexible.

// Without DI — tightly coupled, hard to test
public class AccountService {
    private AccountRepository repository = new AccountRepositoryImpl(); // hard dependency
}

// With DI — loosely coupled, easy to test
@Service
public class AccountService {
    private final AccountRepository repository; // Spring injects this

    public AccountService(AccountRepository repository) { // constructor injection
        this.repository = repository;
    }
}

Spring’s annotations signal what role each class plays:

AnnotationMeaning
@ComponentGeneric Spring-managed bean
@ServiceBusiness logic layer
@RepositoryData access layer (also translates exceptions)
@Controller / @RestControllerHTTP request handlers
@ConfigurationConfiguration class (defines beans)

9.2 Building REST Endpoints

The REST Request Lifecycle — What Happens on Every API Call

Trace every step a request takes through your Spring Boot application:

CLIENT (React / Postman / curl)
│
│  POST /api/accounts/1/deposit
│  Content-Type: application/json
│  Authorization: Bearer eyJhbGci...
│  Body: {"amount": "500.00"}
│
▼
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│                    SPRING SECURITY FILTER                   │
│                                                             │
│  1. Extract JWT token from Authorization header            │
│  2. Validate token signature and expiry                    │
│  3. Load user from database by token subject               │
│  4. Set SecurityContext — "request is authenticated"       │
│  5. If invalid → return 401 Unauthorized immediately       │
└─────────────────────────────────────┬───────────────────────┘
                                      │ (if valid)
                                      ▼
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│                   DISPATCHER SERVLET                        │
│                                                             │
│  Maps URL + HTTP method to the correct controller method   │
│  POST /api/accounts/{id}/deposit → AccountController       │
└─────────────────────────────────────┬───────────────────────┘
                                      │
                                      ▼
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              @RestController — AccountController            │
│                                                             │
│  @PostMapping("/api/accounts/{id}/deposit")                │
│  public ResponseEntity<AccountDTO> deposit(                │
│      @PathVariable Long id,                                │
│      @Valid @RequestBody DepositRequest request) {         │
│                                                             │
│  • @Valid triggers Bean Validation                         │
│  • If validation fails → 400 Bad Request (auto)            │
│  • Calls AccountService.deposit(id, amount)                │
│  • Returns ResponseEntity with status code                 │
└─────────────────────────────────────┬───────────────────────┘
                                      │
                                      ▼
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              @Service — AccountService                      │
│                                                             │
│  • Verifies account exists (throws 404 if not)             │
│  • Verifies account belongs to authenticated user (403)    │
│  • Validates business rules (positive amount, active acct) │
│  • Calls AccountRepository.save(account)                   │
│  • Creates transaction record                              │
│  @Transactional ensures all-or-nothing                     │
└─────────────────────────────────────┬───────────────────────┘
                                      │
                                      ▼
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              @Repository — AccountRepository                │
│                                                             │
│  Spring Data JPA translates method calls to SQL:           │
│  • findById(id)     →  SELECT * FROM accounts WHERE id=?   │
│  • save(account)    →  UPDATE accounts SET balance=? ...   │
│  Executes in a database transaction                        │
└─────────────────────────────────────┬───────────────────────┘
                                      │
                                      ▼
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│                      PostgreSQL                             │
│                                                             │
│  UPDATE accounts                                           │
│  SET balance = balance + 500.00,                           │
│      updated_at = CURRENT_TIMESTAMP                        │
│  WHERE account_id = 1;                                     │
│                                                             │
│  INSERT INTO transactions (account_id, type, amount, ...)  │
│  VALUES (1, 'DEPOSIT', 500.00, ...);                       │
│                                                             │
│  COMMIT;  ← both writes succeed or both fail (ACID)        │
└─────────────────────────────────────┬───────────────────────┘
                                      │
                              Response travels
                              back up the chain
                                      │
                                      ▼
CLIENT receives:
  HTTP 200 OK
  {"accountId": 1, "balance": "3000.00", "accountNumber": "CHK-0001"}
@RestController
@RequestMapping("/api/accounts")
@RequiredArgsConstructor
public class AccountController {

    private final AccountService accountService;

    // GET /api/accounts — all accounts for the authenticated customer
    @GetMapping
    public ResponseEntity<List<AccountResponse>> getMyAccounts(
            @AuthenticationPrincipal UserDetails userDetails) {
        List<AccountResponse> accounts = accountService
            .getAccountsForCustomer(userDetails.getUsername());
        return ResponseEntity.ok(accounts);
    }

    // GET /api/accounts/{id} — single account
    @GetMapping("/{id}")
    public ResponseEntity<AccountResponse> getAccount(
            @PathVariable Long id,
            @AuthenticationPrincipal UserDetails userDetails) {
        AccountResponse account = accountService.getAccount(id, userDetails.getUsername());
        return ResponseEntity.ok(account);
    }

    // POST /api/accounts — create a new account
    @PostMapping
    public ResponseEntity<AccountResponse> createAccount(
            @Valid @RequestBody CreateAccountRequest request,
            @AuthenticationPrincipal UserDetails userDetails) {
        AccountResponse created = accountService.createAccount(
            request, userDetails.getUsername());
        URI location = URI.create("/api/accounts/" + created.getId());
        return ResponseEntity.created(location).body(created);
    }

    // POST /api/accounts/{id}/deposit
    @PostMapping("/{id}/deposit")
    public ResponseEntity<TransactionResponse> deposit(
            @PathVariable Long id,
            @Valid @RequestBody AmountRequest request,
            @AuthenticationPrincipal UserDetails userDetails) {
        TransactionResponse result = accountService.deposit(
            id, request.getAmount(), userDetails.getUsername());
        return ResponseEntity.ok(result);
    }

    // POST /api/accounts/{id}/withdraw
    @PostMapping("/{id}/withdraw")
    public ResponseEntity<TransactionResponse> withdraw(
            @PathVariable Long id,
            @Valid @RequestBody AmountRequest request,
            @AuthenticationPrincipal UserDetails userDetails) {
        TransactionResponse result = accountService.withdraw(
            id, request.getAmount(), userDetails.getUsername());
        return ResponseEntity.ok(result);
    }
}

Request/Response DTOs

DTOs (Data Transfer Objects) are what cross the API boundary — never expose your JPA entities directly.

// Incoming request — create a new account
public class CreateAccountRequest {
    @NotNull(message = "Account type is required")
    @Pattern(regexp = "CHECKING|SAVINGS", message = "Account type must be CHECKING or SAVINGS")
    private String accountType;

    @NotNull(message = "Opening balance is required")
    @DecimalMin(value = "0.00", message = "Opening balance cannot be negative")
    private BigDecimal openingBalance;

    // getters/setters or use @Data (Lombok)
}

// Outgoing response — account data sent to the client
public class AccountResponse {
    private Long id;
    private String accountNumber;
    private String accountType;
    private BigDecimal balance;
    private boolean active;
    private String ownerName;
    private LocalDateTime createdAt;
}

// Amount for deposits/withdrawals
public class AmountRequest {
    @NotNull
    @DecimalMin(value = "0.01", message = "Amount must be at least $0.01")
    @Digits(integer = 10, fraction = 2, message = "Amount must have at most 2 decimal places")
    private BigDecimal amount;
}

Global Exception Handler

@RestControllerAdvice
public class GlobalExceptionHandler {

    @ExceptionHandler(InsufficientFundsException.class)
    public ResponseEntity<ErrorResponse> handleInsufficientFunds(InsufficientFundsException ex) {
        return ResponseEntity.status(HttpStatus.UNPROCESSABLE_ENTITY)
            .body(new ErrorResponse("INSUFFICIENT_FUNDS", ex.getMessage()));
    }

    @ExceptionHandler(AccountNotFoundException.class)
    public ResponseEntity<ErrorResponse> handleAccountNotFound(AccountNotFoundException ex) {
        return ResponseEntity.status(HttpStatus.NOT_FOUND)
            .body(new ErrorResponse("ACCOUNT_NOT_FOUND", ex.getMessage()));
    }

    @ExceptionHandler(MethodArgumentNotValidException.class)
    public ResponseEntity<ValidationErrorResponse> handleValidationErrors(
            MethodArgumentNotValidException ex) {
        Map<String, String> fieldErrors = ex.getBindingResult()
            .getFieldErrors()
            .stream()
            .collect(Collectors.toMap(
                FieldError::getField,
                FieldError::getDefaultMessage,
                (e1, e2) -> e1
            ));
        return ResponseEntity.badRequest()
            .body(new ValidationErrorResponse("VALIDATION_ERROR", fieldErrors));
    }

    @ExceptionHandler(AccessDeniedException.class)
    public ResponseEntity<ErrorResponse> handleAccessDenied(AccessDeniedException ex) {
        return ResponseEntity.status(HttpStatus.FORBIDDEN)
            .body(new ErrorResponse("ACCESS_DENIED", "You do not have permission to access this resource"));
    }
}

9.3 The Service Layer

@Service
@Transactional
@RequiredArgsConstructor
public class AccountService {

    private final AccountRepository accountRepository;
    private final CustomerRepository customerRepository;
    private final TransactionRepository transactionRepository;
    private final AccountMapper accountMapper;

    @Transactional(readOnly = true)
    public List<AccountResponse> getAccountsForCustomer(String email) {
        Customer customer = customerRepository.findByEmail(email)
            .orElseThrow(() -> new CustomerNotFoundException("Customer not found: " + email));
        return accountRepository.findByCustomer(customer)
            .stream()
            .map(accountMapper::toResponse)
            .collect(Collectors.toList());
    }

    public TransactionResponse deposit(Long accountId, BigDecimal amount, String customerEmail) {
        Account account = getAccountOwnedBy(accountId, customerEmail);

        account.deposit(amount);
        accountRepository.save(account);

        Transaction transaction = new Transaction(
            TransactionType.DEPOSIT, amount, "Deposit to " + account.getAccountNumber(), account
        );
        Transaction saved = transactionRepository.save(transaction);

        return transactionMapper.toResponse(saved);
    }

    public TransferResponse transfer(TransferRequest request, String customerEmail) {
        Account fromAccount = getAccountOwnedBy(request.getFromAccountId(), customerEmail);
        Account toAccount = accountRepository.findById(request.getToAccountId())
            .orElseThrow(() -> new AccountNotFoundException(request.getToAccountId().toString()));

        fromAccount.withdraw(request.getAmount());
        toAccount.deposit(request.getAmount());

        accountRepository.saveAll(List.of(fromAccount, toAccount));

        Transaction debit = transactionRepository.save(new Transaction(
            TransactionType.TRANSFER_OUT, request.getAmount(),
            request.getDescription(), fromAccount, toAccount
        ));
        Transaction credit = transactionRepository.save(new Transaction(
            TransactionType.TRANSFER_IN, request.getAmount(),
            request.getDescription(), toAccount, fromAccount
        ));

        return new TransferResponse(debit, credit);
    }

    private Account getAccountOwnedBy(Long accountId, String customerEmail) {
        Account account = accountRepository.findById(accountId)
            .orElseThrow(() -> new AccountNotFoundException(accountId.toString()));

        if (!account.getOwner().getEmail().equals(customerEmail)) {
            throw new AccessDeniedException("Account does not belong to this customer");
        }

        return account;
    }
}

Chapter Summary

  • Dependency Injection decouples classes from their dependencies, making code testable and flexible.
  • @RestController + @RequestMapping + method-level annotations define REST endpoints.
  • Use DTOs (not entities) at the API boundary. Validate input with @Valid and Bean Validation.
  • @RestControllerAdvice provides centralized exception handling for consistent error responses.
  • Keep controllers thin — they receive requests, call the service, and return responses. Business logic belongs in @Service classes.

Interview Prep

  • What is dependency injection? What problem does it solve?
  • What is the difference between @Controller and @RestController?
  • What HTTP status code should a successful POST return? A successful DELETE?
  • What is the purpose of the service layer? What should NOT go in a controller?
  • How does @Valid work in Spring Boot?

Try This — REST Endpoint

Without looking at the chapter, build a POST /api/accounts/{id}/withdraw endpoint:

Requirements: - Accepts a request body: { "amount": 500.00, "description": "ATM withdrawal" } - Validates that amount is positive (return 400 if not) - Validates that the account exists (return 404 if not) - Validates that the account has sufficient funds (return 400 with a clear error message if not) - On success: deducts the amount, creates a transaction record, returns the updated account with 200 - Proper JSON error responses for all failure cases

Test using curl or Postman before moving on.

Common Mistakes in Chapter 9

❌ Business logic in the controller Controllers should be thin: receive request, call service, return response. Business rules — balance checks, fee calculations, validation logic — belong in the service layer.

❌ Returning raw entity objects from endpoints Returning your JPA entity directly from a controller exposes your database structure, can cause circular reference errors, and makes it hard to change your schema later. Always use DTOs (Data Transfer Objects).

❌ Not returning appropriate HTTP status codes

return ResponseEntity.ok(account);        // 200 for successful GET
return ResponseEntity.created(uri).body(account); // 201 for successful POST
return ResponseEntity.notFound().build(); // 404 for missing resource
return ResponseEntity.badRequest().body(error); // 400 for invalid input

❌ Missing @Transactional on service methods that write Without @Transactional, a service method that makes two database writes can partially succeed — one write succeeds, the application throws an error, and the second write never happens. Your data is now inconsistent.

❌ Hardcoding configuration in source code Database URLs, passwords, secret keys — never in source code. Always in application.properties or environment variables.

Using AI Effectively — Chapter 9

Use AI to understand Spring’s ‘magic’

Spring Boot hides a lot of complexity behind annotations. Use AI to unpack it:

“When I annotate a class with @Service and inject it with @Autowired, what is Spring actually doing behind the scenes? Explain without using Spring terminology.”

Ask AI to review your API design

“Here are my REST endpoints for a banking API: [list]. Are the URLs, HTTP methods, and status codes RESTful? What would you change?”

Use AI to generate Postman/curl test commands

“Generate curl commands to test my deposit endpoint. Include a valid request, a request with a negative amount, and a request for an account that doesn’t exist.”

A critical warning about Spring + AI

Spring Boot has changed significantly across versions. AI trained on older data will sometimes generate code for Spring Boot 2 that doesn’t work in Spring Boot 3. Always specify the version:

“Using Spring Boot 3.2 and Java 21, how do I…”

Complete these tasks before moving to Chapter 10. Estimated time: 4–5 hours.

Task 1 — Your First Spring Boot Application (Beginner)

Using Spring Initializr (start.spring.io), create a new Spring Boot project with dependencies: Spring Web, Spring Boot DevTools. Build a HelloController with:

  • GET /hello — returns “Hello from FinTrack!”
  • GET /hello/{name} — returns “Hello, [name]! Welcome to FinTrack.”
  • GET /status — returns a JSON object {"status": "running", "version": "1.0"}

Run the application and test all three endpoints using your browser or a tool like Postman or curl.

Task 2 — Build the Accounts REST API (Intermediate)

Create a AccountController with full CRUD endpoints:

  • GET /api/accounts — returns all accounts (use a hard-coded list for now, no database yet)
  • GET /api/accounts/{id} — returns one account or a 404 response if not found
  • POST /api/accounts — creates a new account from the request body, returns 201 with the created account
  • PUT /api/accounts/{id}/deposit — accepts an amount in the request body, adds it to the account balance
  • DELETE /api/accounts/{id} — marks the account as inactive (soft delete)

Task 3 — Request Validation (Intermediate)

Add Bean Validation (spring-boot-starter-validation) to your project. Add validation annotations to your request DTOs:

  • Account type must be “CHECKING” or “SAVINGS”
  • Opening balance must be at least $25.00
  • Customer email must be a valid email format
  • Deposit amount must be positive

Add a @RestControllerAdvice class that catches MethodArgumentNotValidException and returns a structured error response listing all field errors. Test by sending invalid data and confirming you receive a helpful error response.

Task 4 — Service Layer Separation (Intermediate)

Refactor your controller to be thin: it should only receive the request, call the service, and return the response. Move all business logic to an AccountService class annotated with @Service. The service should: validate business rules (beyond what Bean Validation covers), manage the in-memory list of accounts, and be the only place that throws business exceptions like AccountNotFoundException.

Task 5 — FinTrack Extension (Challenge)

Build the complete transfer endpoint: POST /api/transfers that accepts fromAccountId, toAccountId, amount, and description. The endpoint must validate both accounts exist, the source account has sufficient funds, and the accounts are different. Return a TransferResponse containing both the debit and credit transaction records. Write at least three integration tests for this endpoint using Spring’s MockMvc.

Chapter 10Spring

Spring Data JPA and Spring Security

Chapter Objectives

By the end of this chapter you will be able to:

✓ Map Java classes to database tables using JPA annotations

✓ Define relationships between entities: OneToMany, ManyToOne, ManyToMany

✓ Write Spring Data repository methods using derived query syntax

✓ Write custom JPQL and native SQL queries in repositories

✓ Configure Spring Security to protect API endpoints

✓ Implement JWT-based authentication: register, login, and validate tokens on every request

✓ Enforce account ownership — customers can only access their own accounts

Introduction

Chapter 9 built the API layer. This chapter adds the two remaining pillars: persistence (Spring Data JPA, which replaces our manual JDBC code) and security (Spring Security with JWT, which ensures customers can only access their own data).

10.1 Spring Data JPA — Object-Relational Mapping

JPA (Java Persistence API) maps Java objects to database tables automatically. No more hand-writing SQL for basic CRUD — Spring Data JPA generates it.

Entity Classes

@Entity
@Table(name = "customers")
@Data @NoArgsConstructor
public class Customer {

    @Id
    @GeneratedValue(strategy = GenerationType.IDENTITY)
    private Long customerId;

    @Column(name = "first_name", nullable = false, length = 100)
    private String firstName;

    @Column(name = "last_name", nullable = false, length = 100)
    private String lastName;

    @Column(nullable = false, unique = true, length = 200)
    private String email;

    @Column(name = "phone_number", length = 20)
    private String phoneNumber;

    @OneToMany(mappedBy = "owner", cascade = CascadeType.ALL, fetch = FetchType.LAZY)
    private List<Account> accounts = new ArrayList<>();

    @Column(name = "created_at", nullable = false, updatable = false)
    @CreationTimestamp
    private LocalDateTime createdAt;
}

@Entity
@Table(name = "accounts")
@Inheritance(strategy = InheritanceType.SINGLE_TABLE)
@DiscriminatorColumn(name = "account_type")
public abstract class Account {

    @Id
    @GeneratedValue(strategy = GenerationType.IDENTITY)
    private Long accountId;

    @Column(name = "account_number", nullable = false, unique = true, length = 20)
    private String accountNumber;

    @Column(nullable = false, precision = 15, scale = 2)
    private BigDecimal balance;

    @Column(name = "is_active", nullable = false)
    private boolean active = true;

    @ManyToOne(fetch = FetchType.LAZY)
    @JoinColumn(name = "customer_id", nullable = false)
    private Customer owner;

    @OneToMany(mappedBy = "account", cascade = CascadeType.ALL, fetch = FetchType.LAZY)
    @OrderBy("timestamp DESC")
    private List<Transaction> transactions = new ArrayList<>();

    @CreationTimestamp
    @Column(name = "created_at", nullable = false, updatable = false)
    private LocalDateTime createdAt;
}

@Entity
@DiscriminatorValue("CHECKING")
public class CheckingAccount extends Account {
    // Checking-specific fields, if any
}

@Entity
@DiscriminatorValue("SAVINGS")
public class SavingsAccount extends Account {
    @Column(name = "daily_withdrawal_limit", precision = 15, scale = 2)
    private BigDecimal dailyWithdrawalLimit = new BigDecimal("2000.00");
}

Repositories

// Spring Data JPA generates implementation automatically
public interface AccountRepository extends JpaRepository<Account, Long> {

    // Method naming convention — Spring generates the query
    List<Account> findByOwnerAndActiveTrue(Customer owner);
    Optional<Account> findByAccountNumber(String accountNumber);

    // Custom JPQL query
    @Query("SELECT a FROM Account a WHERE a.owner.email = :email AND a.active = true")
    List<Account> findActiveAccountsByCustomerEmail(@Param("email") String email);

    // Native SQL when JPQL is insufficient
    @Query(value = """
        SELECT a.* FROM accounts a
        JOIN customers c ON a.customer_id = c.customer_id
        WHERE c.email = :email
        AND a.balance > :minBalance
        ORDER BY a.balance DESC
        """, nativeQuery = true)
    List<Account> findHighBalanceAccountsByEmail(
        @Param("email") String email,
        @Param("minBalance") BigDecimal minBalance
    );
}

public interface TransactionRepository extends JpaRepository<Transaction, Long> {

    @Query("SELECT t FROM Transaction t WHERE t.account.accountId = :accountId " +
           "AND t.timestamp BETWEEN :start AND :end ORDER BY t.timestamp DESC")
    List<Transaction> findByAccountAndDateRange(
        @Param("accountId") Long accountId,
        @Param("start") LocalDateTime start,
        @Param("end") LocalDateTime end
    );

    // Paginated query for large transaction histories
    Page<Transaction> findByAccountOrderByTimestampDesc(Account account, Pageable pageable);
}

10.2 Spring Security with JWT

JWT Authentication — The Complete Flow

JSON Web Tokens (JWT) allow your API to verify who is making each request without storing session data on the server.

REGISTRATION (one time)
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Client                          Server                    Database
  │                               │                          │
  │  POST /api/auth/register      │                          │
  │  {email, password, name}      │                          │
  │ ──────────────────────────►   │                          │
  │                               │  Hash password           │
  │                               │  (BCrypt rounds=12)      │
  │                               │ ────────────────────────►│
  │                               │  INSERT INTO customers   │
  │                               │◄────────────────────────-│
  │  201 Created                  │                          │
  │  {token: "eyJhbGci..."}      │                          │
  │◄──────────────────────────── │                          │
  │                               │                          │
  │ [Client stores token in       │                          │
  │  localStorage or memory]      │                          │

LOGIN (each session)
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
  │  POST /api/auth/login         │                          │
  │  {email, password}            │                          │
  │ ──────────────────────────►   │                          │
  │                               │ ────────────────────────►│
  │                               │  SELECT WHERE email=?    │
  │                               │◄────────────────────────-│
  │                               │  BCrypt.verify(          │
  │                               │    rawPassword,          │
  │                               │    storedHash)           │
  │  200 OK                       │                          │
  │  {token: "eyJhbGci..."}      │  Sign JWT with secret    │
  │◄──────────────────────────── │  Expiry: 24 hours        │
  │                               │                          │

AUTHENTICATED REQUEST (every API call)
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
  │  GET /api/accounts            │                          │
  │  Authorization: Bearer        │                          │
  │    eyJhbGciOiJIUzI1NiJ9...   │                          │
  │ ──────────────────────────►   │                          │
  │                               │  Extract token           │
  │                               │  Verify signature ✓      │
  │                               │  Check expiry ✓          │
  │                               │  Get userId from claims  │
  │                               │ ────────────────────────►│
  │                               │  SELECT accounts WHERE   │
  │                               │  customer_id = {userId}  │
  │                               │◄────────────────────────-│
  │  200 OK                       │                          │
  │  [{account data...}]          │                          │
  │◄──────────────────────────── │                          │

JWT Structure — Three Base64-encoded parts separated by dots:

  eyJhbGciOiJIUzI1NiJ9     ← HEADER  {"alg":"HS256","typ":"JWT"}
       .
  eyJzdWIiOiI0MiIsImlhdCI  ← PAYLOAD {"sub":"42","iat":1704067200,
  6MTcwNDA2NzIwMCwiZXhwIj           "exp":1704153600,"email":
  oiMTcwNDE1MzYwMH0              "alice@example.com"}
       .
  SflKxwRJSMeKKF2QT4fwpMeJ ← SIGNATURE HMAC-SHA256(header+payload,
  fw6U                                              SECRET_KEY)

The payload is readable by anyone — it is NOT encrypted.
The signature proves it has not been tampered with.
Never put sensitive data (passwords, card numbers) in a JWT payload.

Security Configuration

@Configuration
@EnableWebSecurity
@EnableMethodSecurity
@RequiredArgsConstructor
public class SecurityConfig {

    private final JwtAuthenticationFilter jwtFilter;
    private final UserDetailsService userDetailsService;

    @Bean
    public SecurityFilterChain filterChain(HttpSecurity http) throws Exception {
        return http
            .csrf(csrf -> csrf.disable())             // REST APIs don't need CSRF
            .sessionManagement(sm -> sm
                .sessionCreationPolicy(SessionCreationPolicy.STATELESS))  // no server-side sessions
            .authorizeHttpRequests(auth -> auth
                .requestMatchers("/api/auth/**").permitAll()               // public endpoints
                .requestMatchers("/actuator/health").permitAll()
                .anyRequest().authenticated()                               // everything else needs auth
            )
            .addFilterBefore(jwtFilter, UsernamePasswordAuthenticationFilter.class)
            .build();
    }

    @Bean
    public PasswordEncoder passwordEncoder() {
        return new BCryptPasswordEncoder(12); // cost factor 12 — strong but not too slow
    }

    @Bean
    public AuthenticationManager authenticationManager(
            AuthenticationConfiguration config) throws Exception {
        return config.getAuthenticationManager();
    }
}

JWT Service

@Service
public class JwtService {

    @Value("${fintrack.jwt.secret}")
    private String jwtSecret;

    @Value("${fintrack.jwt.expiration-ms}")
    private long jwtExpirationMs;

    public String generateToken(UserDetails userDetails) {
        return Jwts.builder()
            .setSubject(userDetails.getUsername())
            .setIssuedAt(new Date())
            .setExpiration(new Date(System.currentTimeMillis() + jwtExpirationMs))
            .signWith(getSigningKey(), SignatureAlgorithm.HS256)
            .compact();
    }

    public String extractUsername(String token) {
        return Jwts.parserBuilder()
            .setSigningKey(getSigningKey())
            .build()
            .parseClaimsJws(token)
            .getBody()
            .getSubject();
    }

    public boolean isTokenValid(String token, UserDetails userDetails) {
        try {
            String username = extractUsername(token);
            Date expiration = Jwts.parserBuilder()
                .setSigningKey(getSigningKey())
                .build()
                .parseClaimsJws(token)
                .getBody()
                .getExpiration();
            return username.equals(userDetails.getUsername())
                && expiration.after(new Date());
        } catch (JwtException e) {
            return false; // expired, malformed, tampered
        }
    }

    private Key getSigningKey() {
        byte[] keyBytes = Decoders.BASE64.decode(jwtSecret);
        return Keys.hmacShaKeyFor(keyBytes);
    }
}

Authentication Endpoints

@RestController
@RequestMapping("/api/auth")
@RequiredArgsConstructor
public class AuthController {

    private final AuthService authService;

    @PostMapping("/register")
    public ResponseEntity<AuthResponse> register(@Valid @RequestBody RegisterRequest request) {
        return ResponseEntity.status(HttpStatus.CREATED).body(authService.register(request));
    }

    @PostMapping("/login")
    public ResponseEntity<AuthResponse> login(@Valid @RequestBody LoginRequest request) {
        return ResponseEntity.ok(authService.login(request));
    }
}

@Service
@RequiredArgsConstructor
public class AuthService {

    private final CustomerRepository customerRepository;
    private final PasswordEncoder passwordEncoder;
    private final JwtService jwtService;
    private final AuthenticationManager authenticationManager;

    public AuthResponse register(RegisterRequest request) {
        if (customerRepository.existsByEmail(request.getEmail())) {
            throw new EmailAlreadyExistsException("Email already registered: " + request.getEmail());
        }

        Customer customer = new Customer();
        customer.setFirstName(request.getFirstName());
        customer.setLastName(request.getLastName());
        customer.setEmail(request.getEmail());
        customer.setPasswordHash(passwordEncoder.encode(request.getPassword()));

        customerRepository.save(customer);

        UserDetails userDetails = loadUserDetails(customer);
        String token = jwtService.generateToken(userDetails);

        return new AuthResponse(token, customer.getEmail(), customer.getFullName());
    }

    public AuthResponse login(LoginRequest request) {
        authenticationManager.authenticate(
            new UsernamePasswordAuthenticationToken(request.getEmail(), request.getPassword())
        );

        Customer customer = customerRepository.findByEmail(request.getEmail())
            .orElseThrow(() -> new CustomerNotFoundException(request.getEmail()));

        UserDetails userDetails = loadUserDetails(customer);
        String token = jwtService.generateToken(userDetails);

        return new AuthResponse(token, customer.getEmail(), customer.getFullName());
    }
}

Chapter Summary

  • JPA entities map Java classes to database tables using annotations. Relationships are mapped with @OneToMany, @ManyToOne.
  • Spring Data JPA generates repository implementations — you write an interface, Spring writes the SQL.
  • Spring Security with JWT provides stateless authentication: login, receive a token, include it in subsequent requests.
  • BCrypt is the correct password hashing algorithm — it is slow by design, making brute-force attacks impractical.
  • Never expose JPA entities directly in API responses — always map to DTOs.

Interview Prep

  • What is the difference between FetchType.LAZY and FetchType.EAGER? Which should you default to?
  • What is the N+1 query problem? How does JPA’s @EntityGraph or JOIN FETCH solve it?
  • Explain how JWT authentication works end to end.
  • Why should passwords be hashed with BCrypt rather than encrypted?
  • What does @Transactional(readOnly = true) do? Why use it for read operations?

Try This — Spring Security

Without looking at the chapter, configure Spring Security so that:

  • POST /api/auth/register is public (anyone can register)
  • POST /api/auth/login is public (anyone can log in)
  • All other /api/** endpoints require a valid JWT token
  • If no token is provided, the API returns 401 Unauthorized
  • If a valid token is provided for the wrong account (customer A accessing customer B’s account), return 403 Forbidden

Test each case with curl before moving on.

Common Mistakes in Chapter 10

❌ FetchType.EAGER on collections

@OneToMany(fetch = FetchType.EAGER)  // WRONG — loads ALL transactions every time you load an account
@OneToMany(fetch = FetchType.LAZY)   // CORRECT — loads transactions only when you access them

EAGER loading is a common cause of performance problems in Spring applications.

❌ Storing plain-text passwords Never store passwords in the database. Always hash with BCrypt:

passwordEncoder.encode(rawPassword)  // store this
passwordEncoder.matches(rawPassword, storedHash) // verify this

❌ Returning the password hash in API responses Your User or Customer entity may have a passwordHash field. Never include it in your API response DTO. Ever.

❌ Not validating JWT claims on every request Just because a JWT is well-formed does not mean it is valid. Check expiry, check the signature, check that the user in the token actually exists in the database.

❌ N+1 queries with lazy loading Fetching 10 accounts and then accessing account.getTransactions() on each in a loop = 11 queries. Use @EntityGraph or JOIN FETCH in your JPQL to load related data in one query.

Using AI Effectively — Chapter 10

Ask AI to explain JWT structure

“Decode this JWT token and explain what each part means: [paste a token]. Why is the payload not encrypted? Why is that acceptable?”

Use AI to design your security configuration

“I have a banking REST API. Which endpoints should be public, which should require authentication, and which should require the user to own the specific resource? Help me think through the security model.”

Ask AI to find security vulnerabilities in your code

“Review this Spring Security configuration for vulnerabilities: [paste code]. What could an attacker exploit?”

The most important AI warning in this chapter

Security is the one area where AI confidently produces wrong answers. Outdated Spring Security configurations, deprecated JWT libraries, missing CSRF protection — these look correct but are not. For anything security-related, verify against the official Spring Security documentation, not just AI output.

📝 Chapter 10 Homework

Complete these tasks before moving to Chapter 11. Estimated time: 4–5 hours.

Task 1 — JPA Entity Mapping (Beginner)

Convert your three domain classes (Customer, Account, Transaction) into proper JPA entities:

  • Add all required JPA annotations: @Entity, @Table, @Id, @GeneratedValue, @Column
  • Map the relationship: Customer has many Accounts (@OneToMany), Account has many Transactions
  • Use FetchType.LAZY on all collections
  • Add @CreationTimestamp for createdAt fields
  • Configure the accounts relationship with cascade = CascadeType.ALL

Run the application with spring.jpa.hibernate.ddl-auto=create and confirm the tables are created in your database.

Task 2 — Spring Data Repository Methods (Intermediate)

Create repository interfaces for each entity. Write method signatures (Spring Data generates the SQL automatically) for:

  • Find all active accounts for a customer by email
  • Find all transactions for an account in a date range, ordered newest first
  • Find the account with the highest balance for a given customer
  • Count the number of transactions of each type for an account this month
  • Find all customers who have not logged in since a given date

Write a test for each using @DataJpaTest and an H2 in-memory database.

Task 3 — Spring Security Setup (Intermediate)

Add Spring Security to your FinTrack backend:

  • Configure a SecurityFilterChain that permits /api/auth/** and requires authentication everywhere else
  • Create a CustomerUserDetailsService that loads customers by email from the database
  • Add a password_hash field to your Customer entity and encode passwords with BCrypt when creating a new customer

Test by starting the app and confirming that /api/accounts returns 401 when called without credentials.

Task 4 — JWT Authentication (Intermediate)

Implement JWT-based authentication:

  • POST /api/auth/register — creates a new customer account and returns a JWT
  • POST /api/auth/login — validates credentials and returns a JWT
  • JwtAuthenticationFilter — validates the JWT on each request and sets the Security context

Test the full flow: register, receive a token, use the token to call GET /api/accounts, and confirm it returns your accounts.

Task 5 — FinTrack Extension (Challenge)

Add account ownership security: after authentication, every endpoint that accesses accounts must verify the authenticated customer owns the requested account. If a customer tries to access another customer’s account, return a 403 Forbidden. Write a security integration test that: creates two customers, logs in as customer one, and attempts to access customer two’s account — confirming a 403 is returned.

Chapter 11Deployment

Deployment — Docker, CI/CD, and AWS

Chapter Objectives

By the end of this chapter you will be able to:

✓ Write a multi-stage Dockerfile that builds and runs a Spring Boot application efficiently

✓ Write a docker-compose.yml that runs the full FinTrack stack locally with one command

✓ Explain what a container is and how it differs from a virtual machine

✓ Set up a GitHub Actions CI pipeline that runs tests on every push

✓ Deploy FinTrack to AWS using ECS (containers) and RDS (managed PostgreSQL)

✓ Configure environment variables and secrets securely — never in source code

✓ Explain the purpose of a load balancer and how it improves reliability and scale

Introduction

A banking application running only on your laptop is not a banking application — it is a prototype. This chapter deploys FinTrack to the real internet using Docker containers, automated CI/CD pipelines, and AWS cloud infrastructure.

11.1 Docker — Consistent Environments

Docker Architecture — Containers, Images, and Volumes

YOUR MACHINE
┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Operating System (Windows / macOS / Linux)                   │
│                                                                │
│  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐     │
│  │  Docker Engine  (manages everything below)           │     │
│  │                                                      │     │
│  │  CONTAINERS (running instances)                      │     │
│  │  ┌─────────────────┐  ┌─────────────────┐           │     │
│  │  │  fintrack-app   │  │  fintrack-db    │           │     │
│  │  │                 │  │                 │           │     │
│  │  │  Spring Boot    │  │  PostgreSQL 16  │           │     │
│  │  │  Port: 8080     │  │  Port: 5432     │           │     │
│  │  │                 │  │                 │           │     │
│  │  │  Built FROM:    │  │  Built FROM:    │           │     │
│  │  │  eclipse-       │  │  postgres:16    │           │     │
│  │  │  temurin:21-jre │  │  (official img) │           │     │
│  │  └────────┬────────┘  └───────┬─────────┘           │     │
│  │           │  fintrack-network  │                     │     │
│  │           └────────────────────┘                     │     │
│  │                                                      │     │
│  │  IMAGES (blueprints stored on disk)                  │     │
│  │  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │     │
│  │  │  fintrack-backend:latest  (your built image)  │  │     │
│  │  │  eclipse-temurin:21-jre   (pulled from Hub)   │  │     │
│  │  │  postgres:16              (pulled from Hub)   │  │     │
│  │  └───────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │     │
│  │                                                      │     │
│  │  VOLUMES (persistent data)                           │     │
│  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │     │
│  │  │  postgres-data → /var/lib/postgresql/data      │ │     │
│  │  │  (database files survive container restarts)   │ │     │
│  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │     │
│  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘     │
└────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

The key distinction:

  IMAGE   = a snapshot, like a recipe. Immutable. Can be shared.
  CONTAINER = a running image, like a meal made from the recipe.
              Containers are isolated from each other and the host.
  VOLUME  = persistent storage that survives container deletion.

docker-compose.yml starts all three containers with one command:
  $ docker-compose up

The Multi-Stage Dockerfile Explained

# ── STAGE 1: BUILD ────────────────────────────────────────
FROM maven:3.9-eclipse-temurin-21 AS build    # use Maven image
WORKDIR /app
COPY pom.xml .                                # copy config first
RUN mvn dependency:resolve                    # download deps (cached)
COPY src ./src                                # copy source
RUN mvn package -DskipTests                   # compile and package

# ── STAGE 2: RUNTIME ──────────────────────────────────────
FROM eclipse-temurin:21-jre-alpine            # tiny JRE only image
WORKDIR /app
COPY --from=build /app/target/*.jar app.jar   # only the JAR needed
EXPOSE 8080
ENTRYPOINT ["java", "-jar", "app.jar"]

# Result: ~200MB image instead of ~600MB
# Maven, source code, and build tools are NOT in the final image

The classic developer problem: “It works on my machine.” Docker solves this by packaging your application with everything it needs — JDK version, dependencies, configuration — into a container that runs identically everywhere.

Dockerfile for Spring Boot

# Multi-stage build: build stage + runtime stage
# Stage 1: Build the JAR
FROM maven:3.9-eclipse-temurin-21 AS builder
WORKDIR /app

# Copy and download dependencies first (cached if pom.xml unchanged)
COPY pom.xml .
RUN mvn dependency:go-offline -q

# Copy source and build
COPY src ./src
RUN mvn package -DskipTests -q

# Stage 2: Minimal runtime image
FROM eclipse-temurin:21-jre-alpine
WORKDIR /app

# Security: don't run as root
RUN addgroup -S fintrack && adduser -S fintrack -G fintrack
USER fintrack

COPY --from=builder /app/target/fintrack-*.jar app.jar

# JVM tuning for containers
ENV JAVA_OPTS="-XX:+UseContainerSupport -XX:MaxRAMPercentage=75.0 -XX:+ExitOnOutOfMemoryError"

EXPOSE 8080

ENTRYPOINT ["sh", "-c", "java $JAVA_OPTS -jar app.jar"]

Dockerfile for React Frontend

# Stage 1: Build React app
FROM node:20-alpine AS builder
WORKDIR /app
COPY package*.json ./
RUN npm ci --silent
COPY . .
RUN npm run build

# Stage 2: Serve with Nginx
FROM nginx:alpine
COPY --from=builder /app/build /usr/share/nginx/html
COPY nginx.conf /etc/nginx/conf.d/default.conf
EXPOSE 80

Docker Compose — Run Everything Together

# docker-compose.yml
version: '3.9'

services:
  postgres:
    image: postgres:16-alpine
    environment:
      POSTGRES_DB: fintrack
      POSTGRES_USER: fintrack
      POSTGRES_PASSWORD: ${DB_PASSWORD}
    volumes:
      - postgres_data:/var/lib/postgresql/data
    healthcheck:
      test: ["CMD", "pg_isready", "-U", "fintrack"]
      interval: 10s
      retries: 5

  backend:
    build: ./backend
    ports:
      - "8080:8080"
    environment:
      SPRING_DATASOURCE_URL: jdbc:postgresql://postgres:5432/fintrack
      SPRING_DATASOURCE_USERNAME: fintrack
      SPRING_DATASOURCE_PASSWORD: ${DB_PASSWORD}
      FINTRACK_JWT_SECRET: ${JWT_SECRET}
    depends_on:
      postgres:
        condition: service_healthy

  frontend:
    build: ./frontend
    ports:
      - "80:80"
    depends_on:
      - backend

volumes:
  postgres_data:
# Start everything
docker-compose up -d

# View logs
docker-compose logs -f backend

# Stop everything
docker-compose down

11.2 CI/CD with GitHub Actions

CI/CD Pipeline — From Code to Production

Developer                GitHub Actions                   AWS
    │                         │                            │
    │  git push               │                            │
    │  origin main            │                            │
    │ ───────────────────────►│                            │
    │                         │                            │
    │                         │  1. CHECKOUT               │
    │                         │  Clone repository          │
    │                         │                            │
    │                         │  2. BUILD                  │
    │                         │  mvn package               │
    │                         │  npm run build             │
    │                         │                            │
    │                         │  3. TEST                   │
    │                         │  mvn test                  │
    │                         │  ✓ 47 tests passed         │
    │                         │  ✗ 0 tests failed          │
    │                         │                            │
    │                         │  4. BUILD DOCKER IMAGE     │
    │                         │  docker build -t fintrack  │
    │                         │                            │
    │                         │  5. PUSH TO REGISTRY       │
    │                         │  docker push ECR/fintrack  │
    │                         │ ──────────────────────────►│
    │                         │                            │  6. DEPLOY
    │                         │                            │  Update ECS
    │                         │                            │  Task Definition
    │                         │                            │  Rolling update:
    │                         │                            │  Old → New
    │                         │◄──────────────────────────-│
    │                         │  Deployment complete ✓      │
    │◄────────────────────────│                            │
    │  ✅ Pipeline passed      │                            │
    │                         │                            │

If ANY step fails, the pipeline stops and you receive an email.
Your main branch is NEVER broken because code that fails tests
never reaches production.

This is how professional teams ship software:
  ● Every push is tested automatically
  ● Humans review code, machines verify it
  ● Deployment is automated and repeatable
  ● No manual steps that can be forgotten or done wrong

Every push to main should automatically run tests, build, and deploy.

# .github/workflows/deploy.yml
name: Build, Test, and Deploy

on:
  push:
    branches: [main]
  pull_request:
    branches: [main]

jobs:
  test-backend:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    services:
      postgres:
        image: postgres:16
        env:
          POSTGRES_DB: fintrack_test
          POSTGRES_USER: fintrack
          POSTGRES_PASSWORD: testpassword
        ports:
          - 5432:5432
        options: --health-cmd pg_isready --health-interval 10s

    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v4

      - uses: actions/setup-java@v4
        with:
          java-version: '21'
          distribution: 'temurin'

      - name: Cache Maven dependencies
        uses: actions/cache@v3
        with:
          path: ~/.m2
          key: ${{ runner.os }}-maven-${{ hashFiles('**/pom.xml') }}

      - name: Run tests
        run: mvn verify
        working-directory: backend
        env:
          SPRING_DATASOURCE_URL: jdbc:postgresql://localhost:5432/fintrack_test
          SPRING_DATASOURCE_USERNAME: fintrack
          SPRING_DATASOURCE_PASSWORD: testpassword

  test-frontend:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v4
      - uses: actions/setup-node@v4
        with:
          node-version: '20'
          cache: 'npm'
          cache-dependency-path: frontend/package-lock.json
      - run: npm ci
        working-directory: frontend
      - run: npm test -- --watchAll=false
        working-directory: frontend

  deploy:
    needs: [test-backend, test-frontend]
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    if: github.ref == 'refs/heads/main'

    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v4

      - name: Configure AWS credentials
        uses: aws-actions/configure-aws-credentials@v4
        with:
          aws-access-key-id: ${{ secrets.AWS_ACCESS_KEY_ID }}
          aws-secret-access-key: ${{ secrets.AWS_SECRET_ACCESS_KEY }}
          aws-region: us-east-1

      - name: Login to Amazon ECR
        id: login-ecr
        uses: aws-actions/amazon-ecr-login@v2

      - name: Build and push backend Docker image
        env:
          ECR_REGISTRY: ${{ steps.login-ecr.outputs.registry }}
          IMAGE_TAG: ${{ github.sha }}
        run: |
          docker build -t $ECR_REGISTRY/fintrack-backend:$IMAGE_TAG ./backend
          docker push $ECR_REGISTRY/fintrack-backend:$IMAGE_TAG

      - name: Deploy to ECS
        run: |
          aws ecs update-service \
            --cluster fintrack-cluster \
            --service fintrack-backend \
            --force-new-deployment

11.3 AWS Deployment Architecture

Internet
    |
    ▼
CloudFront CDN (serves React frontend, caches static assets)
    |
    ▼
Application Load Balancer (distributes traffic, SSL termination)
    |
    ▼
ECS Fargate (runs Spring Boot containers — serverless, auto-scaling)
    |
    ▼
RDS PostgreSQL (managed database, automated backups, Multi-AZ)
    |
    ▼ (caching layer)
ElastiCache Redis (caches session data, rate limiting, frequent queries)

Key AWS Services

ECS Fargate: Runs Docker containers without managing EC2 instances. You define how much CPU and memory each container needs; AWS handles the rest.

RDS: Managed PostgreSQL. Automated daily backups, one-click read replicas for scaling reads, Multi-AZ for high availability.

CloudFront: CDN that caches your React build files at 400+ locations worldwide. A user in Tokyo gets your JavaScript files from a server in Tokyo, not one in Virginia.

Parameter Store / Secrets Manager: Never put secrets (JWT secret, database password) in environment variables or code. Store them in AWS Secrets Manager and retrieve them at startup.

Chapter Summary

  • Docker packages your application with all its dependencies into a portable container.
  • Multi-stage Docker builds produce small, secure runtime images.
  • Docker Compose orchestrates multiple containers locally.
  • GitHub Actions automates testing and deployment on every code push.
  • Production AWS architecture uses ECS for containers, RDS for databases, CloudFront for the frontend, and Secrets Manager for credentials.

Interview Prep

  • What problem does Docker solve that a virtual machine also solves, but differently?
  • What is the difference between Continuous Delivery and Continuous Deployment?
  • What is CORS? Why does the browser enforce it instead of the server?
  • What is horizontal scaling? How does it differ from vertical scaling?
  • Walk me through what happens — at every layer of the architecture — when a user clicks “Transfer” in the deployed FinTrack application.

Try This — Docker

Without looking at the chapter, write a Dockerfile for the FinTrack backend:

Requirements: - Use a multi-stage build (stage 1: Maven build, stage 2: JRE runtime only) - The final image should NOT contain Maven or the source code — just the JAR - Expose port 8080 - Pass the database URL as an environment variable (not hardcoded) - Build it: docker build -t fintrack-backend . - Run it: docker run -p 8080:8080 -e DB_URL=... fintrack-backend - Verify: curl http://localhost:8080/api/status returns a response

Common Mistakes in Chapter 11

❌ Running as root in Docker Your container should not run as the root user. Add a non-root user to your Dockerfile:

RUN addgroup -S fintrack && adduser -S fintrack -G fintrack
USER fintrack

❌ Secrets in environment variables passed via docker run Command-line arguments are visible in process listings. Use Docker secrets or a secrets manager like AWS Secrets Manager.

❌ Not adding health checks Without a health check, Docker considers a container “healthy” the moment it starts — even if the application inside has not finished starting. Add a health check that pings your /actuator/health endpoint.

❌ Large Docker images A Spring Boot application does not need a full JDK at runtime. Use a JRE-only base image in your second build stage. Use Alpine variants where possible. Every megabyte costs deployment time.

❌ Stopping CI on the first failure Configure your CI pipeline to run all tests before failing, so you get a complete picture of what is broken — not just the first thing that failed.

Using AI Effectively — Chapter 11

“Walk me through this Dockerfile line by line and explain what each instruction does and why the order matters: [paste Dockerfile]”

Use AI to debug Docker networking

Docker networking confuses almost everyone at first. When your app cannot connect to the database:

“My Spring Boot container cannot connect to my PostgreSQL container in Docker Compose. The error is [paste error]. What are the most common causes and how do I diagnose each one?”

Ask AI to review your GitHub Actions workflow

“Review this GitHub Actions workflow for best practices: [paste .yml]. Is it secure? Is it efficient? What would you improve?”

A realistic expectation about DevOps + AI

AWS, Docker, and Kubernetes configurations are areas where AI gives advice that was accurate six months ago but has been superseded. AWS especially changes rapidly. Always check the official AWS and Docker documentation against AI suggestions for anything you put in production.

Chapter 12System Design

System Design, Design Patterns, and the Expert Mindset

Chapter Objectives

By the end of this chapter you will be able to:

✓ Design a system for 10 million users — explain the role of load balancers, caches, and database replicas

✓ Explain and implement four design patterns: Singleton, Factory, Strategy, and Observer

✓ Describe how a CDN, API Gateway, Redis cache, and message queue work together

✓ Explain what Kafka is and why event-driven architecture matters for large systems

✓ Describe the role of Kubernetes in managing containerised applications at scale

✓ Explain what microservices are, their benefits, and their costs

✓ Articulate how learning Java, Spring Boot, React, and PostgreSQL positions you for an AI-driven world

Introduction

The final chapter shifts perspective from “how do I build this feature” to “how do I design systems that scale, stay maintainable, and survive production for years.” These are the skills that separate mid-level from senior engineers — and they are exactly what senior technical interviews test.

12.1 System Design Fundamentals

Scalability

Vertical scaling (scale up): make the server bigger — more CPU, more RAM. Simple, but limited — you can only buy the biggest server in existence.

Horizontal scaling (scale out): add more servers. Unlimited in theory, but requires your application to be stateless — any server must be able to handle any request, which is why we store sessions in Redis instead of in-memory.

Load Balancing

A load balancer distributes incoming requests across multiple application servers. Common strategies:

  • Round robin: requests go to servers in rotation (1→2→3→1→2→3…)
  • Least connections: next request goes to the server with fewest active connections
  • IP hash: same client always routed to same server (useful for sticky sessions)

Caching Strategies

Cache aside (lazy loading):
1. Application checks cache
2. On miss: load from database, store in cache, return
3. On hit: return from cache

Write through:
1. Write to cache and database simultaneously
2. Reads always get fresh data
3. Slower writes, consistent reads

Write behind (write back):
1. Write to cache only
2. Cache asynchronously flushes to database
3. Risk of data loss if cache crashes before flush

For FinTrack, cache aside works well for account data — read frequently, updated occasionally.

@Service
public class CachedAccountService {

    private final AccountRepository accountRepository;
    private final RedisTemplate<String, AccountResponse> redisTemplate;
    private static final Duration CACHE_TTL = Duration.ofMinutes(5);

    public AccountResponse getAccount(Long accountId, String customerEmail) {
        String cacheKey = "account:" + accountId;

        // Check cache first
        AccountResponse cached = redisTemplate.opsForValue().get(cacheKey);
        if (cached != null) {
            return cached;
        }

        // Miss — load from database
        Account account = accountRepository.findById(accountId)
            .orElseThrow(() -> new AccountNotFoundException(accountId.toString()));

        AccountResponse response = accountMapper.toResponse(account);

        // Store in cache
        redisTemplate.opsForValue().set(cacheKey, response, CACHE_TTL);

        return response;
    }

    // Invalidate cache when account is modified
    public void invalidateAccountCache(Long accountId) {
        redisTemplate.delete("account:" + accountId);
    }
}

Database Scaling

Read replicas: One primary database handles writes; multiple replicas handle reads. Most banking queries are reads (checking balance, viewing history) — offloading these to replicas dramatically increases throughput.

Connection pooling: Opening a database connection is expensive (authentication, SSL handshake). A connection pool (HikariCP in Spring Boot) maintains a pool of open connections, reusing them across requests.

spring:
  datasource:
    hikari:
      maximum-pool-size: 20      # maximum open connections
      minimum-idle: 5             # keep 5 connections open always
      connection-timeout: 30000   # fail fast if no connection in 30s
      idle-timeout: 600000        # close idle connections after 10m

12.2 Design Patterns

Design patterns are proven solutions to recurring design problems. They are a shared vocabulary — when a senior engineer says “use a Strategy here,” everyone knows immediately what the structure looks like.

Strategy Pattern — Interchangeable Algorithms

// Define the strategy interface
public interface FeeCalculationStrategy {
    BigDecimal calculateFee(Transaction transaction);
}

// Concrete strategies
public class StandardFeeStrategy implements FeeCalculationStrategy {
    @Override
    public BigDecimal calculateFee(Transaction transaction) {
        if (transaction.getType() == TransactionType.TRANSFER_OUT) {
            return new BigDecimal("2.50"); // flat $2.50 transfer fee
        }
        return BigDecimal.ZERO;
    }
}

public class PremiumFeeStrategy implements FeeCalculationStrategy {
    @Override
    public BigDecimal calculateFee(Transaction transaction) {
        return BigDecimal.ZERO; // premium customers pay no fees
    }
}

// Context — uses whichever strategy is injected
@Service
public class TransactionService {

    private final FeeCalculationStrategy feeStrategy;

    public TransactionService(FeeCalculationStrategy feeStrategy) {
        this.feeStrategy = feeStrategy;
    }

    public BigDecimal processTransaction(Transaction transaction) {
        BigDecimal fee = feeStrategy.calculateFee(transaction);
        // ... apply fee
        return fee;
    }
}

Builder Pattern — Complex Object Construction

public class TransferRequest {
    private final Long fromAccountId;
    private final Long toAccountId;
    private final BigDecimal amount;
    private final String description;
    private final boolean scheduleForLater;
    private final LocalDateTime scheduledTime;

    private TransferRequest(Builder builder) {
        this.fromAccountId = builder.fromAccountId;
        this.toAccountId = builder.toAccountId;
        this.amount = builder.amount;
        this.description = builder.description;
        this.scheduleForLater = builder.scheduleForLater;
        this.scheduledTime = builder.scheduledTime;
    }

    public static class Builder {
        private Long fromAccountId;
        private Long toAccountId;
        private BigDecimal amount;
        private String description;
        private boolean scheduleForLater = false;
        private LocalDateTime scheduledTime;

        public Builder from(Long accountId)   { this.fromAccountId = accountId; return this; }
        public Builder to(Long accountId)     { this.toAccountId = accountId;   return this; }
        public Builder amount(BigDecimal amt)  { this.amount = amt;              return this; }
        public Builder description(String d)   { this.description = d;           return this; }
        public Builder scheduledFor(LocalDateTime time) {
            this.scheduleForLater = true;
            this.scheduledTime = time;
            return this;
        }

        public TransferRequest build() {
            Objects.requireNonNull(fromAccountId, "fromAccountId required");
            Objects.requireNonNull(toAccountId,   "toAccountId required");
            Objects.requireNonNull(amount,         "amount required");
            if (amount.compareTo(BigDecimal.ZERO) <= 0) {
                throw new IllegalArgumentException("Amount must be positive");
            }
            return new TransferRequest(this);
        }
    }
}

// Usage is readable and explicit
TransferRequest request = new TransferRequest.Builder()
    .from(checkingAccountId)
    .to(savingsAccountId)
    .amount(new BigDecimal("500.00"))
    .description("Monthly savings transfer")
    .scheduledFor(LocalDateTime.now().plusDays(1))
    .build();

Observer Pattern — Event-Driven Architecture

// Event
public record TransactionCompletedEvent(
    Long transactionId,
    TransactionType type,
    BigDecimal amount,
    Long accountId,
    LocalDateTime timestamp
) {}

// Publisher
@Service
public class AccountService {

    private final ApplicationEventPublisher eventPublisher;

    public void deposit(Long accountId, BigDecimal amount, String customerEmail) {
        // ... perform deposit ...
        eventPublisher.publishEvent(new TransactionCompletedEvent(
            transaction.getId(), TransactionType.DEPOSIT,
            amount, accountId, LocalDateTime.now()
        ));
    }
}

// Subscribers — react to the event independently
@Component
public class NotificationListener {
    @EventListener
    public void onTransaction(TransactionCompletedEvent event) {
        // Send SMS/email notification
        notificationService.sendTransactionAlert(event);
    }
}

@Component
public class FraudDetectionListener {
    @EventListener
    @Async
    public void onTransaction(TransactionCompletedEvent event) {
        // Async fraud analysis — don't block the main thread
        fraudService.analyze(event);
    }
}

@Component
public class AuditListener {
    @EventListener
    public void onTransaction(TransactionCompletedEvent event) {
        auditLog.record(event);
    }
}

12.3 How FinTrack Scales to a Million Users

ConcernSolution
High read trafficRead replicas for the database; Redis caching
High write trafficHorizontal scaling of the Spring Boot service
Large transaction tablePartition by month; archive old data to cold storage
Slow balance queriesMaterialized view or dedicated balance table updated by triggers
International usersCloudFront CDN for the frontend; multi-region for global latency
Downtime preventionMulti-AZ RDS; blue-green deployment
ComplianceImmutable audit log; field-level encryption for PII

Chapter Summary

  • Horizontal scaling (more servers) scales further than vertical (bigger server) but requires stateless services.
  • Caching dramatically reduces database load for frequently read, infrequently changed data.
  • Read replicas scale read traffic; connection pooling reduces connection overhead.
  • The Strategy pattern makes algorithms interchangeable without changing the code that uses them.
  • The Builder pattern makes construction of complex objects readable and validated.
  • The Observer/Event pattern decouples business logic from side effects like notifications and auditing.

Interview Prep

  • Walk me through designing a banking system that must handle 10,000 transactions per second. Where are the bottlenecks and how do you address them?
  • What is the difference between a monolith and a microservices architecture? What are the tradeoffs?
  • Explain the Strategy pattern. When is it the right choice?
  • How would you implement an undo feature for bank transactions?
  • How does caching work in a distributed system where multiple application servers share one cache?

Quick Knowledge Check

  • True or False: Horizontal scaling means upgrading the CPU of an existing server. (False — that is vertical scaling.)
  • What design pattern should you use when you have multiple interchangeable algorithms for the same task? (Strategy pattern)
  • Fill in the blank: A ___ builds complex objects step-by-step with a fluent API and validates required fields before construction. (Builder)

Try This — System Design

Design a system to handle this requirement:

“1 million customers check their account balance simultaneously at 9am on the first of the month (payday).”

On paper or in a diagram: 1. Where does the initial request go? (CDN? Load balancer? Directly to the server?) 2. How many application servers do you need? How do you decide? 3. Would you cache the balance? Where? For how long? What invalidates the cache? 4. How does the database handle 1 million read queries in seconds? 5. What happens if one application server crashes during this rush? 6. What monitoring would tell you there is a problem before customers notice?

Write a one-page design document. There is no single correct answer — the reasoning matters more than the conclusion.

Common Mistakes in Chapter 12

❌ Designing microservices before you understand the monolith Split a monolith into microservices too early and you get a distributed monolith — all the complexity of microservices with none of the benefits. Build the monolith first. Understand the boundaries. Then extract services where the load or team structure justifies it.

❌ Forgetting that every distributed call can fail In a monolith, a method call either works or throws an exception. In microservices, every network call can fail, time out, or return a partial response. Your system must handle all three for every single call.

❌ Assuming caching solves everything Caches add complexity: they can serve stale data, they need invalidation strategies, they can cause stampedes when they expire. Only cache when you have measured that the database query is the bottleneck.

❌ Ignoring the CAP theorem when designing distributed systems A distributed system cannot simultaneously guarantee Consistency, Availability, and Partition tolerance. Understanding this trade-off is the foundation of all distributed system design.

❌ Optimising before measuring The most common system design mistake is adding complexity — caches, queues, multiple databases — to solve problems you do not yet have. Measure first. Optimise where the measurement points.
