You have now completed the book. Here is how to use AI as a professional developer rather than as a student.

Use AI as a design sounding board

“I’m designing the caching strategy for FinTrack’s account balance endpoint. Here’s my current approach: [describe]. What are the failure modes? What would you do differently?”

At this level, AI works best when you already have a proposal and are pressure-testing it.

Ask AI to help you prepare for interviews

“Ask me a system design interview question appropriate for a mid-level Java developer and evaluate my answer: [give answer].”
“Give me a hard Java OOP interview question and tell me if my answer is correct and complete.”

Use AI to stay current

“What are the most important changes in Spring Boot 3.x that a developer coming from Spring Boot 2 needs to know?”
“What new features in Java 21 should I be using in production code?”

The AI rule that applies for the rest of your career

The more you understand, the better AI works for you. The less you understand, the more dangerous AI is. Every hour you spent working through this book has increased the value you get from every AI interaction going forward.

You are not competing with AI. You are the judgement layer that makes AI useful, safe, and trustworthy.

That is what engineers do.

12.6 Enterprise System Design — Building for Scale

The previous sections taught you design patterns and how to think architecturally. This section gives you the specific technologies that large-scale systems use — the ones that come up in every senior developer interview and that you will encounter in enterprise environments.

By the time FinTrack serves ten million customers, its architecture looks nothing like a single Spring Boot application. Here is what it looks like, and why.

Load Balancers — Spreading the Work

A load balancer sits in front of your application servers and distributes incoming requests across multiple instances.

                    Internet Traffic
                          │
                          ▼
                  ┌───────────────┐
                  │ Load Balancer │  ← AWS ALB (Application Load Balancer)
                  │  (the router) │
                  └──┬─────────┬──┘
                     │         │
              ┌──────▼──┐  ┌───▼──────┐
              │ App      │  │ App      │  ← Spring Boot instances
              │ Server 1 │  │ Server 2 │    in separate containers
              └──────────┘  └──────────┘

Load balancing algorithms:

Round Robin — request 1 goes to Server 1, request 2 to Server 2, request 3 back to Server 1. Simple and effective when servers are identical.

Least Connections — route each request to whichever server has the fewest active connections. Better when requests vary in processing time.

IP Hash — the same client IP always goes to the same server. Useful when you need session stickiness (though JWT makes this less necessary).

Health checks are how a load balancer knows which servers are healthy:

// Spring Boot Actuator provides this automatically
// GET /actuator/health returns:
{
  "status": "UP",
  "components": {
    "db": { "status": "UP" },
    "diskSpace": { "status": "UP" }
  }
}

If a server returns a non-200 response or times out, the load balancer stops sending traffic to it. Your application stays available even during a server failure.

API Gateway — The Front Door

An API Gateway is a single entry point for all client requests. It handles cross-cutting concerns that would otherwise need to be duplicated across every service.

Clients                    API Gateway               Microservices
  │                             │                         │
  │  GET /api/accounts          │                         │
  │ ───────────────────────────►│                         │
  │                             │  1. Authenticate JWT   │
  │                             │  2. Rate limit check   │
  │                             │  3. Log request        │
  │                             │  4. Route request      │
  │                             │ ───────────────────────►│ Account Service
  │                             │◄───────────────────────-│
  │◄────────────────────────────│                         │
  │                             │                         │
  │  POST /api/transfers         │                         │
  │ ───────────────────────────►│                         │
  │                             │ ───────────────────────►│ Transfer Service
  │                             │◄───────────────────────-│
  │◄────────────────────────────│                         │

What the API Gateway handles:

  • Authentication — verify JWT on every request in one place instead of every service
  • Rate limiting — prevent any single client from making too many requests (stops abuse and DDoS)
  • Request logging — every request logged centrally before it reaches any service
  • SSL termination — HTTPS handled at the gateway; internal traffic can be HTTP
  • Request transformation — translate between external API format and internal format
  • Circuit breaking — if a downstream service is failing, stop sending requests to it

In AWS, this is API Gateway or AWS ALB. For microservices, Kong and NGINX are popular choices.

Redis — Caching That Changes Everything

Redis is an in-memory data store — think of it as a dictionary that lives in RAM and can be accessed in under a millisecond.

Without cache:
  Client → Backend → PostgreSQL → Backend → Client
  Time: 50-200ms per request

With cache:
  Client → Backend → Redis (cache hit) → Backend → Client
  Time: 2-5ms per request

  (PostgreSQL only queried on cache miss)

What to cache in FinTrack:

@Service
public class AccountService {

    @Cacheable(value = "accountBalance", key = "#accountId")
    public BigDecimal getBalance(Long accountId) {
        // Only runs if not in cache
        return accountRepository.findBalance(accountId);
    }

    @CacheEvict(value = "accountBalance", key = "#accountId")
    public void deposit(Long accountId, BigDecimal amount) {
        // After a deposit, invalidate the cached balance
        accountRepository.deposit(accountId, amount);
    }
}

Cache invalidation is one of the hardest problems in computer science. The key questions are:

When does cached data become stale? After a deposit, the cached balance is wrong. You must evict (delete) the cache entry immediately.

What if the cache and database are temporarily out of sync? A user deposits money, you update the database, the cache eviction fails. Now the cache shows the old balance. Solutions: short TTL (time-to-live), cache-aside pattern, write-through cache.

Beyond caching — Redis also does:

  • Sessions — store user session data so any server can serve any request
  • Distributed locks — prevent two servers processing the same request simultaneously
  • Message queues — lightweight pub/sub messaging between services
  • Rate limiting — count requests per user across all servers

Kafka — Event-Driven Architecture at Scale

Apache Kafka is a distributed event streaming platform. Instead of services calling each other directly, they publish events to Kafka topics and subscribe to the events they care about.

Direct calls (tight coupling):
  Transfer Service ──► Account Service ──► Notification Service
                                      └──► Audit Service
                                      └──► Fraud Service
  Problem: if any downstream service is slow or down, transfer fails

Event-driven (loose coupling):
  Transfer Service ──► Kafka (transfer.completed topic)
                            │
                   ┌────────┼────────┐
                   ▼        ▼        ▼
               Account   Notify   Fraud
               Service   Service  Service
  Benefit: Transfer Service finishes immediately. Other services
           process the event in their own time.

A Kafka banking event flow:

// Transfer Service publishes an event
@Service
public class TransferService {

    @Autowired
    private KafkaTemplate<String, TransferEvent> kafka;

    @Transactional
    public void transfer(TransferRequest req) {
        // 1. Update database (in same transaction)
        accountRepository.debit(req.fromAccount(), req.amount());
        accountRepository.credit(req.toAccount(), req.amount());

        // 2. Publish event to Kafka
        kafka.send("transfer.completed", new TransferEvent(
            req.fromAccount(), req.toAccount(),
            req.amount(), LocalDateTime.now()
        ));
        // Transfer Service is done. Everything else is async.
    }
}

// Notification Service subscribes to events
@KafkaListener(topics = "transfer.completed")
public void onTransfer(TransferEvent event) {
    emailService.sendTransferConfirmation(event);
    pushNotificationService.notify(event);
}

// Fraud Service subscribes to the same events
@KafkaListener(topics = "transfer.completed")
public void detectFraud(TransferEvent event) {
    if (fraudDetector.isAnomalous(event)) {
        flagForReview(event);
    }
}

Kafka concepts you need to know:

Topic — a named stream of events. Like a database table but for events. transfer.completed, account.created, login.failed are topics.

Partition — a topic is split into partitions for parallelism. 10 partitions allow 10 consumer instances to read simultaneously.

Consumer Group — multiple instances of a service reading from Kafka. Kafka ensures each partition is read by only one instance in the group, so events are never processed twice.

Offset — a position in a partition. Kafka remembers where each consumer group is up to, so events are never missed even if a service goes down and comes back up.

Event Sourcing is the pattern of storing events rather than current state:

Traditional: accounts table stores current balance
Event Sourced: event_store table stores every transaction

To get the balance: replay all events for that account
  DEPOSIT    500 → balance: 500
  WITHDRAWAL  75 → balance: 425
  DEPOSIT   1000 → balance: 1425
  WITHDRAWAL 200 → balance: 1225

Benefits: complete audit trail, time travel (balance at any point),
          easier debugging, natural Kafka integration

Kubernetes — Orchestrating Containers at Scale

Kubernetes (K8s) manages containers across a cluster of servers. Where Docker Compose runs containers on one machine, Kubernetes runs them across hundreds.

Kubernetes Cluster
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│                                                     │
│  Node 1              Node 2              Node 3    │
│  ┌────────────┐      ┌────────────┐      ┌──────┐  │
│  │ Pod: App   │      │ Pod: App   │      │ Pod: │  │
│  │ (replica1) │      │ (replica2) │      │ DB   │  │
│  └────────────┘      └────────────┘      └──────┘  │
│  ┌────────────┐                                     │
│  │ Pod: Redis │                                     │
│  └────────────┘                                     │
│                                                     │
│  Control Plane (manages everything):                │
│  - Scheduler: decides which node runs each pod      │
│  - Controller: ensures desired state matches actual │
│  - etcd: stores cluster state                       │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Key Kubernetes concepts:

Pod — the smallest deployable unit. Usually one container. Has an IP address.

Deployment — declares “I want 3 replicas of this container running at all times.” If a pod crashes, Kubernetes starts a new one automatically.

Service — a stable network address that routes to healthy pods. Pods come and go, but the Service address stays constant.

ConfigMap / Secret — inject configuration and secrets into containers at runtime.

HorizontalPodAutoscaler — automatically scale replicas based on CPU or custom metrics.

# deployment.yaml — tells Kubernetes what to run
apiVersion: apps/v1
kind: Deployment
metadata:
  name: fintrack-api
spec:
  replicas: 3                          # always run 3 copies
  selector:
    matchLabels:
      app: fintrack-api
  template:
    spec:
      containers:
      - name: fintrack-api
        image: fintrack:1.2.0
        ports:
        - containerPort: 8080
        env:
        - name: DB_URL
          valueFrom:
            secretKeyRef:              # read from K8s Secret
              name: fintrack-secrets
              key: database-url
        resources:
          requests:
            memory: "256Mi"
            cpu: "250m"
          limits:
            memory: "512Mi"
            cpu: "500m"

Microservices — When to Split and When Not To

A microservice is an independently deployable service that owns one business capability.

FinTrack as a Microservices Architecture:

  ┌───────────────┐  ┌────────────────┐  ┌──────────────┐
  │ Account       │  │ Transfer       │  │ Notification │
  │ Service       │  │ Service        │  │ Service      │
  │               │  │                │  │              │
  │ - Create      │  │ - Transfer     │  │ - Email      │
  │ - Balance     │  │ - History      │  │ - SMS        │
  │ - Deposit     │  │ - Limits       │  │ - Push       │
  │ - Withdraw    │  │                │  │              │
  │               │  │                │  │              │
  │ DB: accounts  │  │ DB: transfers  │  │ External     │
  └───────────────┘  └────────────────┘  └──────────────┘

  ┌───────────────┐  ┌────────────────┐
  │ Auth          │  │ Fraud          │
  │ Service       │  │ Service        │
  │               │  │                │
  │ - Register    │  │ - Rule engine  │
  │ - Login       │  │ - ML model     │
  │ - JWT         │  │ - Alerts       │
  │               │  │                │
  │ DB: customers │  │ DB: events     │
  └───────────────┘  └────────────────┘

The honest trade-offs:

BenefitCost
Each service deploys independentlyNetwork calls instead of method calls (slower, can fail)
Services scale independentlyData consistency across services is hard
Teams own their servicesMuch more infrastructure to manage
Technology flexibility per serviceDebugging spans multiple services
Fault isolationTesting requires all services running

The rule: start with a monolith. Build FinTrack as one Spring Boot application first (what this book teaches). When you can identify where the seams are — which parts of the code change together, which teams own which features — then extract services.

Most companies that built microservices too early are now building “macro services” (fewer, larger services) because the overhead was not worth it at their scale.

Distributed Transactions — The Hardest Problem

When a bank transfer touches two accounts in two different microservices, how do you ensure both updates succeed or neither does?

In a single Spring Boot application with one database, @Transactional handles this. In microservices, you cannot use database transactions across services.

The Saga Pattern — the industry solution:

Transfer Saga:
  1. Transfer Service: reserve $500 from Account A
         → publishes "funds.reserved" event
  2. Account Service: credit $500 to Account B
         → publishes "transfer.completed" event
  3. Transfer Service: confirms the transfer

If step 2 fails:
  2a. Account Service: publishes "transfer.failed" event
  3a. Transfer Service: releases the $500 reservation on Account A
      (this is the "compensating transaction")

Each step either succeeds and publishes an event, or fails and triggers a reversal. No distributed lock, no two-phase commit.

The Outbox Pattern — ensures the event is always published:

@Transactional  // this is ONE local transaction
public void transfer(TransferRequest req) {
    // 1. Update balance in accounts table
    accountRepository.debit(req.fromAccount(), req.amount());

    // 2. Write event to outbox table (same transaction!)
    outboxRepository.save(new OutboxEvent(
        "transfer.completed", req
    ));
    // If this transaction commits, BOTH the balance update
    // AND the outbox event are saved. Atomically.
}

// Separate process reads outbox and publishes to Kafka
@Scheduled(fixedDelay = 1000)
public void publishOutboxEvents() {
    List<OutboxEvent> pending = outboxRepository.findPending();
    pending.forEach(event -> {
        kafka.send(event.getTopic(), event.getPayload());
        event.markPublished();
        outboxRepository.save(event);
    });
}

The outbox pattern guarantees that if the database transaction succeeds, the event will eventually be published to Kafka — even if the application crashes immediately after.

Caching Patterns — Beyond Simple Get/Set

Cache-Aside (Lazy Loading) — application checks cache first, loads from database on miss:

Read:  Check cache → miss → read DB → write to cache → return
Write: Update DB → evict cache (next read will reload from DB)

Best for: read-heavy workloads where data is frequently read but
          not every item is needed in cache

Write-Through — write to cache and database simultaneously:

Write: Update DB + update cache (atomically if possible)
Read:  Check cache → always hit (for recently written data)

Best for: data that is read immediately after being written
          Downside: cache fills with data that may never be read

TTL (Time-To-Live) — every cache entry expires automatically:

@Cacheable(value = "exchangeRates", key = "#currency", unless = "#result == null")
@CacheExpiry(ttl = 300, timeUnit = TimeUnit.SECONDS) // refresh every 5 minutes
public ExchangeRate getRate(String currency) {
    return externalRateApi.fetchRate(currency);
}

For FinTrack specifically: - Account balance: no cache (must always be exact) or very short TTL (1 second) - Exchange rates: cache with 5-minute TTL (acceptable staleness) - Customer profile: cache with 30-minute TTL (rarely changes) - Transaction history page 1: cache with 1-minute TTL (most accessed)

12.7 The Technology Landscape — Where You Fit In

You have now completed twelve chapters. You have built a complete banking application from a blank screen to a live AWS deployment. Before this book closes, let us answer the question that has been in the background since Chapter 1.

How Do AI, Cloud, and Quantum Computing Affect Your Career?

Artificial Intelligence — Your Most Powerful Tool

In the time you have been reading this book, AI coding tools have become standard practice. GitHub Copilot, ChatGPT, Amazon CodeWhisperer — every professional developer uses at least one of them daily.

Here is what they are good at: generating boilerplate, suggesting syntax, explaining error messages, writing first drafts of standard patterns. Here is what they cannot do: understand your business domain, evaluate trade-offs, design systems that meet requirements you have not yet stated, debug production incidents that require judgment about which logs matter.

The developers who thrive with AI are the ones who finished books like this one. They understand what AI is generating, can evaluate whether it is correct, and know when to trust it and when to override it. The developers who struggle are the ones who used AI to skip the fundamentals — and now cannot tell when the AI is confidently wrong.

Cloud — Already Your Default

Every skill you learned in Chapter 11 — Docker, CI/CD, AWS deployment — is already what the industry expects. Job descriptions that listed “Docker and Kubernetes” as “nice to have” in 2019 list them as requirements today. You have them.

Quantum Computing — The Horizon

Quantum computers will not replace classical computing in your career. They will complement it for specific problem classes — cryptography, molecular simulation, optimisation. The developers who work on quantum systems will be people who learned classical computing deeply first, which is exactly what you have done.

The Java, Spring Boot, React, PostgreSQL, Docker, and AWS skills in this book are skills that will compound for the next decade. Fundamentals do not go out of date. The specific versions and frameworks will change. Your ability to learn new ones will not.

A Final Word

You started this book, perhaps, with some uncertainty. Maybe you were not sure if you were capable. Maybe someone had told you that you were not a “technical person.” Maybe you had tried to learn before and got lost.

Finish it, and you will have built something real — a banking application that handles accounts, transactions, security, and cloud deployment. You will understand every line of code in it. You will know why every architectural decision was made.

That is not a small thing. Most people who start never finish. Most people who finish do not understand what they built. You will be different.

The philosophy has not changed since page one:

Anyone can become a Full Stack Java Developer — with dedication, discipline, and the right guide.

You had the dedication. This was the guide.

Go build something.

📝 Chapter 12 Homework

These are your final consolidation tasks. Estimated time: 5–8 hours.

Task 1 — System Design Exercise (Intermediate)

On paper (or in a drawing tool), design how FinTrack would need to change to support 10 million active customers. Your design must address:

  • Where does the load balancer sit?
  • How many application servers do you need and how do they share state?
  • How do you scale the database for read-heavy traffic?
  • What do you cache and why?
  • How do you handle the case where a transfer request arrives during a database failover?

Write a one-page summary explaining each decision. There is no single correct answer — the exercise is the thinking.

Task 2 — Design Patterns (Intermediate)

Implement two design patterns in FinTrack:

Strategy Pattern: Create a FeeStrategy interface with a calculateFee(Transaction t) method. Implement three strategies: NoFeeStrategy (for premium customers), FlatFeeStrategy ($2.50 per transfer), and PercentageFeeStrategy (0.5% of the transaction amount, minimum $1). Inject the right strategy based on customer tier.

Observer Pattern: When a transaction completes, automatically notify three observers: AuditLogObserver (logs to a file), LowBalanceObserver (checks if balance dropped below $100 and would send an alert), and FraudDetectionObserver (flags transactions over $10,000). The transfer service should not know about any of these observers directly.

Task 3 — Build Your Capstone Project (Challenge)

Build a second, complete full-stack application entirely independently — no step-by-step guidance, no copying from FinTrack. Choose a domain you find interesting: a library management system, a simple e-commerce cart, a restaurant ordering app, or anything else.

Requirements: - Spring Boot REST API with JPA and Spring Security - React frontend with routing and form validation - PostgreSQL database with a normalised schema - At least one custom exception type and centralised error handling - At least ten unit tests - Deployed and accessible at a public URL - A clean README with setup instructions and screenshots

This project is your portfolio piece. Future employers will look at it.

Task 4 — Code Review (Intermediate)

Go back to the FinTrack code you wrote in Chapters 3 through 8 — before you knew Spring, JPA, and security. With fresh eyes:

  • Find three places where error handling was missing or insufficient and fix them
  • Find two methods that are doing too many things (violating Single Responsibility) and refactor them
  • Find any hardcoded values that should be configuration and extract them
  • Write tests for any critical methods that currently have no test coverage

Commit each fix separately with a clear message explaining what you changed and why.

Task 5 — Interview Preparation (All Levels)

Schedule a one-hour mock technical interview with a classmate or record yourself answering questions. Cover:

  • Explain FinTrack end-to-end: what it does, how it is structured, what database it uses, how authentication works, how it is deployed — as if explaining to a technical interviewer
  • Pick any three classes from FinTrack and walk through them line by line, explaining every decision
  • Answer: “How would you add a new feature — international wire transfers — to FinTrack? Walk me through everything from requirements to deployment.”
  • Solve one LeetCode easy/medium problem live and talk through your thinking out loud

Write down any questions you could not answer confidently, and revisit those sections of the book.

Appendix BAppendix

Glossary

ACID — Atomicity, Consistency, Isolation, Durability. The four properties that make database transactions reliable. Atomicity means all-or-nothing. Consistency means data always satisfies constraints. Isolation means concurrent transactions do not interfere. Durability means committed data survives failures.

API (Application Programming Interface) — A defined contract for how one software component communicates with another. In this book, REST APIs expose application functionality over HTTP.

Agile — A software development philosophy that values iterative delivery, feedback, and adaptation over rigid upfront planning.

Bean (Spring) — An object managed by the Spring IoC container. Spring creates, configures, and injects beans based on annotations.

Big O Notation — A mathematical notation describing how an algorithm’s runtime or space requirements grow as input size grows.

BCrypt — A password hashing function designed to be slow, making brute-force attacks computationally expensive. The standard choice for storing passwords.

CI/CD — Continuous Integration / Continuous Delivery (or Deployment). Automating the building, testing, and releasing of software on every code change.

CORS — Cross-Origin Resource Sharing. A browser security mechanism that restricts web pages from making requests to a different domain than the one that served the page.

CRUD — Create, Read, Update, Delete. The four basic database operations corresponding to POST, GET, PUT/PATCH, DELETE in REST.

DAO — Data Access Object. A design pattern that separates data access logic from business logic. Spring Data repositories are DAOs.

DTO — Data Transfer Object. A simple object used to carry data between layers or across API boundaries, with no business logic.

Dependency Injection — A design pattern where an object receives its dependencies from an external source (the Spring container) rather than creating them itself.

Docker — A platform for packaging applications and their dependencies into portable containers that run consistently across environments.

ECS — Amazon Elastic Container Service. AWS’s managed service for running Docker containers without managing the underlying servers.

Entity — In JPA, a Java class mapped to a database table. Each instance corresponds to a row.

Fetch Type — In JPA, controls when related entities are loaded. LAZY: only when accessed. EAGER: immediately with the parent.

Foreign Key — A column (or group of columns) in a database table that references the primary key of another table, enforcing referential integrity.

HTTP — HyperText Transfer Protocol. The application-level protocol used for communication between web clients and servers.

IoC — Inversion of Control. The principle that a framework controls the flow of execution rather than application code. Spring’s container is an IoC container.

JWT — JSON Web Token. A compact, signed token format for representing claims between parties. Used for stateless authentication in REST APIs.

JPA — Java Persistence API. The standard Java specification for ORM. Spring Data JPA builds on it with generated repositories.

JDBC — Java Database Connectivity. The low-level Java API for executing SQL against relational databases. JPA builds on top of it.

Lombok — A Java library that generates boilerplate code (getters, setters, constructors, builders) through annotations like @Data, @Builder, @RequiredArgsConstructor.

Maven — A Java build tool that manages dependencies, compiles code, runs tests, and packages applications. Configuration lives in pom.xml.

Microservices — An architectural style that structures an application as a collection of small, independently deployable services, each responsible for a specific domain.

Mockito — A Java testing library for creating mock objects — fake implementations of dependencies — enabling isolated unit testing.

ORM — Object-Relational Mapping. A technique that maps database tables to Java objects, eliminating most manual SQL. Hibernate is the most common JPA implementation.

Optional — A Java container type that explicitly represents the possible absence of a value, forcing callers to handle both cases instead of risking NullPointerException.

Pagination — Returning large datasets in fixed-size pages rather than all at once. Spring Data supports this through Pageable and Page<T>.

Polymorphism — An OOP principle where a method call on a parent type executes the appropriate subclass implementation at runtime.

PreparedStatement — A JDBC feature that pre-compiles SQL with parameter placeholders, preventing SQL injection attacks.

RDS — Amazon Relational Database Service. AWS’s managed database service for PostgreSQL, MySQL, and other relational databases.

REST — Representational State Transfer. An architectural style for networked APIs using standard HTTP methods to operate on resources identified by URLs.

Scrum — A specific Agile framework using fixed-length sprints, daily standups, and defined team roles.

SDLC — Software Development Life Cycle. The structured sequence of phases a software project moves through from requirements to maintenance.

Sprint — A fixed-length iteration (typically 1–2 weeks) in Scrum during which the team commits to completing a defined set of work.

Spring Boot — An opinionated Spring framework that auto-configures a production-ready Spring application with minimal configuration.

Spring Security — The Spring Framework module handling authentication, authorization, and protection against common attacks.

SQL Injection — A security vulnerability where untrusted user input is embedded in SQL queries, allowing attackers to manipulate the database. Always use PreparedStatement.

Stream (Java 8) — A sequence of elements supporting sequential and parallel aggregate operations. Not a data structure — a pipeline of operations on a source collection.

Transaction (Database) — A sequence of database operations that execute as an atomic unit: all succeed or all fail together.

User Story — A brief, plain-language description of a software feature from the end user’s perspective, in the format: “As a [user], I want [goal], so that [benefit].”

Virtual DOM — React’s lightweight in-memory representation of the actual DOM. React diffs the virtual DOM to minimize expensive real DOM updates.

Appendix DAppendix

FinTrack Complete Database Schema

-- FinTrack Banking Application — Complete Schema
-- PostgreSQL 16

-- Enable UUID extension
CREATE EXTENSION IF NOT EXISTS "pgcrypto";

-- ============================================================
-- CUSTOMERS
-- ============================================================
CREATE TABLE customers (
    customer_id     BIGSERIAL       PRIMARY KEY,
    first_name      VARCHAR(100)    NOT NULL,
    last_name       VARCHAR(100)    NOT NULL,
    email           VARCHAR(200)    NOT NULL,
    phone_number    VARCHAR(20),
    password_hash   VARCHAR(255)    NOT NULL,
    is_active       BOOLEAN         NOT NULL DEFAULT TRUE,
    created_at      TIMESTAMP       NOT NULL DEFAULT CURRENT_TIMESTAMP,
    updated_at      TIMESTAMP       NOT NULL DEFAULT CURRENT_TIMESTAMP,
    CONSTRAINT customers_email_unique UNIQUE (email)
);

-- ============================================================
-- ACCOUNTS
-- ============================================================
CREATE TABLE accounts (
    account_id          BIGSERIAL       PRIMARY KEY,
    account_number      VARCHAR(20)     NOT NULL,
    account_type        VARCHAR(20)     NOT NULL,
    balance             DECIMAL(15,2)   NOT NULL DEFAULT 0.00,
    is_active           BOOLEAN         NOT NULL DEFAULT TRUE,
    customer_id         BIGINT          NOT NULL,
    daily_withdrawal_limit DECIMAL(15,2),
    created_at          TIMESTAMP       NOT NULL DEFAULT CURRENT_TIMESTAMP,
    updated_at          TIMESTAMP       NOT NULL DEFAULT CURRENT_TIMESTAMP,
    CONSTRAINT accounts_account_number_unique UNIQUE (account_number),
    CONSTRAINT accounts_account_type_check CHECK (account_type IN ('CHECKING', 'SAVINGS')),
    CONSTRAINT accounts_balance_check CHECK (balance >= 0),
    CONSTRAINT accounts_customer_fk FOREIGN KEY (customer_id)
        REFERENCES customers(customer_id) ON DELETE RESTRICT
);

-- ============================================================
-- TRANSACTIONS
-- ============================================================
CREATE TABLE transactions (
    transaction_id      BIGSERIAL       PRIMARY KEY,
    transaction_type    VARCHAR(30)     NOT NULL,
    amount              DECIMAL(15,2)   NOT NULL,
    description         VARCHAR(500),
    timestamp           TIMESTAMP       NOT NULL DEFAULT CURRENT_TIMESTAMP,
    account_id          BIGINT          NOT NULL,
    related_account_id  BIGINT,
    status              VARCHAR(20)     NOT NULL DEFAULT 'COMPLETED',
    created_at          TIMESTAMP       NOT NULL DEFAULT CURRENT_TIMESTAMP,
    CONSTRAINT transactions_amount_check CHECK (amount > 0),
    CONSTRAINT transactions_type_check CHECK (transaction_type IN (
        'DEPOSIT', 'WITHDRAWAL', 'TRANSFER_OUT', 'TRANSFER_IN', 'FEE', 'INTEREST'
    )),
    CONSTRAINT transactions_status_check CHECK (status IN (
        'PENDING', 'COMPLETED', 'FAILED', 'REVERSED'
    )),
    CONSTRAINT transactions_account_fk FOREIGN KEY (account_id)
        REFERENCES accounts(account_id) ON DELETE RESTRICT,
    CONSTRAINT transactions_related_account_fk FOREIGN KEY (related_account_id)
        REFERENCES accounts(account_id) ON DELETE RESTRICT
);

-- ============================================================
-- AUDIT LOG — immutable record of all significant events
-- ============================================================
CREATE TABLE audit_log (
    audit_id        BIGSERIAL       PRIMARY KEY,
    event_type      VARCHAR(50)     NOT NULL,
    customer_id     BIGINT,
    account_id      BIGINT,
    details         TEXT,
    ip_address      INET,
    user_agent      VARCHAR(500),
    created_at      TIMESTAMP       NOT NULL DEFAULT CURRENT_TIMESTAMP
);

-- ============================================================
-- INDEXES
-- ============================================================
-- Customer lookups
CREATE INDEX idx_customers_email ON customers(email);

-- Account lookups
CREATE INDEX idx_accounts_customer_id ON accounts(customer_id);
CREATE INDEX idx_accounts_account_number ON accounts(account_number);

-- Transaction queries — most performance-critical
CREATE INDEX idx_transactions_account_id ON transactions(account_id);
CREATE INDEX idx_transactions_timestamp ON transactions(timestamp DESC);
CREATE INDEX idx_transactions_account_timestamp
    ON transactions(account_id, timestamp DESC);

-- Audit log queries
CREATE INDEX idx_audit_log_customer_id ON audit_log(customer_id);
CREATE INDEX idx_audit_log_created_at ON audit_log(created_at DESC);

-- ============================================================
-- VIEWS
-- ============================================================
-- Account balance summary per customer
CREATE VIEW customer_balance_summary AS
SELECT
    c.customer_id,
    c.first_name || ' ' || c.last_name AS customer_name,
    c.email,
    COUNT(a.account_id) AS account_count,
    SUM(a.balance) AS total_balance,
    SUM(CASE WHEN a.account_type = 'CHECKING' THEN a.balance ELSE 0 END) AS checking_balance,
    SUM(CASE WHEN a.account_type = 'SAVINGS'  THEN a.balance ELSE 0 END) AS savings_balance
FROM customers c
LEFT JOIN accounts a ON c.customer_id = a.customer_id AND a.is_active = TRUE
WHERE c.is_active = TRUE
GROUP BY c.customer_id, c.first_name, c.last_name, c.email;

-- Monthly transaction summary per account
CREATE VIEW monthly_transaction_summary AS
SELECT
    account_id,
    DATE_TRUNC('month', timestamp) AS month,
    COUNT(*) AS transaction_count,
    SUM(CASE WHEN transaction_type IN ('DEPOSIT', 'TRANSFER_IN') THEN amount ELSE 0 END) AS total_credits,
    SUM(CASE WHEN transaction_type IN ('WITHDRAWAL', 'TRANSFER_OUT', 'FEE') THEN amount ELSE 0 END) AS total_debits
FROM transactions
WHERE status = 'COMPLETED'
GROUP BY account_id, DATE_TRUNC('month', timestamp);
